\renewcommand{\accession}{III}
\title{\textbf{\Huge Halo 2 Guide}\\[6pt]\large The Halo 2 proof system, from arithmetisation to recursion}
\author{m@rek.onl}
\date{}
\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
This volume of \emph{The Zcash Arboretum} develops the proof system that
Orchard uses: Halo~2, the argument by which the author of a shielded
Action convinces every validator that the Action is well formed while
disclosing nothing about the note, the key, or the Merkle path behind
it. It assumes the \emph{Math Guide}, whose finite fields, polynomials
and evaluation domains, elliptic curves, and probability are used
throughout without re-derivation, and the \emph{Crypto Guide}, whose
definitions it instantiates rather than restates: arguments of
knowledge and zero knowledge, the Fiat--Shamir transform, Pedersen
vector commitments, and the polynomial-commitment interface with its
compilation recipe.

The running example is knowledge of $x$ with $x^3 + x + 5 = 35$ over
the field $\F_{97}$, a size at which every number of the example can be
checked by hand; every number is computed by script. The volume
reduces the example to a grid of gates, the grid to polynomials, the
polynomials to a single identity, and the identity to one evaluation at
a random point, at which the prover must open a commitment without
sending the committed polynomial. The inner-product argument (IPA) that
performs the opening has a section of its own: the problem stated
without machinery, one fold written out step by step, the recursion from
the prover's and the verifier's side, the verifier's structured scalars,
the extraction from three transcripts that proves the argument knowledge
sound under the discrete-logarithm assumption on Vesta, with the hash
that derives the generators modelled as a random oracle, the blinders and
what they do not hide, and the running example opened on a curve of
prime order $97$. The deployed proof system follows: the Action
circuit's arithmetisation, the binding of the public statement, the
Fiat--Shamir transcript, the reduction of many openings to one, the
complete protocol, and its zero knowledge. The volume then analyses the
knowledge soundness of the Fiat--Shamir-compiled argument, lists the
verifier's checks on the running example in the order he performs
them, and closes with recursive composition and accumulation, which
Orchard does not deploy.
\end{abstract}
\clearpage
\tableofcontents
\clearpage

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:intro}

\subsection{Scope and layering}\label{sec:intro-scope}

This is the third volume of \emph{The Zcash Arboretum}, and it develops
the cryptography behind Zcash's Orchard protocol: the proof system
Halo~2, with which every Orchard Action carries a zero-knowledge proof
of its own well-formedness. The protocol specification fixes that use:
Halo~2 is used with the Vesta curve (the \emph{Math Guide}, \S``Pallas
and Vesta assembled'') to prove and verify the Orchard Action statement
(protocol specification
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#abstractzk}{\S~4.1.13},
``Zero-Knowledge Proving System''), and the proving system is defined
by reference to the halo2 book
(\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#halo2}{\S~5.4.10.3},
``Halo 2''). The statement itself
(\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actionstatement}{\S~4.18.4},
``Action Statement (Orchard)'') is read in \S\ref{sec:intro-claim}.
Actions of the Orchard pool and of the Ironwood pool, the second shielded
pool of Orchard-protocol notes, prove this statement, extended at NU6.3
by one public bit, $\mathsf{disableCrossAddress}$, that when set forces
the new note's address to equal the spent note's, with one circuit and
one verifying key (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actiondesc}{\S~4.6},
``Action Descriptions''; \href{https://zips.z.cash/zip-0229}{ZIP~229};
\href{https://zips.z.cash/zip-0258}{ZIP~258}), so
the proof system and proof format described here serve both pools.

The two volumes below this one built its material from the ground up.
The \emph{Math Guide} constructed the mathematics: finite fields,
elliptic curves, polynomials and evaluation domains, probability. The
\emph{Crypto Guide} constructed the cryptographic primitives: hash
functions, commitments, $\Sigma$-protocols and the Fiat--Shamir
transform, zero knowledge, polynomial commitment schemes. This volume
assumes all of these and defines none of them afresh; it assembles them
into a proof system. In particular the \emph{Crypto Guide} owns every
definition this volume instantiates: the non-interactive argument and
the SNARK, together with the polynomial interactive oracle proof and the
compilation recipe that turns one into the other
(\S``SNARKs: succinct non-interactive arguments of knowledge''); the
Fiat--Shamir transform (\S``The Fiat--Shamir transform: from interactive
to non-interactive''); the Pedersen vector commitment (\S``Pedersen
vector commitments''); and the polynomial-commitment interface, with
its sketch of an inner-product opening (\S``Polynomial commitment
schemes''). Where a lower volume's result is needed it is cited at the
point of use, by italic volume name and section title: the volumes are
separate documents, so there is no cross-document reference, and this
volume forward-references only its own later sections.

\subsection{The running example and the security notions}
\label{sec:intro-claim}

% toy_trace.py:
% row 0 (omega^0 =  1): (a, b, c) = (3, 3, 9);   selectors (q_M, q_L, q_R,
%   q_O, q_C) = (1, 0, 0, -1, 0); PI = 0
% row 1 (omega^1 = 22): (a, b, c) = (9, 3, 27);  selectors (1, 0, 0, -1, 0)
% row 2 (omega^2 = 96): (a, b, c) = (27, 3, 30); selectors (0, 1, 1, -1, 0)
% row 3 (omega^3 = 75): (a, b, c) = (30, 0, 0);  selectors (0, 1, 0, 0, 5);
%   PI = -35
% ipa_toy.py: y^2 = x^3 + 3 over F_79; #E(F_79) = 97, prime
The running example is the statement
\[
\text{knowledge of } x \in \F_{97} \text{ with } x^3 + x + 5 = 35,
\]
one of whose witnesses (the \emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Languages,
relations, and witnesses'') is $x = 3$, since $27 + 3 + 5 = 35$; the
others in $\F_{97}$ are $18$ and $76$, and the prover of the example
holds $x = 3$. It is arithmetised
in \S\ref{sec:arith}, committed and opened in \S\ref{sec:ipa}, bound to
its public input $35$ and compiled into a proof string in
\S\ref{sec:halo2}, and verified in \S\ref{sec:verdict}. Its field
arithmetic, that of the claim, the grid, the polynomials and every
scalar, is in the prime field $\F_{97}$ (the \emph{Math Guide},
\S``The prime field and two inversion routes''), a size at which every
product can be checked by hand; the commitments of \S\ref{sec:ipa} are
points of a curve of order $97$ whose coordinates lie in a second prime
field, $\F_{79}$. Every number printed is computed by script, and the
script's output is the source of the text.

A proof system for such a statement is required to have three
properties, each a definition of the \emph{Crypto Guide}
(\S``Interactive proofs, zero knowledge, and SNARKs'') used here without
restatement. \emph{Overwhelming completeness}: an honest prover holding
a witness is accepted with overwhelming probability, in the sense of the
\emph{Math Guide} (\S``Polynomial, exponential, and negligible
functions''). \emph{Knowledge soundness} (the \emph{Crypto Guide},
\S``Knowledge soundness and extractors''): an efficient extractor, given
rewindable access to any prover the verifier accepts with non-negligible
probability, outputs a valid witness. \emph{Zero knowledge} (the
\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``The simulation paradigm and zero knowledge''):
a simulator given only the public input produces transcripts
indistinguishable from those of real executions.

The volume establishes these properties to different extents. Design-stage
claims are classified in three grades: \emph{specified}, when the
protocol specification or a ZIP states the property;
\emph{designed-but-unspecified}, when the design intends it but no
specification, ZIP or theorem of this volume states it for the deployed
system; and \emph{open problem}, when no design is known to achieve it. The
Halo~2 protocol is complete except with negligible probability: for each random
challenge, a number of values at most proportional to the size of the circuit,
out of the $|\F|$ possible, makes undefined a division that the verifier or the
honest prover performs; \S\ref{sec:halo2-zk} lists these degenerate challenges
once the protocol is defined. The inner-product opening argument is knowledge
sound under the discrete-logarithm assumption on Vesta, with the hash that
derives its generators modelled as a random oracle (Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks}
and, for the deployed opening, Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks}). Knowledge
soundness of the deployed non-interactive argument rests on an analysis in the
algebraic group model and the random-oracle model whose instantiation to the
deployed transcript is designed-but-unspecified (\S\ref{sec:ks}). Zero knowledge
is recorded in \S\ref{sec:halo2-zk} from the halo2 book's claim for the
interactive protocol, under the composition and
Fiat--Shamir hypotheses stated there.

% orchard_params.py: d = n = 2^11 = 2048; k = 11 rounds
The running example has the form of the Orchard Action statement. The
prover of an Action shows knowledge of a note, the keys that control
it, and a Merkle path such that the note's commitment is well formed,
the path, unless the spent value is zero, leads from that commitment to
the published tree root (the
\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Merkle trees and commitments to sets''), the
net value commitment is a Pedersen commitment to the old value minus
the new value (the \emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``The Pedersen
commitment''), the nullifier, the unique tag whose publication marks
the note spent, is computed correctly, and the key that authorises the
spend is a re-randomisation of the owner's key (the \emph{Crypto
Guide}, \S``Key re-randomisation and unlinkability''). The protocol
specification states this as nine conditions on a public input and a private
witness
(\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actionstatement}{\S~4.18.4},
``Action Statement (Orchard)''), the five just paraphrased and four further:
that the spent note's address is derived from the same keys, that the new note's
commitment is well formed, and that two public bits, when zero, force the spent
value and the created value, respectively, to zero. From NU6.3 the circuit
checks a tenth condition on a further public bit, which restricts
cross-address transfers and is set for every Orchard-pool Action
(\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actiondesc}{\S~4.6};
\href{https://zips.z.cash/zip-0258}{ZIP~258}).
Each condition unfolds into small arithmetic facts over the field
$\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$ (the \emph{Math Guide}, \S``Base fields, scalar
fields, and the Pasta cycle''): the note commitment alone is a
Sinsemilla commitment, a Sinsemilla hash to a point plus a blinding
multiple of a fixed generator (the \emph{Crypto Guide},
\S``Sinsemilla: an algebraic hash-based commitment''), whose hash is
evaluated in the circuit with the help of a lookup table. Together the
conditions are laid out on the $2^{11} = 2048$ rows of the deployed
Action circuit; the lookup table and the row count are given in
\S\ref{sec:halo2}, ``From statement to circuit: arithmetisation in
practice''. The running example itself is a conjunction of \emph{four}
arithmetic facts, $x \cdot x = x^2$, $x^2 \cdot x = x^3$,
$x^3 + x = s$ and $s + 5 = 35$, one per row of the grid that
\S\ref{sec:arith} builds. Both the toy and the Action circuit are
instances of the constraint system that \S\ref{sec:arith} defines; the
toy's constraint on each row reads that row alone and consults no table.

\paragraph{The chain of reductions.} Five reductions connect the claim
to the checks the verifier performs.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Computation $\to$ grid} (\S\ref{sec:arith}, ``From
computation to a grid of constraints''). The claim is rewritten as a
table of rows, each row one elementary gate ($\times$ or $+$), with copy
constraints between rows forcing shared values.
\item \textbf{Grid $\to$ polynomials} (\S\ref{sec:arith}, ``From the
grid to polynomials''). Each column of the table is interpolated into a
polynomial (the \emph{Math Guide}, \S``Lagrange interpolation''). The
condition that every row obeys its gate becomes the condition that one
polynomial $G(X)$ is zero at every row.
\item \textbf{Vanishing $\to$ identity} (\S\ref{sec:arith}, ``From
vanishing to a single identity''). The statement that $G$ is zero at
every row becomes the algebraic identity $G(X) = t(X)\,\ZH(X)$ for some
quotient $t$, where $\ZH$ is the vanishing polynomial of the set $H$ of
row names (the \emph{Math Guide}, \S``The evaluation domain $H$ and its
vanishing polynomial''). A trace that satisfies every gate yields such
an identity, and a trace that violates a gate cannot. The copy
constraints are not part of $G$; the permutation argument enforces them
(item~5).
\item \textbf{Identity $\to$ one random check} (\S\ref{sec:arith},
``Why checking one random point is a proof''). The verifier tests the
identity at a single random point $z$. A false identity holds at a
uniformly random point with probability at most its degree divided by
the field size (the \emph{Math Guide}, \S``The Schwartz--Zippel
lemma'').
\item \textbf{Random check $\to$ committed openings.} A binding
commitment (the \emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Hiding and binding'') prevents
the prover from changing her polynomials after $z$ is drawn
(\S\ref{sec:arith}, ``Why the prover cannot wriggle: binding
commitments''), and the inner-product argument (IPA) of
\S\ref{sec:ipa} opens a commitment at $z$ without sending the
polynomial; the permutation argument enforces the copy constraints
(\S\ref{sec:arith}, ``Enforcing the wiring: the permutation argument'');
a dedicated public column, the instance column, fixes the public input
$35$ (\S\ref{sec:halo2}, ``Instance columns and binding the public
statement''); and blinding hides the witness (\S\ref{sec:halo2}, ``Zero
knowledge: hiding the witness in Halo 2'').
\end{enumerate}
Section~\ref{sec:verdict} composes the five reductions into one chain
of implications, from the verifier's accepted checks back to the claim.

% toy_trace.py:
% roots of D in F_97: [22, 75, 96] = H minus {1}:
%   Pr[random z hides this cheat] = 3/97 = 0.0309
% at Orchard scale: deg of the identity ~ 2^14, |F| = p_Pallas ~ 2^254.000:
%   2^14 / p = 2^(-240.0)
% pallas_constants.py:
% p_Pallas = 2^254 + 0x224698fc094cf91b992d30ed00000001 (255 bits)
\paragraph{The scale caveat, stated once.} The field $\F_{97}$ is a
toy. Over Orchard's field $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$, whose prime has $255$
bits, a \emph{fixed} false identity of degree around $2^{14}$ (the
degree of the deployed constraint polynomial, bounded in
\S\ref{sec:halo2}, ``The complete Halo 2 protocol'') survives one fresh
uniform evaluation challenge with probability around
$2^{14}/2^{254} = 2^{-240}$, in place of the visible $3/97$ that
\S\ref{sec:arith} exhibits for the toy's cheat. Every such number in
this volume is one Schwartz--Zippel term: the probability that one
random point hides one fixed lie. It is not the security level of the
complete proof system, which also rests on the binding of the
commitments, the extractor of the opening argument, and the loss of the
Fiat--Shamir transform, treated in \S\ref{sec:ipa}, \S\ref{sec:halo2}
and \S\ref{sec:ks}. The caveat is stated here and not re-derived; every
probability printed later is read with it.

\subsection{Outline}\label{sec:intro-road}

The sections follow the running example; Figure~\ref{fig:spine} shows
the section in which each stage is built.

Section~\ref{sec:arith} takes the example from the computation to committed
polynomials: the grid, the column polynomials, the single identity, the random
point, and then the general form of the constraint system, PLONKish
arithmetisation, of which the toy is an instance, followed by the permutation
argument for the copy constraints and the lookup argument for facts that no
low-degree gate states economically. It ends with the problem a vector
commitment alone does not solve: proving the value of a committed polynomial at
$z$ without sending the polynomial, in fewer group elements than the polynomial
has coefficients.

Section~\ref{sec:ipa} solves that problem with the inner-product argument. It
states the inner-product problem without machinery, works one fold with every
algebra step written out, gives the recursion with both parties' computations
side by side, makes the verifier's work explicit, proves the argument knowledge
sound by extraction from three transcripts under the discrete-logarithm
assumption with uniform generators, separates what the blinders hide from what
they do not, and opens the example's own polynomial through every round on a
curve of prime order $97$, by script. Its last subsection gives Halo~2's
parameters, the deployed form of the argument as a change of variables, the
bytes of a proof, the deferred multi-scalar multiplication and the batching that
amortises it, and accumulation, which Orchard does not deploy.

Section~\ref{sec:halo2} gives the deployed proof system: the Action
circuit's arithmetisation, the instance column that binds the public
statement, the Fiat--Shamir transform that replaces the verifier's
challenges by hash outputs, the reduction of many openings to one, the
complete protocol in seven phases, its zero knowledge, and the
compiled-polynomial-IOP pattern the protocol instantiates.

Section~\ref{sec:ks} treats knowledge soundness of the non-interactive
argument: the extractor of \S\ref{sec:ipa} when the challenges are hash
outputs, the algebraic group model in which the halo2 book proves its
tight bound for the analysed protocol, and the gap between concrete and
asymptotic security.

Section~\ref{sec:verdict} lists the verifier's checks on the running
example in the order he performs them, the chain of implications back
to the prover's knowledge of advice satisfying every gate and every copy
constraint, and the same chain at Orchard scale.

Section~\ref{sec:pcd} treats recursive proof composition, accumulation
schemes, the Halo trick, and the cycle of curves that makes native
recursion efficient. Orchard proves with Halo~2 on the Pasta curves but
does not recurse, and the section states at the outset that Orchard
does not deploy its material.

% 02-arith.tex -- sec:arith. Every number in this section is read from
% compute/OUTPUT.md, block toy_trace.py (and, for the three permutation
% products, block protocol_params.py); the relevant lines are repeated as
% comments above each passage that uses them.

\section{Arithmetisation: from a computation to committed polynomials}
\label{sec:arith}
\sectionmark{Arithmetisation}

This section carries the running example of \S\ref{sec:intro} --- the
prover knows $x = 3$ with $x^3 + x + 5 = 35$ over $\F_{97}$ --- from a
computation to a handful of polynomials that a verifier can query at one
point, toy first and general second. It ends with the property the
argument has not yet provided: that the queried polynomials are fixed
before the evaluation point is chosen.

\subsection{From computation to a grid of constraints}
\label{sec:arith-grid}

The verifier cannot evaluate the computation without the witness.
Arithmetisation dismantles the computation into \emph{local} facts, each
involving only a handful of values, arranged so that ``every local fact holds
and the values are consistently shared'' is equivalent to ``the whole
computation ran correctly''. The dialect of arithmetisation Halo~2 uses is
called \emph{PLONKish}; \S\ref{sec:arith-plonkish} defines it once the toy has
shown what it generalises.

\paragraph{Execution trace.}
Evaluate $x^3 + x + 5$ the way a machine would, naming every intermediate
value:
\[
x^2 = x \cdot x, \qquad x^3 = x^2 \cdot x, \qquad s = x^3 + x, \qquad
s + 5 = 35 .
\]
Four elementary operations, two multiplications and two additions, and each
becomes one row of a table. A row has three \emph{wires} $a, b, c$ (two
inputs and an output) and a bank of five \emph{selectors}
$q_M, q_L, q_R, q_O, q_C$ that switch on whichever gate the row performs.
The universal gate equation, imposed at every row, is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:arith-gate}
q_M\,a\,b + q_L\,a + q_R\,b + q_O\,c + q_C + \mathrm{PI} = 0,
\end{equation}
where $\mathrm{PI}$ carries the negated public input on that row (here
$-35$, for the target $35$). A multiplication gate $a \cdot b = c$ is the
choice $q_M = 1$, $q_O = -1$ and the rest zero; an addition gate
$a + b = c$ is $q_L = q_R = 1$, $q_O = -1$. Filling in the four
operations gives the
\emph{execution trace} of Table~\ref{tab:arith-trace}.
Definition~\ref{def:plonkish} generalises this fixed selector bank to
arbitrary custom gates and lookup arguments (the halo2 book's
\emph{PLONKish Arithmetization} chapter); \S\ref{sec:halo2} gives the
form of the Action circuit's gates, and one in full, under ``From
statement to circuit: arithmetisation in practice''.

% toy_trace.py, block "trace":
% row 0 (omega^0 =  1): (a, b, c) = (3, 3, 9); selectors (q_M, q_L, q_R, q_O, q_C) = (1, 0, 0, -1, 0); PI = 0
% row 1 (omega^1 = 22): (a, b, c) = (9, 3, 27); selectors (q_M, q_L, q_R, q_O, q_C) = (1, 0, 0, -1, 0); PI = 0
% row 2 (omega^2 = 96): (a, b, c) = (27, 3, 30); selectors (q_M, q_L, q_R, q_O, q_C) = (0, 1, 1, -1, 0); PI = 0
% row 3 (omega^3 = 75): (a, b, c) = (30, 0, 0); selectors (q_M, q_L, q_R, q_O, q_C) = (0, 1, 0, 0, 5); PI = -35
\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{c ccc c ccccc c l}
\toprule
row & $a$ & $b$ & $c$ & \phantom{x} & $q_M$ & $q_L$ & $q_R$ & $q_O$ & $q_C$
  & $\mathrm{PI}$ & gate performed \\
\midrule
$0$ & $3$  & $3$ & $9$
  && $1$ & $0$ & $0$ & $-1$ & $0$ & $0$   & $x \cdot x = x^2$ \\
$1$ & $9$  & $3$ & $27$
  && $1$ & $0$ & $0$ & $-1$ & $0$ & $0$   & $x^2 \cdot x = x^3$ \\
$2$ & $27$ & $3$ & $30$
  && $0$ & $1$ & $1$ & $-1$ & $0$ & $0$   & $x^3 + x = s$ \\
$3$ & $30$ & $0$ & $0$
  && $0$ & $1$ & $0$ & $0$  & $5$ & $-35$ & $s + 5 = 35$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The execution trace for $x = 3$, all entries in $\F_{97}$ (so
$-1 = 96$ and $-35 = 62$). Every row satisfies the gate
equation~\eqref{eq:arith-gate}: row $0$ reads
$1 \cdot 3 \cdot 3 + (-1) \cdot 9 = 0$ and row $3$ reads
$1 \cdot 30 + 5 + (-35) = 0$. The selector columns $q_\bullet$ are fixed
circuit data and $\mathrm{PI}$ is the negated public input, both known to the
verifier; the wires $a, b, c$ are the prover's \emph{advice}, her secret
trace.}
\label{tab:arith-trace}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Copy constraints.}
The table as printed does not yet encode the computation. Nothing forces the $x$
in row~$0$ to equal the $x$ in rows~$1$ and~$2$, nor the output $x^2 = 9$ of
row~$0$ to be the input of row~$1$. A cheating prover could put $x = 3$ in
row~$0$ and $x = 8$ in row~$2$ and satisfy each gate \emph{in isolation} without
the rows forming one computation. The missing assertions are that certain cells
are \emph{equal}, the \emph{copy constraints} (also called \emph{equality
constraints}):
\[
\underbrace{a_0 = b_0 = b_1 = b_2}_{\text{all are } x = 3}, \qquad
\underbrace{c_0 = a_1}_{x^2 = 9}, \qquad
\underbrace{c_1 = a_2}_{x^3 = 27}, \qquad
\underbrace{c_2 = a_3}_{s = 30},
\]
where $a_j$ denotes the cell of column $a$ in row $j$.
Figure~\ref{fig:toy-grid} draws them as wires. They turn a list of
unrelated gates into a single dataflow; enforcing them is an argument in
its own right, the permutation argument of \S\ref{sec:arith-perm}.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  cell/.style={draw, fill=white, minimum width=1.25cm, minimum height=0.6cm,
    inner sep=2pt, font=\small},
  wire/.style={line width=1.1pt, -{Latex[length=2.2mm]}, rounded corners=3pt},
  x=1.65cm, y=-1.15cm]
  \node[font=\small\itshape] at (0,-0.7) {$a$};
  \node[font=\small\itshape] at (1,-0.7) {$b$};
  \node[font=\small\itshape] at (2,-0.7) {$c$};
  \foreach \j/\va/\vb/\vc/\gate in {0/3/3/9/{$x \cdot x = x^2$},
      1/9/3/27/{$x^2 \cdot x = x^3$}, 2/27/3/30/{$x^3 + x = s$},
      3/30/0/0/{$s + 5 = 35$}} {
    \node[font=\small] at (-0.85,\j) {row $\j$};
    \node[cell] (a\j) at (0,\j) {$\va$};
    \node[cell] (b\j) at (1,\j) {$\vb$};
    \node[cell] (c\j) at (2,\j) {$\vc$};
    \node[font=\small, anchor=west] at (2.55,\j) {\gate};
  }
  \begin{scope}[on background layer]
    \draw[wire, tierin] (a0.east) -- (b0.west);
    \draw[wire, tierin] (b0.south) -- (b1.north);
    \draw[wire, tierin] (b1.south) -- (b2.north);
    \draw[wire, tierrecv] (c0.south) -- ++(0cm,-0.28cm) -| (a1.north);
    \draw[wire, tierfull] (c1.south) -- ++(0cm,-0.28cm) -| (a2.north);
    \draw[wire, tierspend] (c2.south) -- ++(0cm,-0.28cm) -| (a3.north);
  \end{scope}
  \node[font=\small, anchor=north, text width=7.2cm, align=flush center]
    at ([yshift=-3mm]current bounding box.south) {wires: $x$ shared by
    rows $0$--$2$ ({\color{tierin}blue}); the output of each row fed to
    the next
    ({\color{tierrecv}$c_0 \to a_1$}, {\color{tierfull}$c_1 \to a_2$},
    {\color{tierspend}$c_2 \to a_3$}).};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The four-row trace of Table~\ref{tab:arith-trace} with the copy
constraints drawn as wires. Each wire colour is one class of cells that
must hold a single value: the blue wire ties the four occurrences of $x$,
and the three coloured wires carry each row's output $c_j$ into the next
row's input $a_{j+1}$. The gates check rows; the wires make the rows one
computation.}
\label{fig:toy-grid}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Satisfiability of the grid.}
The claim is now equivalent to a purely combinatorial assertion:
\emph{there exist values for the advice cells $a, b, c$ such that (i) every
row satisfies the gate equation~\eqref{eq:arith-gate} with the fixed
selectors, and (ii) every copy constraint holds.} Knowing $x$ is exactly
knowing how to fill the advice columns. The verifier knows the fixed
columns and the public input; the prover must convince him that satisfying
advice exists while revealing none of it. Everything that follows is
machinery for doing precisely that.

\subsection{From the grid to polynomials}
\label{sec:arith-polys}

The grid has finitely many rows, and the aim is a statement about all rows
at once that the verifier can check at \emph{one} point. A polynomial of
degree less than $n$ is determined by its values at $n$ distinct points
(\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Lagrange interpolation''), and the rest of the
section relies on this.

\paragraph{An index set for the rows.}
The table has $n = 4$ rows, and four distinct field elements must name
them. The chosen four are the $4$th roots of unity. A \emph{primitive
$n$-th root of unity} is an element $\omega$ with $\omega^n = 1$ and no
smaller positive power equal to $1$ (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Roots of
unity''), and the \emph{evaluation domain} it generates is
$H = \{\omega^0, \dots, \omega^{n-1}\}$, a multiplicative subgroup of
$\F^\times$ of order $n$ (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The evaluation domain $H$
and its vanishing polynomial''). In $\F_{97}$ take $\omega = 22$: then
$\omega^2 = 96 = -1$ and $\omega^4 = 1$, so
% toy_trace.py: F_97: omega = 22 (omega^2 = 96 = -1), H = [1, 22, 96, 75]
\[
H = \{\omega^0, \omega^1, \omega^2, \omega^3\} = \{1, 22, 96, 75\}
  = \{1, 22, -1, -22\},
\]
and row $i$ lives at the point $\omega^i$ (Figure~\ref{fig:toy-domain}).
Roots of unity are chosen
because they form a cyclic group: the row after $\omega^i$ is
$\omega \cdot \omega^i$, so reading a column at the next row is evaluating
its polynomial at $\omega X$; the polynomial vanishing on $H$ is
$X^n - 1$; and interpolation over $H$ is a fast Fourier transform
(\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The fast Fourier transform''). The gate check of
this subsection alone would hold over any four distinct points.

% numbers.py (.wip/deck-harvest/scripts/math-halo2/), item 4:
% omega = 22: H = [1, 22, 96, 75], each step x -> 22x mod 97,
%   75*22 = 1650 = 17*97 + 1
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  pt/.style={draw, fill=white, minimum width=1.9cm, minimum height=0.65cm,
    inner sep=2pt, font=\small},
  mul/.style={line width=0.9pt, tierin, -{Latex[length=2.2mm]}},
  lab/.style={font=\small, text=tierin!80!black},
  x=1cm, y=1cm]
  \node[pt] (w0) at (0,0)    {$\omega^0 = 1$};
  \node[pt] (w1) at (4,0)    {$\omega^1 = 22$};
  \node[pt] (w2) at (4,-1.8) {$\omega^2 = 96$};
  \node[pt] (w3) at (0,-1.8) {$\omega^3 = 75$};
  \node[font=\small, anchor=east] at (w0.west) {row $0$\ };
  \node[font=\small, anchor=west] at (w1.east) {\ row $1$};
  \node[font=\small, anchor=west] at (w2.east) {\ row $2$};
  \node[font=\small, anchor=east] at (w3.west) {row $3$\ };
  \draw[mul] (w0) -- node[lab, above] {$\cdot\,\omega$} (w1);
  \draw[mul] (w1) -- node[lab, right] {$\cdot\,\omega$} (w2);
  \draw[mul] (w2) -- node[lab, below] {$\cdot\,\omega$} (w3);
  \draw[mul] (w3) -- node[lab, left] {$\cdot\,\omega$} (w0);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The evaluation domain $H$ of the toy in $\F_{97}$, generated by
$\omega = 22$. Row $i$ sits at $\omega^i$, and each arrow is
multiplication by $\omega$, so following an arrow moves to the next row.
Four steps return to $\omega^0 = 1$, since $\omega^4 = 1$: the last arrow
is $75 \cdot 22 = 1650 = 17 \cdot 97 + 1$.}
\label{fig:toy-domain}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Columns become polynomials.}
Each column of the table is four numbers, one per row. Interpolate: for
the advice column $a$ there is a unique polynomial $a(X)$ of degree $< 4$
with
\[
a(\omega^0) = 3, \quad a(\omega^1) = 9, \quad a(\omega^2) = 27, \quad
a(\omega^3) = 30
\]
(\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Lagrange interpolation''), and likewise $b(X)$,
$c(X)$, the publicly known selector polynomials $q_M, q_L, q_R, q_O, q_C$
and the public-input polynomial $\mathrm{PI}$, each of degree $< 4$.
Computed over $\F_{97}$, the nine interpolants are
% toy_trace.py, block "interpolants (coefficients low degree first)":
% a   (X) = 90 + 61X + 22X^2 + 24X^3;  on H: [3, 9, 27, 30]
% b   (X) = 75 + 32X + 25X^2 + 65X^3;  on H: [3, 3, 3, 0]
% c   (X) = 65 + 16X + 3X^2 + 22X^3;  on H: [9, 27, 30, 0]
% q_M (X) = 49 + 19X + 30X^3;  on H: [1, 1, 0, 0]
% q_L (X) = 49 + 78X + 67X^3;  on H: [0, 0, 1, 1]
% q_R (X) = 73 + 24X + 73X^2 + 24X^3;  on H: [0, 0, 1, 0]
% q_O (X) = 72 + 54X + 24X^2 + 43X^3;  on H: [96, 96, 96, 0]
% q_C (X) = 74 + 76X + 23X^2 + 21X^3;  on H: [0, 0, 0, 5]
% PI  (X) = 64 + 50X + 33X^2 + 47X^3;  on H: [0, 0, 0, 62]
\begin{align*}
a(X) &= 90 + 61X + 22X^2 + 24X^3, &
q_M(X) &= 49 + 19X + 30X^3, \\
b(X) &= 75 + 32X + 25X^2 + 65X^3, &
q_L(X) &= 49 + 78X + 67X^3, \\
c(X) &= 65 + 16X + 3X^2 + 22X^3, &
q_R(X) &= 73 + 24X + 73X^2 + 24X^3, \\
\mathrm{PI}(X) &= 64 + 50X + 33X^2 + 47X^3, &
q_O(X) &= 72 + 54X + 24X^2 + 43X^3, \\
&& q_C(X) &= 74 + 76X + 23X^2 + 21X^3,
\end{align*}
and each returns its column at the rows: $b$ takes the values
$(3, 3, 3, 0)$ on $H$, $q_O$ the values $(96, 96, 96, 0)$, and
$\mathrm{PI}$ the values $(0, 0, 0, 62)$. Interpolation is thus a
bijection between columns of four values and polynomials of degree
$< 4$: the encoding loses no information.

\paragraph{The gate polynomial.}
Assemble the left-hand side of the gate equation~\eqref{eq:arith-gate}
with the polynomials in place of the per-row numbers.

\begin{theorem}[The gate polynomial]\label{thm:arith-gate-vanish}
Let
\begin{equation}\label{eq:arith-Gpoly}
G(X) := q_M(X)\,a(X)\,b(X) + q_L(X)\,a(X) + q_R(X)\,b(X)
        + q_O(X)\,c(X) + q_C(X) + \mathrm{PI}(X).
\end{equation}
Then $\deg G \le 9$, and $G(\omega^i) = 0$ for every row $i$ if and only if
every gate of the trace is satisfied.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The term $q_M a b$ multiplies three polynomials of degree at most $3$, and
every other term has degree at most $6$, so $\deg G \le 9$. Evaluate $G$ at
the label $\omega^i$ of row $i$: each column polynomial returns that row's
value there, so $G(\omega^i)$ is exactly the left-hand side
of~\eqref{eq:arith-gate} for row $i$. It is zero precisely when gate $i$
holds, and the conjunction over $i$ gives the claim.
\end{proof}

% toy_trace.py, block "identity":
% G(X) = 36 + 27X + 54X^2 + 50X^3 + 77X^4 + 24X^5 + 43X^6 + 47X^7 + 81X^8 + 46X^9, deg G = 9; G on H = [0, 0, 0, 0]
For the honest trace,
\[
G(X) = 36 + 27X + 54X^2 + 50X^3 + 77X^4 + 24X^5 + 43X^6 + 47X^7 + 81X^8
       + 46X^9,
\]
of degree exactly $9$, and it vanishes at all four points of $H$ by
construction. ``Every gate is satisfied'' has become ``$G$ vanishes on
$H$''; the copy constraints remain separate. Four separate checks are now
one statement about one polynomial.

\subsection{From vanishing to a single identity}
\label{sec:arith-vanish}

The condition ``$G$ vanishes on $H$'' is still a statement about four
points. This subsection replaces it by a polynomial identity that refers
to no individual row.

\paragraph{The vanishing polynomial.}
The \emph{vanishing polynomial} of $H$ is the monic polynomial (leading
coefficient $1$) whose roots are exactly the points of $H$,
\[
\ZH(X) := \prod_{i=0}^{3} (X - \omega^i) = X^4 - 1 ,
\]
where the closed form $X^n - 1$ holds for every evaluation domain of order
$n$ (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The evaluation domain $H$ and its vanishing
polynomial''): each $\omega^i$ is a root of $X^n - 1$, there are $n$
distinct ones, and comparing leading coefficients finishes. The closed
form lets both parties evaluate $\ZH$ at any point with $O(\log n)$
multiplications.

\begin{theorem}[Gate satisfaction is a quotient]\label{thm:arith-quotient}
Every gate of the trace holds if and only if there is a polynomial $t(X)$
with $G(X) = t(X)\,\ZH(X)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-gate-vanish}, every gate holds if and only if
$G$ vanishes on $H$. The factor theorem (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Roots and
the factor theorem'') gives $G(\omega^i) = 0$ if and only if
$(X - \omega^i) \mid G$; and because the four roots are distinct, $G$
vanishes at all of them if and only if the product of the four linear
factors divides $G$ (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Lagrange interpolation'', its
remark on the vanishing polynomial: a polynomial vanishes on a set of
distinct nodes if and only if the product of the corresponding linear
factors divides it).
Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:arith-divis}
G \text{ vanishes on } H
\iff (X - \omega^i) \mid G \text{ for each } i
\iff \ZH(X) \mid G(X),
\end{equation}
and divisibility is the existence of the quotient $t$.
\end{proof}

Satisfaction of the \emph{gate} constraints is precisely the existence of
$t$; correct wiring still requires the copy constraints. For the honest
trace the division comes out even: $G$ has degree $9$, $\ZH$ has degree
$4$, and
% toy_trace.py: t(X) = G / Z_H = 61 + 70X + 43X^2 + 47X^3 + 81X^4 + 46X^5, deg t = 5, remainder 0
\[
t(X) = \frac{G(X)}{X^4 - 1} = 61 + 70X + 43X^2 + 47X^3 + 81X^4 + 46X^5
\]
is a polynomial of degree $5$ with remainder zero, as it must be.

\begin{example}[Why a cheat cannot produce $t$]\label{ex:arith-cheat}
Suppose the advice is wrong: some gate fails. Then $G$ does not vanish on
all of $H$, so by~\eqref{eq:arith-divis} $\ZH$ does not divide $G$, and
\emph{no} polynomial $t$ satisfies $G = t\,\ZH$. The dividing line is
exact: the quotient exists if and only if every gate is satisfied.
Concretely, take a prover who claims $x^2 = 10$ and writes $c_0 = 10$ in
row~$0$ of Table~\ref{tab:arith-trace} instead of $9$. Row~$0$'s gate now
reads $3 \cdot 3 - 10 = -1 \ne 0$, so the tampered gate polynomial
$G^{*}$ has $G^{*}(\omega^0) = -1 = 96$ while still vanishing at the other
three rows:
% toy_trace.py, block "the cheat x^2 = 10":
% G*(X) = 54 + 10X + 43X^2 + 74X^3 + 83X^4 + 65X^5 + 78X^6 + 47X^7 + 81X^8 + 46X^9, deg 9; t*(X) = 67 + 14X + 78X^2 + 47X^3 + 81X^4 + 46X^5; gap D = G* - t* Z_H = 24 + 24X + 24X^2 + 24X^3 = 24(1 + X + X^2 + X^3)
\[
G^{*}(X) = 54 + 10X + 43X^2 + 74X^3 + 83X^4 + 65X^5 + 78X^6 + 47X^7
           + 81X^8 + 46X^9 .
\]
Dividing $G^{*}$ by $\ZH$ with remainder (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Division
with remainder'') gives the quotient
$t^{*}(X) = 67 + 14X + 78X^2 + 47X^3 + 81X^4 + 46X^5$ and a nonzero
remainder. This Euclidean quotient is an illustrative choice for
$t^{*}$, not a claim that it maximises the cheat's chances. Whatever
$t^{*}$ she offers as the quotient, the \emph{gap polynomial}
$D(X) := G^{*}(X) - t^{*}(X)\,\ZH(X)$ is nonzero, since $\ZH$ does not
divide $G^{*}$. For the Euclidean quotient $D$ is the remainder,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:arith-D}
D(X) = 24\,(1 + X + X^2 + X^3),
\end{equation}
of degree $3$. The identity $G^{*} = t^{*}\,\ZH$ is false, $D$ is the
difference of its two sides, and \S\ref{sec:arith-random} shows how the
verifier detects $D \ne 0$.
\end{example}

\subsection{Why checking one random point is a proof}
\label{sec:arith-random}

The prover wishes to convince the verifier that $G(X) = t(X)\,\ZH(X)$ as
polynomials, with $G$ built from her secret advice. The verifier does not
read the polynomials: they encode the witness, and reading them would cost
time at least linear in the number of rows. Instead, the two run a
four-step protocol. A \emph{commitment} is, for now, a short string that fixes a
value without revealing it (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Commitment
schemes''); \S\ref{sec:arith-commit} says what a commitment to a
polynomial must do and which one Halo~2 uses.

\begin{construction}[The random-point check]
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=1pt]
\item The prover commits to her polynomials $a, b, c$ and to the quotient
  $t$; the selector and public-input polynomials the verifier already
  knows.
\item The verifier picks a point $z \in \F$ \emph{uniformly at random} and
  sends it.
\item The prover \emph{opens} her commitments at $z$: she returns the field
  elements $a(z), b(z), c(z), t(z)$ together with short proofs that these
  are the true evaluations of the committed polynomials.
\item The verifier forms $G(z)$ from the openings and the self-evaluated
  public polynomials via~\eqref{eq:arith-Gpoly}, computes
  $\ZH(z) = z^4 - 1$, and checks the single field equation
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:arith-check}
  G(z) \overset{?}{=} t(z)\,\ZH(z).
  \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
He accepts if and only if~\eqref{eq:arith-check} holds.
\end{construction}

\paragraph{The honest case (completeness).}
If the prover is honest then $G = t\,\ZH$ holds as polynomials, hence at
every point, hence at $z$. With the honest trace and the draw $z = 20$,
% toy_trace.py, block "z = 20":
% Z_H(20) = 46, t(20) = 67, G(20) = 75; t(20) Z_H(20) = 75: accepted
\[
\ZH(20) = 20^4 - 1 = 46, \qquad t(20) = 67, \qquad
G(20) = 75 = 67 \cdot 46 = t(20)\,\ZH(20),
\]
and the verifier accepts. He accepts every time, for every $z$.

\paragraph{The dishonest case (soundness).}
Soundness rests on the fact that a nonzero polynomial of low degree has
few roots.

\begin{theorem}[Soundness of the random-point check]\label{thm:arith-random}
Suppose the trace is invalid, so that the prover's gate polynomial $G^{*}$
does not vanish on all of $H$, and let $t^{*}$ be any polynomial she
commits to as the quotient. Set $D(X) := G^{*}(X) - t^{*}(X)\,\ZH(X)$.
Then $D$ is a nonzero polynomial, and if the prover's polynomials
$a, b, c$ (which determine $G^{*}$) and $t^{*}$ are fixed before $z$ is
drawn and the verifier receives their true evaluations at $z$,
\[
\Pr_{z}\bigl[\text{the verifier accepts}\bigr]
  = \Pr_{z}\bigl[D(z) = 0\bigr] \le \frac{\deg D}{|\F|} .
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Were $D$ the zero polynomial, $G^{*} = t^{*}\,\ZH$ would be divisible by
$\ZH$ and so, by~\eqref{eq:arith-divis}, vanish on all of $H$, which it
does not. Hence $D \ne 0$ for every choice of $t^{*}$. By hypothesis
$a, b, c$ and $t^{*}$, and with them $G^{*}$ and $D$, are fixed before
$z$ is drawn (the verifier never receives $G^{*}$ itself; from the
evaluations at $z$ he reconstructs only the single number $G^{*}(z)$).
The check~\eqref{eq:arith-check}
passes at $z$ exactly when $G^{*}(z) - t^{*}(z)\,\ZH(z) = D(z) = 0$, that
is, exactly when $z$ is a root of $D$. A nonzero polynomial of degree at
most $\deg D$ has at most $\deg D$ roots, so a uniformly random $z$ is one
of them with probability at most $\deg D / |\F|$ (\emph{Math Guide},
\S``The Schwartz--Zippel lemma'').
\end{proof}

The bound $\deg D/|\F|$ is negligible when $|\F|$ is exponential in the
security parameter and $\deg D$ is polynomial in it. The verifier does
not recompute the trace; he tests one polynomial identity at one random
point.

\begin{example}[The random-point check on the toy]\label{ex:arith-random-toy}
For the $x^2 = 10$ cheat of Example~\ref{ex:arith-cheat},
$D(X) = 24(1 + X + X^2 + X^3)$ has degree $3$ and so at most $3$ roots;
% toy_trace.py: roots of D in F_97: [22, 75, 96] = H minus {1}: Pr[random z hides this cheat] = 3/97 = 0.0309
in $\F_{97}$ its roots are exactly $\{22, 96, 75\}$, the three rows where
the tampered gate still held (Figure~\ref{fig:gap}). This particular
quotient choice slips past only if the random $z$ lands in that
three-element set:
\[
\Pr[\text{cheat accepted}] = \frac{3}{97} .
\]
% toy_trace.py: cheat at z = 20: G*(20) = 20 != t*(20) Z_H(20) = 64: rejected
At the actual draw $z = 20 \notin \{22, 96, 75\}$ the openings give
$G^{*}(20) = 20$ but $t^{*}(20)\,\ZH(20) = 64$; since $20 \ne 64$, the
verifier rejects.
\end{example}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.125cm, font=\small]
  \draw[-{Latex[length=2mm]}] (-2,0) -- (103,0)
    node[right] {$\F_{97}$};
  \foreach \x in {0,10,...,90}
    \draw (\x,0.1) -- (\x,-0.1) node[below, font=\scriptsize] {$\x$};
  \draw (96,0.1) -- (96,-0.1) node[below, font=\scriptsize] {$96$};
  \draw[gray] (1,0) circle (2pt);
  \node[gray, above, font=\scriptsize] at (1,0.25) {$1 \in H$};
  \foreach \x in {22,75,96} \fill[tierspend] (\x,0) circle (2.4pt);
  \node[tierspend, above] at (22,0.7) {$22$};
  \node[tierspend, above] at (75,0.7) {$75$};
  \node[tierspend, above] at (96,0.7) {$96$};
  \fill[tierin] (20,0) circle (2.4pt);
  \draw[tierin] (20,-0.15) -- (14,-1.3);
  \node[tierin, below left, font=\small] at (14.5,-1.2) {$z = 20$};
  \node[anchor=north west, text width=12.4cm, align=flush left, font=\small]
    at (-2,-2.2) {{\color{tierspend}filled}: the roots of the gap
    polynomial $D$, the set $H \setminus \{1\}$, where the tampered
    identity still holds; $D(z) = 0$ on $3$ of the $97$ points only.
    {\color{tierin}blue}: the draw $z = 20$, where $D(20) \ne 0$ and the
    check rejects, as it does at every unmarked point.};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The gap polynomial $D = 24(1 + X + X^2 + X^3)$ of the $x^2 = 10$
cheat over $\F_{97}$: its three roots $22, 75, 96$ marked in red, the
point $1 \in H$ (not a root) in grey, and the verifier's draw $z = 20$
landing elsewhere. The check~\eqref{eq:arith-check} passes only at a root
of $D$.}
\label{fig:gap}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Scale.}
% toy_trace.py: general bound: deg G* = 9, deg t* <= 2n - 1 = 7 (two pieces of
%   degree < n = 4): deg D <= 11: 11/97 = 0.1134
The fraction $3/97$ is large only because the field is a toy, and it
concerns one particular quotient; the next paragraph bounds every cheat
by $11/97$. What such a term means at the deployed field size is the scale
caveat of \S\ref{sec:intro-claim}.

\paragraph{Bounding the degree.}
The argument needs $\deg D$ small relative to $|\F|$, otherwise
``$\deg D$ roots out of $|\F|$'' is no constraint at all. The commitment
scheme enforces a degree bound on every committed polynomial
(\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Polynomial commitment schemes''), so the prover
cannot commit to a $t^{*}$ of unbounded degree. The bound follows the
degree the commitment admits, not the honest degree. The wires are
committed at degree below $n = 4$, so $\deg G^{*} \le 9$; committed as
the two pieces of degree below $n$ that the deployed rule below
prescribes, $t^{*}$ has degree at most $7$, so $\deg(t^{*}\ZH) \le 11$
and $\deg D \le 11$. Every cheat is therefore accepted with probability
at most $11/97$, and some cheat attains it, where the illustrative
quotient reaches $3/97$:
% toy_trace.py: general bound: deg G* = 9, deg t* <= 2n - 1 = 7 (two pieces of
%   degree < n = 4): deg D <= 11: 11/97 = 0.1134
% toy_trace.py: attained: t* interpolating G*/Z_H at z = 2..9 has deg 7; D has
%   deg 11 and roots [2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 22, 75, 96]: 11/97
the $t^{*}$ of degree $7$ that agrees with $G^{*}/\ZH$ at the eight
points $2, \dots, 9$ gives $D$ the eleven roots $2, \dots, 9, 22, 75, 96$.
This is why a circuit's gates are kept to low degree, and why the quotient
$t$, of degree $5$ here and up to a few times $n$ in general, is split
into degree-bounded pieces. In the deployed system the quotient is cut into
pieces of degree below $n$, each committed separately (the halo2 book's
\emph{Vanishing argument} chapter); ``The complete Halo 2 protocol'' in
\S\ref{sec:halo2} gives the count.

\subsection{PLONKish arithmetisation}
\label{sec:arith-plonkish}

The toy has met every ingredient concretely: an evaluation domain $H$, a
generator $\omega$, the vanishing polynomial $\ZH$, columns interpolated
into polynomials of degree $< n$, and a gate expressed as a polynomial
identity on $H$. The general object can now be named.

\begin{definition}[PLONKish constraint system]\label{def:plonkish}
Fix a prime field $\F$ and an integer $k$ with $2^k \mid |\F| - 1$, so
that $\F^\times$ contains a primitive $2^k$-th root of unity $\omega$
(\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Existence over finite fields: the condition
$n \mid q-1$''), and let
$H := \{\omega^0, \dots, \omega^{n-1}\} \subset \F^\times$ be the
multiplicative subgroup of size $n := 2^k$ that $\omega$ generates. Its
vanishing polynomial is $\ZH(X) := X^n - 1$, zero exactly on $H$, so a
congruence $p(X) \equiv 0 \pmod{\ZH(X)}$ means ``$p$ is zero at every
row $\omega^i$'', that is, $\ZH \mid p$. A \emph{PLONKish circuit}
$\mathcal C$ over $H$ consists of:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=1pt]
\item a finite set $\mathrm{Col}$ of columns, each of $n$ cells, one per
  row, partitioned into \emph{fixed} columns (preprocessed circuit data,
  known to both parties), \emph{advice} columns (the prover's witness)
  and \emph{instance} columns (public input); a \emph{cell} is an element
  of $H \times \mathrm{Col}$, and the circuit assigns every cell of a
  fixed column its value;
\item a maximum constraint degree $d_{\max}$;
\item a finite set $R \subset \Z$ of \emph{rotations} containing $0$; a
  \emph{column expression} is a polynomial over $\F$ in the values
  $v(\omega^r X)$ for $v \in \mathrm{Col}$ and $r \in R$, where the value
  of $v$ at rotation $r$ at row $i$ is the cell of $v$ in row $i + r$
  taken modulo $n$;
\item a finite sequence of \emph{custom gates} $g_j = 0$, each $g_j$ a
  column expression of degree at most $d_{\max}$;
\item a set $H_{\mathrm{act}} \subseteq H$ of \emph{active} rows: all of
  $H$ in the toy, and in the deployed system the first $u < n$ rows
  $\omega^0, \dots, \omega^{u-1}$, the trailing rows being reserved for a
  boundary row and blinding (Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows} fixes $u$);
\item a subset $E \subseteq H_{\mathrm{act}} \times \mathrm{Col}$ of
  \emph{equality-constrained} cells together with a permutation $\sigma$
  of $E$ (a bijection $E \to E$) whose cycles, the orbits
  $\{\kappa, \sigma(\kappa), \sigma^2(\kappa), \dots\}$ of the cells
  $\kappa \in E$, are the classes of cells required to hold a common
  value;
\item a set of \emph{lookup arguments}, each an input tuple
  $(A_0, \dots, A_{m-1})$ of column expressions and a table tuple
  $(S_0, \dots, S_{m-1})$ of columns.
\end{enumerate}
An \emph{assignment} gives every cell a value in $\F$, the fixed cells
their circuit values; write $v_\kappa$ for the value of cell $\kappa$. It
is \emph{satisfying} if every gate vanishes at every row of $H$ (in the
deployed system the selectors are zero outside $H_{\mathrm{act}}$),
$v_\kappa = v_{\sigma(\kappa)}$ for every $\kappa \in E$, and for every
lookup and every active row $\omega^i$ the tuple
$(A_0(\omega^i), \dots, A_{m-1}(\omega^i))$ equals
$(S_0(\omega^{i'}), \dots, S_{m-1}(\omega^{i'}))$ for some active row
$\omega^{i'}$. The \emph{relation} $\Rel_{\mathcal C}$ of the circuit is
the set of pairs (instance-column values, advice-column values) of the
satisfying assignments.
\end{definition}

The letter $d$ is reserved, from \S\ref{sec:ipa} on, for the length of a
committed vector; the constraint degree is therefore written $d_{\max}$
throughout this volume.

\paragraph{Vanilla PLONK as the special case.}
The original PLONK system of Gabizon, Williamson and Ciobotaru uses a
single fixed five-selector universal gate,
\begin{multline*}
q_M(X)\,a(X)\,b(X) + q_L(X)\,a(X) + q_R(X)\,b(X) + q_O(X)\,c(X)\\
+ q_C(X) + \mathrm{PI}(X) \equiv 0 \pmod{\ZH(X)},
\end{multline*}
with three wire polynomials $a, b, c$, selector polynomials
$q_M,\allowbreak q_L,\allowbreak q_R,\allowbreak q_O,\allowbreak q_C$ and the
public-input polynomial $\mathrm{PI}$,
carrying the negated public inputs; the toy's gate~\eqref{eq:arith-gate}
is exactly this gate, with $\mathrm{PI}$ held in an instance column.
PLONKish generalises it by permitting designer-chosen gates of higher
arity and degree, each switched by its own selector $q_i(X)$, a fixed
column that vanishes on the rows where the gate is inactive. In the toy
the five selectors are fixed columns, $a, b, c$ are advice, $\mathrm{PI}$
is the instance column, the single gate has degree $3$ and reads rotation
$0$ only, and the copy
constraints of \S\ref{sec:arith-grid} are the equality set $E$ with
$\sigma$ cycling each class; there are no lookups until
\S\ref{sec:arith-lookups}. Figure~\ref{fig:plonkish-grid} shows the
general shape.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.95cm, y=-0.78cm, font=\small,
  col/.style={draw, minimum width=0.85cm, minimum height=0.6cm,
    inner sep=1pt},
  ann/.style={anchor=west, font=\small, text width=5.1cm, align=flush left},
  lead/.style={-{Latex[length=1.8mm]}, thin}]
  \node[tierfull!80!black] at (0.5,-1.55) {fixed};
  \node[tierin!80!black] at (3,-1.55) {advice};
  \node[tierrecv!80!black] at (5,-1.55) {instance};
  \node[black!60] at (6.6,-1.55) {table};
  \node at (0,-0.8) {$q_0$}; \node at (1,-0.8) {$q_1$};
  \node at (2,-0.8) {$A_0$}; \node at (3,-0.8) {$A_1$};
  \node at (4,-0.8) {$A_2$}; \node at (5,-0.8) {$I$};
  \node at (6.6,-0.8) {$S$};
  \foreach \r/\lab in {0/{$\omega^0$},1/{$\omega^1$},2/{$\omega^2$},
      3/{$\omega^3$},5/{$\omega^{n-1}$}} {
    \node at (-1.05,\r) {\lab};
    \node[col, fill=tierfull!12] at (0,\r) {};
    \node[col, fill=tierfull!12] at (1,\r) {};

    \node[col, fill=tierin!12] (A1r\r) at (3,\r) {};
    \node[col, fill=tierin!12] (A2r\r) at (4,\r) {};

  }
  \foreach \c in {-1.05,0,1,2,3,4,5,6.6} \node at (\c,4) {$\vdots$};
  % gate reading rotations 0 and +1 at rows omega^1, omega^2
  \draw[thick, tierin!80!black, rounded corners=2pt]
    (1.5,0.55) rectangle (4.5,2.45);
  \node[ann] (gate) at (7.5,0.6) {gate at row $\omega^1$: reads
    rotation $0$\\(row $\omega^1$) and rotation $+1$\\(row $\omega^2$),
    degree $\le d_{\max}$};
  \draw[lead, tierin!80!black] (gate.west) -- (4.55,1.0);
  % copy constraint cycle between two advice cells
  \draw[thick, tierrecv, {Latex[length=1.8mm]}-{Latex[length=1.8mm]}]
    (A0r0.south) .. controls (2,2.2) and (3.4,2.6) .. (A2r3.north);
  \node[ann] (copy) at (7.5,2.9) {copy constraint: two cells of $E$
    in one cycle of $\sigma$ hold equal values};
  \draw[lead, tierrecv] (copy.west) -- (3.4,2.9);
  % lookup from an advice cell into the table column
  \draw[thick, tierspend, -{Latex[length=1.8mm]}]
    (A1r5.east) .. controls (5.2,5.7) .. (Tr5.south west);
  \node[ann] (look) at (7.5,5.3) {lookup: the value of $A_1$\\at this

  \draw[lead, tierspend] (look.west) -- (7.05,5.3);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The PLONKish grid of Definition~\ref{def:plonkish}: columns by
kind (fixed $q_0, q_1$; advice $A_0, A_1, A_2$; instance $I$; a fixed
table column $S$), rows indexed by the domain $H$. The outlined gate at
row $\omega^1$ reads its columns at rotations $0$ and $+1$; the green

two advice cells; the red arrow is a lookup asserting that an advice

\label{fig:plonkish-grid}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Columns as committed polynomials.}
With the Lagrange basis $\ell_j(X)$ of $H$, defined by
$\ell_j(\omega^j) = 1$ and $\ell_j(\omega^{j'}) = 0$ for $j' \ne j$
(\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The Lagrange basis on $H$''), a column with cells
$v_0, \dots, v_{n-1}$ is the polynomial
$v(X) = \sum_j v_j\,\ell_j(X)$ of degree $< n$, exactly as the toy's
columns were interpolated in \S\ref{sec:arith-polys}. The prover commits
to each advice polynomial, and the fixed polynomials are committed once
for all proofs, with the inner-product polynomial commitment of
\S\ref{sec:ipa}. Every argument that follows --- gates, permutation,
lookup, vanishing --- is a polynomial identity on these committed
polynomials, verified by querying them at random points exactly as
\S\ref{sec:arith-random} queried $G$ and $t$.

\subsection{Enforcing the wiring: the permutation argument}
\label{sec:arith-perm}

The copy constraints of \S\ref{sec:arith-grid} remain to be enforced.
The gate identity of \S\S\ref{sec:arith-polys}--\ref{sec:arith-random}
checks each row separately; it does not assert that $a_0, b_0, b_1, b_2$
are the same value $x$, or that row~$1$'s input is row~$0$'s output, and
a prover could satisfy every gate with rows that do not form one
computation. The \emph{permutation argument} reduces the equalities to
the equality of two products, which is checked at random challenges.

\paragraph{Equalities as a permutation.}
Group the cells into the classes that must share a value,
$\{a_0, b_0, b_1, b_2\}$, $\{c_0, a_1\}$, $\{c_1, a_2\}$, $\{c_2, a_3\}$,
and let $\sigma$ be the permutation of the twelve cells that cycles each
class in the order written and fixes $b_3$ and $c_3$:
% toy_trace.py, block "permutation argument":
% sigma: a_0 -> b_0, b_0 -> b_1, c_0 -> a_1, a_1 -> c_0, b_1 -> b_2, c_1 -> a_2, a_2 -> c_1, b_2 -> a_0, c_2 -> a_3, a_3 -> c_2
\[
a_0 \to b_0 \to b_1 \to b_2 \to a_0, \qquad
c_0 \leftrightarrow a_1, \qquad c_1 \leftrightarrow a_2, \qquad
c_2 \leftrightarrow a_3 .
\]
Give every cell a distinct \emph{label}: the cell in row $j$ of the $a$-,
$b$-, $c$-column gets $\omega^j$, $k_1\omega^j$, $k_2\omega^j$
respectively, where $k_1, k_2$ place the three columns in disjoint cosets
of $H$ so that no two labels collide. Two cosets of a subgroup are equal
or disjoint (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Cosets and Lagrange's theorem''), so
it suffices that $k_1$, $k_2$ and $k_2/k_1$ all lie outside $H$; in
$\F_{97}$ take $k_1 = 2$, $k_2 = 3$:
% labels: a_j -> omega^j, b_j -> 2 omega^j, c_j -> 3 omega^j; cosets H = [1, 22, 96, 75], 2H = [2, 44, 95, 53], 3H = [3, 66, 94, 31]
\[
H = \{1, 22, 96, 75\}, \qquad 2H = \{2, 44, 95, 53\}, \qquad
3H = \{3, 66, 94, 31\}.
\]
Write $\lambda_{\mathrm{cell}}$ for a cell's label and $v_{\mathrm{cell}}$
for its value. Asserting ``cells in a class are equal'' is then
equivalent to asserting that the \emph{multiset} (a finite collection in
which an element may occur more than once; two multisets are equal when
every element occurs equally often in both) of pairs
$\{(v_{\mathrm{cell}}, \lambda_{\mathrm{cell}})\}$ is unchanged when each
label is replaced by the label of the cell's image under $\sigma$: a pair
$(v, \lambda)$ moves to $(v, \lambda_{\sigma})$, and the moved pair was already
present exactly when the cell at $\lambda_\sigma$ carried the same value $v$.
Figure~\ref{fig:perm-cycles} draws the cycles with their labels. Because
labels are distinct, comparing two multisets of pairs is a job for a
\emph{product}: compress each pair to the linear form
$v + \beta\,\lambda + \gamma$ with verifier challenges $\beta, \gamma$, and
compare
\begin{equation}\label{eq:arith-perm-product}
\prod_{\text{cells}} \bigl(v_{\mathrm{cell}} + \beta\,\lambda_{\mathrm{cell}}
  + \gamma\bigr)
\quad\text{against}\quad
\prod_{\text{cells}} \bigl(v_{\mathrm{cell}}
  + \beta\,\lambda_{\sigma(\mathrm{cell})} + \gamma\bigr).
\end{equation}
If the wiring is honest the two products run over the same multiset of
factors and agree identically in $\beta, \gamma$. If a copy constraint is
false the difference is a nonzero polynomial in $\beta, \gamma$, which can
still vanish at one particular challenge pair, but only rarely; the
converse is probabilistic, not an ``if and only if'' for fixed challenges.
Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-perm} below makes both halves precise.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering

\caption{Left: the four copy classes of the toy as cycles of $\sigma$ on
the grid, every cell showing its value and its label $\lambda$ (column $a$
in $H$, column $b$ in $2H$, column $c$ in $3H$); the arrow closing the
$x$-class cycle, from $b_2$ back to $a_0$, is omitted. Right: the $x$-class
with $b_1$ tampered to $4$; the two arrows leaving and entering the

\paragraph{The running product.}
A product over all cells is not a local fact, so the prover commits to a
\emph{running product}: a column $Z_{\mathrm{perm}}$ with
$Z_{\mathrm{perm}}(\omega^0) = 1$ whose row $j + 1$ equals row $j$ times
the ratio of row $j$'s factors in~\eqref{eq:arith-perm-product},
numerator over denominator. After the last row the product has run over
every cell, and because $\omega^n = \omega^0$ the row-to-row rule at the
last row forces the full product to equal $Z_{\mathrm{perm}}(\omega^0) = 1$
again: the grand product is $1$ exactly when the running product closes
its loop. The deployed circuit imposes the rule cross-multiplied,
``next row times denominator equals this row times numerator'', not as a
field division, so a zero factor never makes the constraint undefined;
the prover commits to $Z_{\mathrm{perm}}$ and the verifier checks the
boundary value and the row-to-row update at the random point, exactly as
he checked the gate identity (the halo2 book's \emph{Permutation argument}
chapter). The identities are written out in
Construction~\ref{con:arith-perm}.

\paragraph{A single wired pair.}
Strip the product down to a single wired pair, $c_0 = a_1$ (both should
be $9$), with labels $\lambda_{c_0} = 3$ and $\lambda_{a_1} = 22$ that $\sigma$
swaps. Their factors satisfy
\[
\bigl(9 + \beta\,\lambda_{c_0} + \gamma\bigr)\bigl(9 + \beta\,\lambda_{a_1}
  + \gamma\bigr)
=
\bigl(9 + \beta\,\lambda_{a_1} + \gamma\bigr)\bigl(9 + \beta\,\lambda_{c_0}
  + \gamma\bigr),
\]
because the shared value $9$ makes the two left-hand factors a mere
reordering of the two right-hand ones: the labels may permute under
$\sigma$ precisely because the values agree. Now let the prover cheat the
copy, $a_1 = 8 \ne 9$. The factor $8 + \beta\,\lambda_{a_1} + \gamma$ on the
left is matched on the right only by $8 + \beta\,\lambda_{c_0} + \gamma$,
which differs since the labels differ; the two products differ as
polynomials in $\beta, \gamma$. A single unequal value thus leaves a
factor without a partner, and the two products differ.

\begin{example}[The permutation check on the toy]\label{ex:arith-perm-toy}
% grand product prod (v + beta label + gamma) / (v + beta sigma-label + gamma) at beta = gamma = 2: honest = 1; with b_1 = 4: 61
For the honest trace with $\beta = \gamma = 2$, the ratio of the two
products in~\eqref{eq:arith-perm-product} over all twelve cells is
exactly $1$. Tamper one wire, setting $b_1 = 4$ and so breaking the copy
$b_1 = x = 3$, and the same ratio at the same challenges becomes
$61 \ne 1$: the inconsistent dataflow is exposed without any value being
revealed.
\end{example}

\paragraph{The general argument.}
Halo~2 states the argument for any number of columns, and the statement
needs one lemma on two-variable polynomials. The Schwartz--Zippel lemma
of the \emph{Math Guide} (\S``The Schwartz--Zippel lemma'') is
univariate; that section's remark on the multivariate generalisation
states the several-variable bound without proof, as a consequence of the
univariate lemma applied once per variable, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:arith-bivariate} carries out that derivation for two
variables.

\begin{lemma}[Two variables]\label{lem:arith-bivariate}
Let $P \in \F[\beta, \gamma]$ be nonzero, of degree at most $N$ in each
variable separately. For $\beta, \gamma$ drawn independently and
uniformly from $\F$, $\Pr[P(\beta, \gamma) = 0] \le 2N / |\F|$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $P = \sum_{e} c_e(\beta)\,\gamma^{e}$ with coefficients
$c_e \in \F[\beta]$ of degree at most $N$. Since $P \ne 0$, some
$c_{e_0} \ne 0$, and $c_{e_0}(\beta) = 0$ with probability at most
$N / |\F|$ (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The Schwartz--Zippel lemma''). When
$c_{e_0}(\beta) \ne 0$, the polynomial $P(\beta, \cdot) \in \F[\gamma]$
is nonzero of degree at most $N$, so it vanishes at the independent
uniform $\gamma$ with probability at most $N / |\F|$. The union bound
(\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The union bound and a birthday calculation'')
adds the two.
\end{proof}

\begin{construction}[Permutation argument over $m$ columns]
\label{con:arith-perm}
Let $v_0(X), \dots, v_{m-1}(X)$ be the interpolants of the $m$ columns
that participate in equality constraints, and let $N = mn$ be the number
of participating cells. Label the cell $(i, j)$ (column $i$, row $j$) by
$\delta_i\,\omega^j$, where $\delta_0, \dots, \delta_{m-1} \in \F^\times$
are chosen so that the cosets $\delta_i H$ are pairwise disjoint and all
$N$ labels are distinct; the deployed choice is $\delta_i = \delta^i$ for
an element $\delta$ whose multiplicative order is the odd part of
$|\F^\times|$ (the halo2 book's \emph{Permutation argument} chapter).
Preprocessing fixes the \emph{permuted-label polynomials} $s_i(X)$ with
$s_i(\omega^j) = \delta_{i'}\,\omega^{j'}$ whenever
$\sigma(i, j) = (i', j')$; they are preprocessed like the fixed columns
but are not circuit columns, and the verifying key --- the verifier's
preprocessed description of the circuit, holding commitments to every
fixed column and every permuted-label polynomial --- contains their
commitments.
After the columns are committed the verifier sends challenges
$\beta, \gamma \in \F$, and the prover commits to a running-product
polynomial $Z_{\mathrm{perm}}(X)$ and proves the two identities, both
modulo $\ZH(X)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:arith-perm-rules}
\begin{aligned}
\ell_0(X)\,\bigl(1 - Z_{\mathrm{perm}}(X)\bigr) &\equiv 0, \\
Z_{\mathrm{perm}}(\omega X) \prod_{i=0}^{m-1}
  \bigl(v_i(X) + \beta\,s_i(X) + \gamma\bigr)
- Z_{\mathrm{perm}}(X) \prod_{i=0}^{m-1}
  \bigl(v_i(X) + \beta\,\delta_i X + \gamma\bigr) &\equiv 0,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
which together assert
\begin{equation}\label{eq:arith-perm-grand}
\prod_{i, j} \bigl(v_i(\omega^j) + \beta\,\delta_i\,\omega^j + \gamma\bigr)
=
\prod_{i, j} \bigl(v_i(\omega^j) + \beta\,s_i(\omega^j) + \gamma\bigr).
\end{equation}
The verifier opens $Z_{\mathrm{perm}}$ at the random point $z$ and at
$\omega z$, and checks the boundary and the cross-multiplied update.
\end{construction}

The first identity fixes $Z_{\mathrm{perm}}(\omega^0) = 1$; the second,
holding at every row $j$, gives
$Z_{\mathrm{perm}}(\omega^{j+1}) = Z_{\mathrm{perm}}(\omega^j)\cdot
 \prod_i (v_i + \beta\delta_i\omega^j + \gamma) /
 \prod_i (v_i + \beta s_i(\omega^j) + \gamma)$ whenever the denominator
is nonzero, and at $j = n - 1$ it returns to row $\omega^0$, whence
\eqref{eq:arith-perm-grand}. The toy is the case $m = 3$ with
$(\delta_0, \delta_1, \delta_2) = (1, 2, 3)$, which is not of the deployed
form $\delta^i$.

\begin{theorem}[Soundness of the permutation argument]
\label{thm:arith-perm}
The assignment satisfies every equality constraint (cells related by
$\sigma$ carry equal values) if and only if the two sides
of~\eqref{eq:arith-perm-grand} are equal as polynomials in $\beta$ and
$\gamma$. Consequently the honest prover always satisfies
\eqref{eq:arith-perm-grand}, and if some constraint is violated the two
sides agree at uniformly random $\beta, \gamma$ with probability at most
$2N / |\F|$, where $N$ is the number of participating cells.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Index cells by $\kappa$, write $v_\kappa$ and $\lambda_\kappa$ for value and
label, and set
\[
P(\beta, \gamma) := \prod_{\kappa} \bigl(\gamma + v_\kappa
  + \beta\,\lambda_\kappa\bigr)
 - \prod_{\kappa} \bigl(\gamma + v_\kappa
   + \beta\,\lambda_{\sigma(\kappa)}\bigr)
\in \F[\beta][\gamma].
\]
Each side is a product of monic linear polynomials in $\gamma$ over the
ring $\F[\beta]$, with roots $r_\kappa := -(v_\kappa + \beta\,\lambda_\kappa)$
and $r'_\kappa := -(v_\kappa + \beta\,\lambda_{\sigma(\kappa)})$; two such
roots are equal in $\F[\beta]$ exactly when their value--label pairs
coincide, since a polynomial in $\beta$ is determined by its coefficients.

If every constraint holds then $v_{\sigma(\kappa)} = v_\kappa$ for all
$\kappa$, so
$r'_\kappa = -(v_{\sigma(\kappa)} + \beta\,\lambda_{\sigma(\kappa)})
= r_{\sigma(\kappa)}$, and the second product is the first with its
factors reindexed by the bijection $\sigma$: $P = 0$.

If some constraint fails, pick $\kappa_0$ with
$v_{\sigma(\kappa_0)} \ne v_{\kappa_0}$. The pair
$(v_{\kappa_0}, \lambda_{\sigma(\kappa_0)})$ occurs among the roots $r'$; among
the roots $r$ the label $\lambda_{\sigma(\kappa_0)}$ occurs exactly once,
paired with $v_{\sigma(\kappa_0)} \ne v_{\kappa_0}$, so the multisets of
roots differ. Cancel every common factor from both products; a factor
$(\gamma - r)$ then remains on one side, say the first, and $r$ is not a
root of the other side. Substitute $\gamma := r$, a ring homomorphism
$\F[\beta][\gamma] \to \F[\beta]$ (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Roots and the factor
theorem'', evaluation is a ring homomorphism; its proof holds verbatim over the
coefficient ring $\F[\beta]$): the first product becomes $0$ and the second
becomes a product of nonzero elements of $\F[\beta]$, which is nonzero because
$\F[\beta]$ is an integral domain (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Degree'',
Corollary ``$F[X]$ is an integral domain''). Hence $P \ne 0$. Its degree in each
variable is at most $N$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:arith-bivariate} bounds the
probability that the random challenges hit a zero of $P$ by $2N / |\F|$.
\end{proof}

The honest prover's ratio is $1$ whenever the denominator is nonzero.
The deployed boundary rule of Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows} admits the
final value $0$, and the error bound changes accordingly.

\begin{corollary}[Permutation argument under the deployed boundary rule]
\label{cor:arith-perm-deployed}
Let the columns be fixed before $\beta, \gamma$, drawn uniformly, and let
the running products be constrained on the active rows
$\omega^0, \dots, \omega^{u-1}$ only, with $Z_{\mathrm{perm}}(\omega^0) = 1$
and the value at the next row $\omega^u$ permitted to be $0$ or $1$ (the
deployed rule; Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows} fixes $u < n$ and gives the
identities). If some copy
constraint fails, the probability over $\beta, \gamma$ that some running
products satisfy the update and boundary identities on the active rows
is at most $3N/|\F|$, with $N$ the number of participating cells.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Suppose some running products satisfy the identities and no numerator
factor $v_\kappa + \beta\,\lambda_\kappa + \gamma$ vanishes. Then
induction from $Z_{\mathrm{perm}}(\omega^0) = 1$ keeps
every running value nonzero, so the boundary value is $1$, the updates
telescope, and the two products over the active rows are equal; by
Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-perm} that happens with probability at most
$2N/|\F|$. For each $\beta$ at most $N$ values of $\gamma$ make a
numerator factor vanish, which adds $N/|\F|$, and the union bound gives
$2N/|\F| + N/|\F| = 3N/|\F|$.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Cost.}
Excluding the final commitment check, the verifier's work for the
argument is $O(m + \log n)$: evaluating the two identities
\eqref{eq:arith-perm-rules} at the random point $z$ needs the $2m$
linear factors, and $\ell_0(z) = (z^n - 1) / (n\,(z - 1))$ costs
$O(\log n)$ multiplications by the closed form of the Lagrange basis
(\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The Lagrange basis on $H$''). The complete
deployed verifier still carries one check linear in $n$, the
multi-scalar multiplication (MSM; \emph{Math Guide}, \S``The mixed
inner product with group elements'') that closes every opening; \S\ref{sec:ipa}
develops it under ``The verifier's work: structured scalars and the final
generator''.

\paragraph{The deployed chunking.}
The update identity in~\eqref{eq:arith-perm-rules} has degree $m + 1$ in
the committed polynomials, and the active-row factor of
Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows}, which masks the rows reserved for blinding,
adds one more; so a single product accommodates at most
$m \le d_{\max} - 2$ columns. Columns beyond that are split into chunks
of at most $d_{\max} - 2$, each with its own running product, and a
boundary identity $\ell_0(X)\,(Z_{\mathrm{perm},a}(X)
 - Z_{\mathrm{perm},a-1}(\omega^{u} X)) \equiv 0$ copies the value at the
boundary row $\omega^u$, the first row after the active rows
$\omega^0, \dots, \omega^{u-1}$, from one product to the start of the
next (the
halo2 book's \emph{Permutation argument} chapter). The column layout is
implementation-specific: the protocol specification fixes the Action
statement, not its circuit.
% protocol_params.py: equality columns: 10 advice + 1 instance + 4 fixed = 15
% protocol_params.py: permutation: 15 equality columns in chunks of D - 2 = 7: 3 running products (chunk sizes [7, 7, 1])
The deployed Action circuit, whose implementation is \texttt{orchard},
has fifteen equality-enabled columns (ten advice, one instance, four fixed) and
maximum constraint degree $d_{\max} = 9$, and therefore uses three
running products of $7$, $7$ and $1$ columns, not one.

\subsection{Proving a value sits in a table: lookups}
\label{sec:arith-lookups}

Some constraints admit no low-degree gate on the constrained cells alone.
``This cell is a ten-bit number'' or ``this chunk indexes an entry of the
Sinsemilla generator table'' (Sinsemilla is the hash of the \emph{Crypto
Guide}, \S``Sinsemilla: an algebraic hash-based commitment'') has no
low-degree formula in the cell itself: written as one polynomial gate,
``$v \in \{0, \dots, 2^{10}-1\}$'' is $\prod_{i=0}^{2^{10}-1} (v - i) = 0$,
a gate of degree $2^{10}$, and a table of arbitrary entries does no
better. A decomposition into ten boolean-constrained bits keeps the degree
at $2$ but spends ten further cells on every such value. The single gate
would exceed the degree bound of \S\ref{sec:arith-random}: in the toy
field its degree $2^{10}$ exceeds $|\F| = 97$, so the bound
$\deg D/|\F|$ is vacuous, and at any field size it multiplies the degree
of the quotient the prover must split, commit and open
(\S\ref{sec:arith-vanish}). The Action
circuit uses range and table constraints throughout.

Membership of every entry of a column $A$ in a table column $S$ is, like
a copy constraint, a statement about \emph{multisets}, but it is not a
permutation: $A$ may repeat one table entry and omit others. The prover
therefore commits to $A'$, a rearrangement of $A$ in which equal values
occupy contiguous \emph{runs}, and to $S'$, a rearrangement of $S$ in
which the first row of each run of $A'$ holds the matching table value.
Two \emph{row-local} conditions then give membership: each row of $A'$
either begins a run, $A'_i = S'_i$, so that its value occurs in $S$, or
continues one, $A'_i = A'_{i-1}$. The rearrangements are certified by the
running product of \S\ref{sec:arith-perm} without labels: labelled
factors fix one wiring $\sigma$, whereas here the rearrangements are the
prover's choice, so a single running product with unlabelled factors, row
by row $(A'_i + \beta)(S'_i + \gamma)$ against
$(A_i + \beta)(S_i + \gamma)$, certifies that each primed column is a
rearrangement of its original. Induction on the row index then gives
membership for every row.

\begin{example}[A two-bit range check on the toy grid]
\label{ex:arith-lookup-toy}
% toy_trace.py, block "lookup":
% table S = [0, 1, 2, 3], column A = [2, 3, 2, 2]; grouped A' = [2, 2, 2, 3]; aligned S' = [2, 0, 1, 3]
% row checks (A'_0 = S'_0; (A'_i - S'_i)(A'_i - A'_(i-1)) = 0): [True, True, True, True]
% multiset check at (beta, gamma) = (5, 7): prod (A' + beta)(S' + gamma) = 82 = prod (A + beta)(S + gamma) = 82
% forged A = [2, 7, 2, 2]: over all 4 arrangements A' and all 24 reshuffles S', row checks passing: 0 (the 7 starts a run and no S' entry equals 7)
Constrain cells to two-bit values with the table $S = (0, 1, 2, 3)$ and
the column $A = (2, 3, 2, 2)$. Grouped, $A' = (2, 2, 2, 3)$; align
$S' = (2, 0, 1, 3)$, a rearrangement of $S$ placing $2$ beside the first run
and $3$ beside the second. Row~$0$ starts a run ($2 = 2$), rows $1$
and~$2$ continue it ($2$ above), row~$3$ starts a run ($3 = 3$): every
check is row-local and every polynomial low-degree, however large the
table (Figure~\ref{fig:lookup-runs}). The unlabelled product certifies the
rearrangements: at the challenges $(\beta, \gamma) = (5, 7)$ both
$\prod_i (A'_i + \beta)(S'_i + \gamma)$ and
$\prod_i (A_i + \beta)(S_i + \gamma)$ equal $82$. The forged column
$A = (2, 7, 2, 2)$ fails the row checks: its $7$ must start a run in any
arrangement $A'$, and no rearrangement of a table without a $7$ can put a
$7$ beside it.
Over all $4$ arrangements of the forged column and all $24$
rearrangements of the table, the row checks pass for none.
\end{example}

\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  cell/.style={draw, fill=white, minimum width=0.85cm, minimum height=0.6cm,
    inner sep=2pt, font=\small},
  x=1.0cm, y=-0.85cm]
  \begin{scope}
  \node[font=\small\itshape] at (0,-0.75) {$A'$};
  \node[font=\small\itshape] at (1,-0.75) {$S'$};

    \node[font=\small] at (-0.9,\j) {row $\j$};
    \node[cell] (A\j) at (0,\j) {$\va$};
    \node[cell] (S\j) at (1,\j) {$\vs$};
  }
  \draw[tierrecv, line width=1pt] (A0.east) -- (S0.west);
  \draw[tierrecv, line width=1pt] (A3.east) -- (S3.west);
  \draw[tierin, line width=1pt, -{Latex[length=1.8mm]}]
    (A1.north west) ++(0.12cm,0) -- ++(0,0.3cm);
  \draw[tierin, line width=1pt, -{Latex[length=1.8mm]}]
    (A2.north west) ++(0.12cm,0) -- ++(0,0.3cm);
  \draw[thick, black!60] (-1.55,-0.3) -- (-1.55,2.3)
    node[midway, left=4pt, font=\scriptsize, align=flush center] {run\\of $2$};
  \draw[thick, black!60] (-1.55,2.7) -- (-1.55,3.3)
    node[midway, left=4pt, font=\scriptsize, align=flush center] {run\\of $3$};
  \node[anchor=west, font=\small, tierrecv] at (1.55,0)
    {start: $A'_0 = S'_0$};
  \node[anchor=west, font=\small, tierin] at (1.55,1)
    {continue: $A'_1 = A'_0$};
  \node[anchor=west, font=\small, tierin] at (1.55,2)
    {continue: $A'_2 = A'_1$};
  \node[anchor=west, font=\small, tierrecv] at (1.55,3)
    {start: $A'_3 = S'_3$};
  \node[font=\small\itshape, anchor=north] at (1.6,3.6)
    {$A = (2, 3, 2, 2)$, honest};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[xshift=6.9cm]
  \node[font=\small\itshape] at (0,-0.75) {$A'$};
  \node[font=\small\itshape] at (1,-0.75) {$S'$};
  \foreach \j/\va/\vs in {0/2/2, 1/2/0, 2/2/1} {
    \node[font=\small] at (-0.9,\j) {row $\j$};
    \node[cell] (FA\j) at (0,\j) {$\va$};
    \node[cell] (FS\j) at (1,\j) {$\vs$};
  }
  \node[font=\small] at (-0.9,3) {row $3$};
  \node[cell, draw=tierspend, line width=1pt] (FA3) at (0,3)
    {$\color{tierspend}\mathbf{7}$};
  \node[cell] (FS3) at (1,3) {$3$};
  \draw[tierrecv, line width=1pt] (FA0.east) -- (FS0.west);
  \draw[tierin, line width=1pt, -{Latex[length=1.8mm]}]
    (FA1.north west) ++(0.12cm,0) -- ++(0,0.3cm);
  \draw[tierin, line width=1pt, -{Latex[length=1.8mm]}]
    (FA2.north west) ++(0.12cm,0) -- ++(0,0.3cm);
  \draw[tierspend, line width=1pt, dashed] (FA3.east) -- (FS3.west);
  \node[anchor=west, font=\small, tierspend, text width=2.8cm,
    align=flush left]
    at (1.55,3) {$7 \ne 3$ and $7 \ne 2$: stranded};
  \node[font=\small\itshape, anchor=north, text width=4.6cm, align=flush center]

\caption{The lookup of Example~\ref{ex:arith-lookup-toy}. Left: the
grouped column $A'$ beside the aligned table $S'$; a run starts where
$A'_i = S'_i$ (green bar) and continues where $A'_i = A'_{i-1}$ (blue
arrow to the row above). Right: the forged column with a $7$; the $7$
starts a run, but no entry of $S'$ equals $7$ and the row above holds
