\label{fig:lookup-runs}
\end{figure}

The Action circuit uses lookups of both kinds: it range-constrains each
ten-bit word of a decomposition by a lookup into the table
$\{0, \dots, 2^{10}-1\}$ (the halo2 book's \emph{Decomposition}
chapter), and it evaluates Sinsemilla by a lookup into a fixed table
whose $2^{10}$ rows hold an index $i$ and the two coordinates of the
$i$-th Sinsemilla generator (the halo2 book's \emph{Sinsemilla} chapter;
the Orchard book's \emph{Circuit} chapter records the deployed uses of
both). A table fact costs a few committed columns and one more running
product, and the gates stay low-degree. The form of the Action circuit's
gates, one gate in full, and its three lookups are given in
\S\ref{sec:halo2} under ``From statement to circuit: arithmetisation in
practice''; the exact identities, the boundary row, and multi-column tables
come next.

\subsection{The lookup argument}
\label{sec:arith-lookup-arg}

\begin{construction}[Lookup argument]\label{con:arith-lookup}
The objective is that every entry of the column $A(X)$ on the active rows
appears as some entry of the column $S(X)$ (the halo2 book's
\emph{Lookup argument} chapter). The prover supplies
polynomials $A'(X)$ and $S'(X)$ where (i) $A'$ is a permutation of $A$
with like values grouped into contiguous runs, and (ii) $S'$ is a
permutation of $S$ in which the first row of each run of like values in
$A'$ holds the matching value. The prover commits to $A'$ and $S'$;
after receiving challenges $\beta, \gamma$ she commits to a
running-product column $Z_{\mathrm{lookup}}(X)$ and proves the four
identities, all modulo $\ZH(X)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:arith-lookup-rules}
\begin{aligned}
\ell_0(X)\,\bigl(1 - Z_{\mathrm{lookup}}(X)\bigr) &\equiv 0, \\
Z_{\mathrm{lookup}}(\omega X)\,\bigl(A'(X) + \beta\bigr)\bigl(S'(X)
  + \gamma\bigr)
 - Z_{\mathrm{lookup}}(X)\,\bigl(A(X) + \beta\bigr)\bigl(S(X)
  + \gamma\bigr) &\equiv 0, \\
\ell_0(X)\,\bigl(A'(X) - S'(X)\bigr) &\equiv 0, \\
\bigl(A'(X) - S'(X)\bigr)\bigl(A'(X) - A'(\omega^{-1} X)\bigr) &\equiv 0,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
the second and fourth additionally masked by the active-row factor of
Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows}.
\end{construction}

\begin{theorem}[Soundness of the lookup argument]\label{thm:arith-lookup}
Let the columns $A, S, A', S'$ be fixed before $\beta, \gamma$ are
drawn. If the four identities~\eqref{eq:arith-lookup-rules} hold on the
active rows, with the boundary conditions of Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows},
then except with probability at most $4n / |\F|$ over the challenges
$\beta, \gamma$, every value of $A$ on the active rows equals the value
of $S$ at some active row.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let the active rows be $0, \dots, u - 1$ (Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows}),
and write $A_i := A(\omega^i)$, likewise for $S, A', S'$, and
$Z_i := Z_{\mathrm{lookup}}(\omega^i)$. Suppose first that no numerator
factor $A_i + \beta$ or $S_i + \gamma$ with $i < u$ vanishes. The
columns are fixed before the challenges, so each of the $u$ values $-A_i$
equals $\beta$ with probability $1/|\F|$, and likewise for $\gamma$; by
the union bound the supposition fails with probability at most
$2u/|\F|$. Under it, the second identity at row $i < u$ reads
$Z_{i+1}\,(A'_i + \beta)(S'_i + \gamma) = Z_i\,(A_i + \beta)(S_i + \gamma)$,
and induction from $Z_0 = 1$ gives $Z_{i+1} \ne 0$ and
$(A'_i + \beta)(S'_i + \gamma) \ne 0$ for every $i < u$. Hence
$Z_u \ne 0$, the boundary condition forces $Z_u = 1$, and multiplying the
identities over $i < u$ and cancelling the nonzero $Z_i$ gives
\[
\prod_{i < u} \bigl(A'_i + \beta\bigr)\bigl(S'_i + \gamma\bigr)
= \prod_{i < u} \bigl(A_i + \beta\bigr)\bigl(S_i + \gamma\bigr).
\]
Set $f(\beta) := \prod_i (A'_i + \beta)$, $g(\beta) := \prod_i (A_i + \beta)$,
$f'(\gamma) := \prod_i (S'_i + \gamma)$, $g'(\gamma) := \prod_i (S_i + \gamma)$
and $P := f f' - g g' \in \F[\beta, \gamma]$. If $A'$ is a permutation of
$A$ and $S'$ of $S$ then $f = g$ and $f' = g'$, so $P = 0$. If instead,
say, $A'$ is not a permutation of $A$, then the multisets of roots
$\{-A'_i\}$ and $\{-A_i\}$ differ, so $f \ne g$ by the cancel-and-evaluate
argument in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-perm} (now over the field
$\F$ itself); comparing the coefficients of the top power of $\gamma$ in
$f f' = g g'$, both $f'$ and $g'$ being monic of the same degree, would
force $f = g$, so $P \ne 0$, of degree at most $u$ in each variable, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:arith-bivariate} bounds the probability that the
challenges hide the difference by $2u / |\F|$. The case of $S'$ is
symmetric. The two terms together are at most
$2u/|\F| + 2u/|\F| \le 4n/|\F|$.

So, with that probability excepted, $A'$ is a rearrangement of $A$ and $S'$ of
$S$ on the active rows. The third identity fixes row $0$: $A'_0 = S'_0$, a table
value. The fourth, at every later active row $i$, says $(A'_i - S'_i)(A'_i -
A'_{i-1}) = 0$: either $A'_i = S'_i$, a table value, or $A'_i = A'_{i-1}$. (At
row $0$ the reference $A'(\omega^{-1}X)$ wraps around to the last row and
carries no information, which is why the third identity is needed there.) By
induction on $i$, every $A'_i$ equals the entry of $S'$ at some active row,
hence that of $S$ at some active row; and since every entry of $A$ on the
active rows occurs in $A'$ there, the claim follows.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Multi-column tables.}
A lookup of $m$ input expressions $A_0, \dots, A_{m-1}$ against $m$ table
columns $S_0, \dots, S_{m-1}$ is reduced to a single column by a random
linear combination with a challenge $\theta$ drawn after the input and
table columns are committed:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:arith-theta}
A_{\mathrm{c}}(X) := \sum_{j=0}^{m-1} \theta^{\,m-1-j} A_j(X), \qquad
S_{\mathrm{c}}(X) := \sum_{j=0}^{m-1} \theta^{\,m-1-j} S_j(X),
\end{equation}
and Construction~\ref{con:arith-lookup} runs on $A_{\mathrm{c}}$ and
$S_{\mathrm{c}}$.

\begin{proposition}[Compression of a multi-column lookup]
\label{prop:arith-theta}
Let $\theta$ be drawn uniformly after all input and table columns are
fixed. If the input tuple at some active row equals the table tuple at no
active row, then the compressed input $A_{\mathrm{c}}$ at that row equals
$S_{\mathrm{c}}$ at some active row with probability at most
$n(m - 1) / |\F|$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For each active table row the difference of the compressed values is a
polynomial in $\theta$ of degree at most $m - 1$ whose coefficients are
the differences of the tuple entries, not all zero; it is therefore
nonzero, and the random $\theta$ zeroes it with probability at most
$(m - 1) / |\F|$ (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The Schwartz--Zippel lemma''). A
union bound over the at most $n$ active rows gives $n(m - 1) / |\F|$.
\end{proof}

Variable tables, where $S$ is itself an advice column, and range checks,
where $S$ enumerates the allowed range, are special cases of the same
argument.

\begin{remark}[Active rows and blinding]\label{rem:active-rows}
The lookup identities, like the permutation identities of
\S\ref{sec:arith-perm}, hold on the \emph{active} rows only. The deployed
system withholds the trailing rows of every column from them: a boundary
row and, after it, $n_{\mathrm{blind}}$ blinding rows, which in every
witness column (advice, and the permuted and running-product columns of
the arguments) hold uniform random values, for the reason ``Zero
knowledge: hiding the witness in Halo~2'' in \S\ref{sec:halo2} gives;
fixed and instance columns carry no random values. Let
$u := n - n_{\mathrm{blind}} - 1$. Rows $0, \dots, u - 1$ are the active
rows, row $u$ is the boundary row, and rows $u + 1, \dots, n - 1$ are the
blinding rows. (The letter $u$ indexes a row here; in \S\ref{sec:ipa} it
denotes a round challenge.) Let $\ell_{\mathrm{blind}}(X)$ be the sum of
the Lagrange basis polynomials of the $n_{\mathrm{blind}}$ blinding rows,
and let $\ell_{\mathrm{last}}(X) := \ell_u(X)$ select the boundary row.
Both are computed
directly from the domain by both parties; they are not committed circuit
columns. Each product-update identity, and the run identity of
\eqref{eq:arith-lookup-rules}, is multiplied by the factor
$\bigl(1 - (\ell_{\mathrm{last}}(X) + \ell_{\mathrm{blind}}(X))\bigr)$,
which is $1$ on the active rows and $0$ elsewhere, so the random rows do
not violate the active-row constraints. The wrap-around that closed the
loop in Construction~\ref{con:arith-perm} is gone, so the running product
is pinned at the boundary row instead, by
$\ell_{\mathrm{last}}(X)\,\bigl(Z(X)^2 - Z(X)\bigr) \equiv 0$, which
allows $Z(\omega^u) \in \{0, 1\}$ (the halo2 book's \emph{Lookup argument}
and \emph{Permutation argument} chapters). The value $0$ is permitted so
that a vanishing \emph{numerator} factor ($A_i + \beta$ or $S_i + \gamma$;
in the permutation argument $v_\kappa + \beta\,\lambda_\kappa + \gamma$)
still leaves the honest prover a satisfying assignment: she sets the
product to $0$ from the next row on. The prover cannot force this event,
because $\beta, \gamma$ are drawn after her columns are committed. A
\emph{denominator} factor that vanishes at an earlier row than every
vanishing numerator factor admits no satisfying running product: the
update at that row $j$ reads $0 = Z(\omega^j)$ times a nonzero numerator,
and $Z(\omega^j) \ne 0$. This happens
with probability at most $2u/|\F|$ for a lookup and $N/|\F|$ for the
permutation argument, so completeness is overwhelming, not perfect. A
boundary value of $0$ is not
a certificate of multiset equality, so soundness also excludes the
challenges at which a numerator factor vanishes: the bound of
Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-lookup} includes that term, and
Corollary~\ref{cor:arith-perm-deployed} adds it for the permutation
argument.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Why the prover cannot wriggle: binding commitments}
\label{sec:arith-commit}

The random-point argument of \S\ref{sec:arith-random} rests on a hypothesis:
Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-random} assumed the prover's polynomials were fixed
before she saw $z$. If she could choose $a, b, c, t$ \emph{after} learning
$z$, she would pick numbers making~\eqref{eq:arith-check} hold and
reveal nothing real. A polynomial commitment enforces that hypothesis.

\paragraph{What a commitment must do.}
A commitment to a polynomial $f$ is a short string $C = \Comm(f)$ with
two properties, both developed in the \emph{Crypto Guide},
\S``Hiding and binding'':
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Binding.} Having published $C$, the prover cannot later open
  it as two different polynomials; $C$ pins down $f$. Committing first,
  then receiving $z$, then opening, leaves her answering for the
  \emph{one} polynomial she fixed in advance, the situation
  Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-random} assumed.
\item \textbf{Hiding.} The string $C$ reveals nothing about $f$. This is
  what will let the proof be zero-knowledge, under ``Zero knowledge:
  hiding the witness in Halo~2'' in \S\ref{sec:halo2}.
\end{itemize}
A \emph{polynomial} commitment adds an evaluation proof: given $C$ and a
point $z$, the prover can prove ``$f(z) = v$'' for the committed $f$
without exposing $f$, and no prover can produce accepting proofs of two
different values at the same point (\emph{evaluation binding},
\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Polynomial commitment schemes'').
Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-random} concerns the idealised protocol, in which
the verifier receives the true evaluations of polynomials fixed in
advance. Compiled with a polynomial commitment scheme that is
evaluation-binding and extractable, the four-step protocol of
\S\ref{sec:arith-random} becomes, under compatible composition
hypotheses, an argument of knowledge (\emph{Crypto Guide},
\S``SNARKs: succinct non-interactive arguments of knowledge'', Theorem
``The compilation recipe, informal''): by its layered extraction, a
prover who passes either answers with the true evaluations of
polynomials fixed before $z$, where Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-random}
applies, or breaks the scheme's evaluation binding or extractability.
This volume asserts no bound on the compiled error as a sum of
$\deg D/|\F|$ and a commitment term (Remark~\ref{rem:halo2-compile},
\S\ref{sec:ks}). For Halo~2 the extractability of the openings is
Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} and, for the deployed opening,
Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks}, under the discrete-logarithm
assumption on Vesta, with the generator hash modelled as a random oracle.

\paragraph{How Halo~2 commits.}
Encode $f$ of degree $< n$ by its coefficient vector
$\mathbf f = (f_0, \dots, f_{n-1})$; the running example's advice
polynomial becomes $\mathbf a = (90, 61, 22, 24)$. Let $\G$ be a group
of prime order $|\F|$ (deployed: Vesta, of order $p_{\Pallas}$), and let
$G_0, \dots, G_{n-1}, H \in \G$ be generators derived from a public hash,
with $\mathbf G := (G_0, \dots, G_{n-1})$; here $H$ is a group element,
not the evaluation domain. The commitment is the single group element
\[
C = \ip{\mathbf f}{\mathbf G} + [r]H = \sum_{i} [f_i]\,G_i + [r]H,
\]
the Pedersen vector commitment of the coefficient vector with blinder
$r \in \F$ (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Pedersen vector commitments''; the
mixed inner product of a scalar vector with a vector of group elements is
the \emph{Math Guide}'s, \S``The mixed inner product with group
elements''). The commitment is perfectly hiding for uniform $r$ and
computationally binding under the discrete-logarithm assumption in $\G$,
with the hash modelled as a random oracle into $\G$
(\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Pedersen vector commitments'', Theorem
``Properties of the vector commitment''): two openings of one $C$ would
exhibit a nontrivial discrete-logarithm relation among the $G_i$ and
$H$. Proving $f(z) = v$ is proving a statement about an inner product,
since
\[
f(z) = \sum_{i} f_i\,z^i = \ip{\mathbf f}{(1, z, z^2, \dots, z^{n-1})},
\]
and the \emph{inner-product argument} does this in a logarithmic number
of rounds, each halving the vectors, until the claim is trivial: the
evaluation proof is $O(\log n)$ group elements, and it needs \emph{no trusted
setup}, because the generators come from a public hash rather than from anyone's
secret (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Polynomial commitment schemes'', the
inner-product alternative). Halo~2 uses exactly this one polynomial commitment
scheme and no other: the inner-product argument over a group with no pairing
(the halo2 book, which the protocol specification names as the definition of
the system,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#halo2}{\S~5.4.10.3},
``Halo 2''; its \emph{Inner product argument} chapter), on the Vesta curve,
where the protocol specification places the Action statement's proofs
(protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#pallasandvesta}{\S~5.4.9.6},
``Pallas and Vesta''). The parameters, the
generators and the deployed byte layout are given at the end of \S\ref{sec:ipa}.

\paragraph{Opening a vector commitment.}
For the argument of this section the commitment must bind, so that the
prover answers for polynomials fixed before $z$ is drawn. A vector
commitment alone does not provide step 3 of the random-point check. The
only opening the Pedersen construction offers is the whole vector
together with its blinder, $n + 1$ field elements: sending it reveals
$f$, and it is no shorter than $f$. The verifier needs
$\ip{\mathbf f}{(1, z, \dots, z^{n-1})} = v$ established for a committed
$\mathbf f$ he never sees, in fewer than $n$ group elements. That is the
inner-product argument of \S\ref{sec:ipa}.

% 03-ipa.tex -- sec:ipa. Every number in this section is read from
% compute/OUTPUT.md, blocks ipa_toy.py, ipa_extract.py, proof_bytes.py and
% orchard_params.py; the relevant lines are repeated as comments above each
% passage that uses them. The script labels the four toy generators G1..G4;
% the text indexes them G_0..G_3 so that the coefficient of X^i rides on G_i.

\section{The inner-product polynomial commitment}
\label{sec:ipa}

This section constructs the inner-product polynomial commitment that
opens the commitments of \S\ref{sec:arith-commit} at the verifier's
point. It states the problem, performs one halving step, recurses it into
the full argument with both parties' tables, gives the verifier's closing
computation, proves knowledge soundness under the discrete-logarithm
assumption, proves honest-verifier zero knowledge of the masked opening,
runs the running example's polynomial through every round on a curve of
prime order $97$, and in the last subsection gives the parameters, the
normalisation, the opening, the bytes and the deferred check of the
deployed Halo~2. Beyond the \emph{Math Guide}, the section relies on
these parts of the \emph{Crypto Guide}: the Pedersen vector commitment
(\S``Pedersen vector commitments''), extractors and the tree extraction
of Sigma-protocols (\S``Knowledge soundness and extractors'', \S``The
Schnorr identification protocol'', \S``Sigma-protocols''), the
simulation paradigm (\S``The simulation paradigm and zero knowledge''),
hashing to a curve (\S``Hashing to a curve point'') and the standing
assumptions (\S``Standing assumptions''). From this volume, the generic
argument takes only the cue of \S\ref{sec:arith-commit}; the toy reuses
the running example's advice polynomial and random point
(\S\ref{sec:arith-polys}, \S\ref{sec:arith-random}), restated where used;
the last subsection takes the deployed circuit's field from
\S\ref{sec:intro-claim} and the advice, permutation, lookup and quotient
objects from \S\ref{sec:arith}, and forward-references \S\ref{sec:halo2}
for the row count and the remaining objects of the proof; the other
references to \S\ref{sec:arith} mark where the argument is used.

\subsection{The problem: an inner product in fewer than
  \texorpdfstring{$d$}{d} group elements}
\label{sec:ipa-problem}

The prover holds a vector $\mathbf a = (a_0, \dots, a_{d-1})$ of $d$
field elements. She has published one group element,
\[
P = \ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf G} + [r]H
  = \sum_{i=0}^{d-1} [a_i]\,G_i + [r]H ,
\]
the Pedersen vector commitment of $\mathbf a$ with blinder $r$ under the
public generators $\mathbf G = (G_0, \dots, G_{d-1})$ and $H$
(\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Pedersen vector commitments''; the mixed inner
product $\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf G}$ of a scalar vector with a vector of
group elements is the \emph{Math Guide}'s, \S``The mixed inner product
with group elements''). The verifier holds $P$, a public vector
$\mathbf b = (b_0, \dots, b_{d-1})$ of his own, and a number $v$ the
prover claims. He wants to be sure that
\[
\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b} = \sum_{i=0}^{d-1} a_i\,b_i = v .
\]

There is a trivial protocol: the prover sends $\mathbf a$ and $r$; the
verifier recomputes $P$ from them, which the binding of the commitment
makes a meaningful check, and then computes $\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b}$
himself. It costs $d + 1$ field elements and reveals $\mathbf a$.
Neither is acceptable here: the vector is the prover's secret advice,
and the verifier of \S\ref{sec:arith-random} asks for evaluations rather
than polynomials because a polynomial is long, so a proof as long as the
vector gains nothing. The problem is therefore:

\begin{quote}
\emph{Convince the verifier that $\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b} = v$ for the
vector $\mathbf a$ committed in $P$, using fewer than $d$ group
elements, and without revealing $\mathbf a$.}
\end{quote}

\paragraph{Why this is polynomial evaluation.}
Let $p(X) = a_0 + a_1 X + \dots + a_{d-1} X^{d-1}$ be the polynomial
with coefficient vector $\mathbf a$, and let $x$ be a point. Then
\[
p(x) = \sum_{i=0}^{d-1} a_i\,x^i = \ip{\mathbf a}{(1, x, x^2, \dots,
  x^{d-1})} .
\]
Evaluating a committed polynomial is therefore \emph{exactly} the
problem above, with the public vector
$\mathbf b(x) := (1, x, x^2, \dots, x^{d-1})$ of powers of the point.
% ipa_toy.py, block "instance":
% a = [90, 61, 22, 24] (coefficients, low degree first); a on H = [3, 9, 27, 30]
% z = 20, b(z) = [1, 20, 12, 46], v = a(20) = <a, b> = 59
The running example makes it concrete. The advice polynomial of
\S\ref{sec:arith-polys} is $a(X) = 90 + 61X + 22X^2 + 24X^3$ over
$\F_{97}$, so $\mathbf a = (90, 61, 22, 24)$ and $d = 4$; the random
point of \S\ref{sec:arith-random} is $z = 20$, so
$\mathbf b(20) = (1, 20, 20^2, 20^3) = (1, 20, 12, 46)$ in $\F_{97}$;
and the value the verifier needs is
$a(20) = \ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b(20)} = 59$. Everything the random-point
check of \S\ref{sec:arith-random} asks of the prover, ``open $a$, $b$,
$c$ and $t$ at $z$'', is four instances of this one problem.

\paragraph{Where the difficulty sits.}
The commitment is linear in the vector: adding two commitments commits
to the sum of their vectors, and scaling one by $\lambda$ commits to
$\lambda\mathbf a$ (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Pedersen vector
commitments'', the remark that linearity survives). The inner product is
linear in $\mathbf a$ too. But the two linear maps land in different
places, one in the group and one in the field, and by the hiding of the
commitment no public operation takes $P$ to $\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b}$.
A claim about a vector of length one is checked by sending the scalar and
its blinder.
The argument that follows reduces the length by \emph{halving}. Each
halving step turns a claim about vectors of length $2^j$ into a claim of
the same shape about vectors of length $2^{j-1}$, at a price of two group
elements, and after $k = \log_2 d$ steps the claim is about a single
scalar. The argument uses exactly this linearity.

\paragraph{The relation.}
Throughout, $\G$ is a cyclic group of prime order $p$ and $\F = \F_p$ its scalar
field, so that $\G$ is a one-dimensional $\F$-vector space (\emph{Math
Guide}, \S``The mixed inner product with group elements'').

\begin{definition}[The opening relation]\label{def:ipa-rel}
Fix public generators $\mathbf G = (G_0, \dots, G_{d-1})$ and $H$ in
$\G \setminus \{\mathcal O\}$. To open the
committed polynomial $p$ at $x \in \F$ with claimed value $v$ is to
prove membership in
\begin{gather*}
\Rel_{\mathrm{IPA}} := \Bigl\{ \bigl((P, x, v);\, (\mathbf a, r)\bigr) :
  P = \ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf G} + [r]H,\;
  v = \ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b(x)},\;
  \mathbf a \in \F^{d} \Bigr\}, \\
\mathbf b(x) := (1, x, \dots, x^{d-1}),
\end{gather*}
where $(P, x, v)$ is the public statement and $(\mathbf a, r)$ the
witness. The condition $\mathbf a \in \F^d$ is the degree bound
$\deg p \le d - 1$: a commitment under $d$ generators can only hold $d$
coefficients.
\end{definition}

\paragraph{Notation for the section.}
The following conventions hold from here to the end of
\S\ref{sec:ipa}.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=1pt]
\item The vector length is $d = 2^k$, and $d$ is also the exclusive
  degree bound and the number of generators. The round count is
  $k = \log_2 d$. The letter $n$, the row count of
  Definition~\ref{def:plonkish}, is not used until the deployed
  parameters at the end of the section, where $d = n$; the constraint
  degree stays $d_{\max}$ as \S\ref{sec:arith-plonkish} fixed it. The
  \emph{Crypto Guide}'s sketch of this argument indexes its generators
  from $0$ to $d$ and bounds the degree by $d$ inclusively; nothing
  changes but the labels.
\item Generators are indexed from $0$, $\mathbf G = (G_0, \dots,
  G_{d-1})$, so that the coefficient $a_i$ of $X^i$ multiplies $G_i$; the
  \emph{Crypto Guide}'s ``Pedersen vector commitments'' indexes its
  generators $G_1, \dots, G_n$ instead, a shift of labels only.
\item The letter $H$ names the blinding generator $H \in \G$ of the
  Pedersen commitment; the evaluation domain $H \subset \F$ of
  \S\ref{sec:arith} does not appear in this section. The letter $p$
  names the group order, and with an argument, $p(X)$, the committed
  polynomial.
\item The letter $z$ is the running example's opening point, $z = 20$,
  and $x$ the generic one. The deployed protocol has a second challenge
  which its description also calls $z$; this volume writes it $\zeta$.
\item The structured scalars $s_i \in \F$ of \S\ref{sec:ipa-verifier}
  are scalars; the permuted-label polynomials $s_i(X)$ of
  Construction~\ref{con:arith-perm} are polynomials. Type and argument
  disambiguate. The mask $s(X)$ of \S\ref{sec:ipa-zk} is one further
  polynomial, with coefficient vector $\mathbf m$.
\item In the byte count of \S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}, $a$ is the number of
  Actions, an integer, not the vector $\mathbf a$.
\item A vector of length $2^j$ splits into its \emph{low half}, the
  first $2^{j-1}$ entries, and its \emph{high half}, the last
  $2^{j-1}$: $\mathbf a = (\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}, \mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}})$,
  and likewise for $\mathbf G$ and $\mathbf b$.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{One fold, every step written out}
\label{sec:ipa-fold}

One halving step is called a \emph{fold}. This subsection performs one
fold on a vector of length $d$.

\paragraph{Carry the claim inside the group element.}
The commitment $P$ says nothing about $\mathbf b$ or $v$. Bring them in:
after $P$ is fixed, the verifier samples a further generator $U$
uniformly from $\G \setminus \{\mathcal O\}$ and sends it. Both parties
form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ipa-C}
C := P + [v]\,U .
\end{equation}
If the prover is honest, $C = \ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf G} + [r]H +
[\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b}]\,U$: a single group element carrying three
kinds of information on three independent sets of generators, the
vector on $\mathbf G$, the blinder on $H$, and the inner product on $U$.
The claim ``$P$ commits to $\mathbf a$ and
$\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b} = v$'' has become the claim that $C$ has this
one \emph{shape}, for some $\mathbf a$ and $r$, with the $U$-coefficient
tied to the $\mathbf G$-coefficients by the inner product with the public
$\mathbf b$. The fold preserves exactly this shape while halving the
vectors.

\paragraph{Split into halves.}
Write $\mathbf a = (\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}, \mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}})$,
$\mathbf G = (\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}}, \mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}})$ and
$\mathbf b = (\mathbf b_{\mathrm{lo}}, \mathbf b_{\mathrm{hi}})$, each
half of length $d/2$. An inner product splits along the halves:
\[
\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf G}
 = \ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}}}
 + \ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}}},
\qquad
\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b}
 = \ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf b_{\mathrm{lo}}}
 + \ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf b_{\mathrm{hi}}} .
\]

\paragraph{The fold.}
Let $u \in \F^\times$ be a nonzero challenge, and define the
\emph{folded} vectors of length $d/2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ipa-fold-vectors}
\mathbf a' := u\,\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}} + u^{-1}\,\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}},
\qquad
\mathbf G' := u^{-1}\,\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}} + u\,\mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}},
\qquad
\mathbf b' := u^{-1}\,\mathbf b_{\mathrm{lo}} + u\,\mathbf b_{\mathrm{hi}},
\end{equation}
entry by entry: $a'_i = u\,a_i + u^{-1}\,a_{i + d/2}$,
$G'_i = [u^{-1}]G_i + [u]G_{i + d/2}$ and $b'_i = u^{-1} b_i + u\,b_{i +
d/2}$ for $0 \le i < d/2$. The coefficient vector is folded with the
weights $(u, u^{-1})$ and the generator and evaluation vectors with the
\emph{inverse} weights $(u^{-1}, u)$; this is the symmetric fold of the
Halo paper and of Bulletproofs, and the reason for the inverse weights
appears in the next computation.

\paragraph{The inner product of the folded vectors.}
Expand $\ip{\mathbf a'}{\mathbf G'}$ using bilinearity of the mixed
inner product (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The mixed inner product with group
elements''), one term per pair of halves:
\begin{align*}
\ip{\mathbf a'}{\mathbf G'}
 &= \ip{u\,\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}} + u^{-1}\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}
       {u^{-1}\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}} + u\,\mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}}} \\
 &= u\,u^{-1}\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}}}
  + u\,u\,\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}}}
  + u^{-1}u^{-1}\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}}}
  + u^{-1}u\,\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}}} \\
 &= \underbrace{\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}}}
  + \ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}}}}_{=\,
  \ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf G}}
  + u^{2}\,\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}}}
  + u^{-2}\,\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}}} .
\end{align*}
The two \emph{diagonal} terms, low with low and high with high, have
weight $u\,u^{-1} = 1$ and reassemble the original
$\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf G}$: that is what the inverse weights were for.
The two \emph{cross} terms, low with high and high with low, survive with
the weights $u^2$ and $u^{-2}$. The same expansion for the scalar inner
product, with $\mathbf b'$ folded by the same weights as $\mathbf G'$,
gives
\[
\ip{\mathbf a'}{\mathbf b'}
 = \ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b}
  + u^{2}\,\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf b_{\mathrm{hi}}}
  + u^{-2}\,\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf b_{\mathrm{lo}}} .
\]

\paragraph{The cross terms do not depend on the challenge.}
The four cross inner
products $\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}}}$,
$\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}}}$,
$\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf b_{\mathrm{hi}}}$ and
$\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf b_{\mathrm{lo}}}$ involve only the
halves, not $u$. The prover can therefore commit to them \emph{before}
the challenge is drawn. She samples two fresh blinders
$\ell, \rho \in \F$ and sends the two group elements
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ipa-LR}
L := \ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}}} + [\ell]H
   + [\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf b_{\mathrm{hi}}}]\,U,
\qquad
R := \ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}}} + [\rho]H
   + [\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf b_{\mathrm{lo}}}]\,U,
\end{equation}
each of the same three-part shape as $C$: a vector part on the
generators, a blinder on $H$, and the matching scalar inner product on
$U$. Only then does the verifier draw $u$.

\paragraph{The fold identity.}
Now add the cross terms to $C$ with the weights the expansion produced
and regroup by generator set:
\begin{align*}
C + [u^{2}]L + [u^{-2}]R
 &= \ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf G} + [r]H + [\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b}]\,U \\
 &\quad + u^{2}\Bigl(\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}}}
   + [\ell]H + [\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf b_{\mathrm{hi}}}]\,U
   \Bigr) \\
 &\quad + u^{-2}\Bigl(\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}}}
   + [\rho]H + [\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf b_{\mathrm{lo}}}]\,U
   \Bigr) \\
 &= \Bigl(\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf G}
   + u^{2}\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}}}
   + u^{-2}\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}}}\Bigr) \\
 &\quad + [\,r + u^{2}\ell + u^{-2}\rho\,]H
   + \Bigl[\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b}
   + u^{2}\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf b_{\mathrm{hi}}}
   + u^{-2}\ip{\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf b_{\mathrm{lo}}}\Bigr]\,U \\
 &= \ip{\mathbf a'}{\mathbf G'} + [r']H + [\ip{\mathbf a'}{\mathbf b'}]\,U,
\end{align*}
where $r' := r + u^{2}\ell + u^{-2}\rho$ and the last line substitutes
the two expansions. Written as one equation, the \emph{fold identity}
is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ipa-fold}
C' := C + [u^{2}]L + [u^{-2}]R
 = \ip{\mathbf a'}{\mathbf G'} + [r']H + [\ip{\mathbf a'}{\mathbf b'}]\,U .
\end{equation}

\paragraph{What has been achieved.}
Compare the two sides of~\eqref{eq:ipa-fold} with~\eqref{eq:ipa-C}. The
element $C'$ has the same shape as $C$, over vectors of half the length:
its $\mathbf G'$-part is the folded coefficient vector, its $H$-part a
blinder, and its $U$-coefficient the inner product of the folded vectors.
The verifier can compute $C'$, $\mathbf G'$ and $\mathbf b'$ entirely on
his own from $C$, $L$, $R$, $u$ and the public data. The prover computes
$\mathbf a'$ and $r'$. The claim about $(\mathbf a, \mathbf b, \mathbf G)$
of length $d$ has been replaced by a claim about
$(\mathbf a', \mathbf b', \mathbf G')$ of length $d/2$, and the exchange
cost exactly two group elements and one field element of challenge.
Figure~\ref{fig:ipa-fold} shows the fold and
identity~\eqref{eq:ipa-fold}. Only completeness is claimed here; that a
prover who cannot open $C$ cannot open $C'$ either is proved in
\S\ref{sec:ipa-binding}.

\paragraph{The evaluation vector folds into a power vector.}
When $\mathbf b = \mathbf b(x)$, the high half is the low half scaled:
$\mathbf b_{\mathrm{hi}} = x^{d/2}\,\mathbf b_{\mathrm{lo}}$, since
$b_{i + d/2} = x^{i + d/2} = x^{d/2} x^{i}$. Hence
\[
\mathbf b' = u^{-1}\mathbf b_{\mathrm{lo}} + u\,x^{d/2}\mathbf b_{\mathrm{lo}}
 = \bigl(u^{-1} + u\,x^{d/2}\bigr)\,(1, x, \dots, x^{d/2 - 1}) :
\]
the folded evaluation vector is again a vector of powers of $x$, scaled
by the scalar $u^{-1} + u x^{d/2}$. The verifier never needs to store
$\mathbf b'$; he needs one scalar per round. This is the seed of the
closed form $g$ of \S\ref{sec:ipa-verifier}.

\paragraph{The fold at $d = 4$, symbolically.}
For the running example's length, the halves are
$\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}} = (a_0, a_1)$, $\mathbf a_{\mathrm{hi}} = (a_2,
a_3)$, $\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}} = (G_0, G_1)$, $\mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}} =
(G_2, G_3)$, $\mathbf b_{\mathrm{lo}} = (1, x)$ and
$\mathbf b_{\mathrm{hi}} = (x^2, x^3)$, so the cross terms
of~\eqref{eq:ipa-LR} read
\begin{align*}
L &= [a_0]G_2 + [a_1]G_3 + [\ell]H + [a_0 x^2 + a_1 x^3]\,U, \\
R &= [a_2]G_0 + [a_3]G_1 + [\rho]H + [a_2 + a_3 x]\,U,
\end{align*}
and the folded vectors are $\mathbf a' = (u a_0 + u^{-1} a_2,\; u a_1 +
u^{-1} a_3)$, $\mathbf G' = ([u^{-1}]G_0 + [u]G_2,\; [u^{-1}]G_1 +
[u]G_3)$ and $\mathbf b' = (u^{-1} + u x^2)\,(1, x)$.
Section~\ref{sec:ipa-toy} fills these in with numbers.

  folded/.style={vec, fill=tierrecv!10, draw=tierrecv},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]}, line width=0.7pt, black!65}]
  % before
  \node[lo] (alo) at (0,2.6) {$\mathbf a_{\mathrm{lo}}$};

\subsection{The recursion: \texorpdfstring{$k$}{k} rounds to a single
  scalar}
\label{sec:ipa-recursion}

The folded claim of \S\ref{sec:ipa-fold} has the shape of the original
claim, so it can be folded again. After $k = \log_2 d$ folds the vectors have
length one, and a length-one claim is checked by sending the scalar and its
blinder. That is the whole protocol; this subsection writes it out as a
construction, numbers the rounds, and lays the two parties' work side by side.

\begin{construction}[IPA-based polynomial commitment]
\label{con:ipa-commit}
Let $\G$ be a cyclic group of prime order $p$ in which the discrete
logarithm problem is hard (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Standing
assumptions''), and let $\F = \F_p$. Choose $d = 2^k$ and sample the
generators $\mathbf G = (G_0, \dots, G_{d-1})$ and $H$ independently and
uniformly from $\G \setminus \{\mathcal O\}$, as in the \emph{Crypto
Guide}'s Construction ``Pedersen vector commitment'' (\S``Pedersen
vector commitments''). Deployed generators are instead hash-to-curve
outputs on public labels, uniform when hash-to-curve is modelled as a
random oracle into $\G$ (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Hashing to a curve
point'', Remark ``Why NUMS generators are binding-safe'').

\emph{Commitment.} For $p(X) = \sum_{i=0}^{d-1} a_i X^i \in \F[X]$ and a
blinder $r \in \F$,
\[
\Comm(p;\, r) := \ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf G} + [r]H \in \G .
\]

\emph{Opening.} To prove $((P, x, v); (\mathbf a, r)) \in
\Rel_{\mathrm{IPA}}$, the two parties proceed as follows.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Setup.} The verifier samples $U$ uniformly from
  $\G \setminus \{\mathcal O\}$ and sends it. Both parties set
  $C^{(k)} := P + [v]U$, $\mathbf G^{(k)} := \mathbf G$ and
  $\mathbf b^{(k)} := \mathbf b(x)$; the prover sets
  $\mathbf a^{(k)} := \mathbf a$ and $r^{(k)} := r$.
\item \textbf{Rounds.} For $j = k, k-1, \dots, 1$, the vectors
  $\mathbf a^{(j)}, \mathbf G^{(j)}, \mathbf b^{(j)}$ have length $2^{j}$
  and are split into halves as in \S\ref{sec:ipa-fold}.
  \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=1pt]
  \item The prover samples independent uniform $\ell_j, \rho_j \in \F$
    and sends
    \begin{align*}
    L_j &:= \ip{\mathbf a^{(j)}_{\mathrm{lo}}}{\mathbf G^{(j)}_{\mathrm{hi}}}
      + [\ell_j]H
      + [\ip{\mathbf a^{(j)}_{\mathrm{lo}}}
           {\mathbf b^{(j)}_{\mathrm{hi}}}]\,U, \\
    R_j &:= \ip{\mathbf a^{(j)}_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf G^{(j)}_{\mathrm{lo}}}
      + [\rho_j]H
      + [\ip{\mathbf a^{(j)}_{\mathrm{hi}}}{\mathbf b^{(j)}_{\mathrm{lo}}}]\,U .
    \end{align*}
  \item The verifier sends a uniform $u_j \in \F^\times$.
  \item Both parties fold by~\eqref{eq:ipa-fold-vectors}:
    \begin{gather*}
    \mathbf G^{(j-1)} := u_j^{-1}\mathbf G^{(j)}_{\mathrm{lo}}
      + u_j\,\mathbf G^{(j)}_{\mathrm{hi}},
    \qquad
    \mathbf b^{(j-1)} := u_j^{-1}\mathbf b^{(j)}_{\mathrm{lo}}
      + u_j\,\mathbf b^{(j)}_{\mathrm{hi}}, \\
    C^{(j-1)} := C^{(j)} + [u_j^{2}]L_j + [u_j^{-2}]R_j ;
    \end{gather*}
    the prover also folds
    $\mathbf a^{(j-1)} := u_j\,\mathbf a^{(j)}_{\mathrm{lo}}
      + u_j^{-1}\mathbf a^{(j)}_{\mathrm{hi}}$ and
    $r^{(j-1)} := r^{(j)} + u_j^{2}\ell_j + u_j^{-2}\rho_j$.
  \end{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Final message.} After round $1$ every vector has length
  one. Write $a := \mathbf a^{(0)}$, $G^{(0)} := \mathbf G^{(0)}$ and
  $b^{(0)} := \mathbf b^{(0)}$ for the single entries. The prover sends
  the scalar $a$ and the aggregated blinder
  \[
  r^{*} := r^{(0)} = r + \sum_{j=1}^{k}
    \bigl(u_j^{2}\,\ell_j + u_j^{-2}\rho_j\bigr) .
  \]
\item \textbf{Check.} The verifier accepts if and only if
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:ipa-final}
  P + [v]U + \sum_{j=1}^{k}\bigl([u_j^{2}]L_j + [u_j^{-2}]R_j\bigr)
  \;\overset{?}{=}\;
  [a]\,G^{(0)} + [r^{*}]H + [a\cdot b^{(0)}]\,U ,
  \end{equation}
  where the left-hand side is $C^{(0)}$ and $G^{(0)}$, $b^{(0)}$ are
  computed as \S\ref{sec:ipa-verifier} describes.
\end{enumerate}
\end{construction}

The round index descends so that round $j$ begins with vectors of
length $2^{j}$; round $k$ is the first round and round $1$ the last.
The scheme instantiates the polynomial-commitment interface of the
\emph{Crypto Guide} (\S``Polynomial commitment schemes'', the
inner-product alternative), and the opening protocol is the
inner-product argument of Bootle, Cerulli, Chaidos, Groth and Petit as
refined in Bulletproofs by B\"unz, Bootle, Boneh, Poelstra, Wuille and
Maxwell, in the symmetric normalisation those papers use.

\paragraph{Completeness.}
The honest prover always passes. By induction on the rounds,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ipa-invariant}
C^{(j)} = \ip{\mathbf a^{(j)}}{\mathbf G^{(j)}} + [r^{(j)}]H
  + [\ip{\mathbf a^{(j)}}{\mathbf b^{(j)}}]\,U
\qquad\text{for } j = k, k-1, \dots, 0 :
\end{equation}
at $j = k$ this is~\eqref{eq:ipa-C} with the honest $P$, and the step
from $j$ to $j - 1$ is the fold identity~\eqref{eq:ipa-fold} applied to
the round's vectors, challenge and blinders. At $j = 0$ the vectors have
one entry each, the inner products are products of scalars, and
\eqref{eq:ipa-invariant} reads $C^{(0)} = [a]G^{(0)} + [r^{*}]H +
[a\,b^{(0)}]U$, which is~\eqref{eq:ipa-final}. The only way an honest
run can fail is a challenge $u_j = 0$, which the verifier excludes.

\paragraph{Proof size.}
The prover sends two group elements per round and two field elements at
the end: $2k$ group elements plus $O(1)$, against the $d + 1 = 2^k + 1$
field elements of the trivial protocol. The verifier keeps only the
challenges $u_1, \dots, u_k$ and the received points; he need not fold
$\mathbf G$ round by round, as the next subsection shows.
Figure~\ref{fig:ipa-rounds} draws the $k$ rounds, and
Tables~\ref{tab:ipa-prover} and~\ref{tab:ipa-verifier} list, one row
per message, what each party holds, computes and sends, so that a reader
can play either role; \S\ref{sec:ipa-toy} refills the same rows with
numbers.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small, x=1cm,
  stage/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, minimum height=7mm,
    inner sep=3pt, align=flush center, fill=tierin!8, draw=tierin},
  msg/.style={font=\scriptsize, align=flush center},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]}, line width=0.7pt, black!65}]
  \node[stage] (s0) at (0,0) {length $d$\\$C^{(k)}$};
  \node[stage] (s1) at (3,0) {length $d/2$\\$C^{(k-1)}$};
  \node[stage] (s2) at (6,0) {length $d/4$\\$C^{(k-2)}$};
  \node (dots) at (8.4,0) {$\cdots$};
  \node[stage, fill=tierrecv!10, draw=tierrecv] (sk) at (10.8,0)
    {length $1$\\$C^{(0)}$};
  \draw[arr] (s0) -- node[msg, above=2pt] {$L_k, R_k \downarrow$\\
    $u_k \uparrow$} (s1);
  \draw[arr] (s1) -- node[msg, above=2pt] {$L_{k-1}, R_{k-1} \downarrow$\\
    $u_{k-1} \uparrow$} (s2);
  \draw[arr] (s2) -- (dots);
  \draw[arr] (dots) -- node[msg, above=2pt] {$L_1, R_1 \downarrow$\\
    $u_1 \uparrow$} (sk);
  \node[msg, below=3pt of s0, text width=2.4cm] {prover: $\mathbf a$,
    $r$\\verifier: $P$, $v$, $\mathbf G$, $\mathbf b(x)$};
  \node[msg, below=3pt of sk, text width=3.2cm] {prover sends $a$, $r^{*}$\\
    verifier computes $G^{(0)} = \ip{\mathbf s}{\mathbf G}$,
    $b^{(0)} = g(x; \mathbf u)$ and checks~\eqref{eq:ipa-final}};
  \node[msg, below=3pt of s1, text width=2.2cm] {verifier keeps
    $L_k, R_k, u_k$};

\subsection{The verifier's work: structured scalars and the final
  generator}
\label{sec:ipa-verifier}

The check~\eqref{eq:ipa-final} needs two quantities the verifier has
not yet been told how to obtain cheaply: the final generator
$G^{(0)}$ and the final evaluation scalar $b^{(0)}$. Folding
$\mathbf G$ round by round costs $d + d/2 + \dots + 2 = 2d - 2$ scalar
multiplications (two per entry of each folded vector), and folding
$\mathbf b$ likewise many field operations. Both have closed forms in
the challenges.

\begin{theorem}[Structured scalars and the polynomial $g$]
\label{thm:ipa-structured}
For $0 \le i < d$ write the binary expansion
$i = \sum_{\ell=0}^{k-1} \beta_\ell\,2^{\ell}$ with digits
$\beta_\ell \in \{0, 1\}$, and define the \emph{structured scalars}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ipa-s}
s_i := \prod_{\ell=1}^{k} u_\ell^{\,(-1)^{1 - \beta_{\ell-1}}}
  \qquad\text{(the factor is $u_\ell$ when } \beta_{\ell-1} = 1
  \text{ and } u_\ell^{-1} \text{ when } \beta_{\ell-1} = 0\text{)}.
\end{equation}
Then, after the $k$ folds of Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ipa-closed}
G^{(0)} = \ip{\mathbf s}{\mathbf G} = \sum_{i=0}^{d-1} [s_i]\,G_i,
\qquad
b^{(0)} = \ip{\mathbf s}{\mathbf b(x)} = g(x;\, u_1, \dots, u_k),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ipa-g}
g(X;\, u_1, \dots, u_k) := \prod_{\ell=1}^{k}
  \bigl(u_\ell^{-1} + u_\ell\,X^{2^{\ell-1}}\bigr),
\end{equation}
a polynomial of degree $d - 1$ in $X$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Follow one original generator $G_m$ through the folds. Claim: after the
rounds $k, k-1, \dots, j+1$ have been performed, $G_m$ contributes to
exactly one entry of $\mathbf G^{(j)}$, the entry at position
$m \bmod 2^{j}$, with coefficient
$\prod_{\ell=j+1}^{k} u_\ell^{(-1)^{1-\beta_{\ell-1}}}$, where
$\beta_{\ell-1}$ is the digit of $m$ at position $\ell - 1$. At $j = k$
nothing has happened and the claim says $G_m$ sits at position $m$ with
coefficient $1$. For the step from $j$ to $j - 1$: round $j$ folds the
length-$2^{j}$ vector by
$G^{(j-1)}_i = [u_j^{-1}]G^{(j)}_i + [u_j]G^{(j)}_{i + 2^{j-1}}$ for
$0 \le i < 2^{j-1}$. The position $m \bmod 2^{j}$ lies in the low half
$[0, 2^{j-1})$ exactly when the digit $\beta_{j-1}$ of $m$ is $0$, in
which case it moves to position $m \bmod 2^{j-1}$ with the extra factor
$u_j^{-1}$; and it lies in the high half exactly when $\beta_{j-1} = 1$,
in which case it is the entry $i + 2^{j-1}$ with $i = m \bmod 2^{j-1}$
and moves to position $i$ with the extra factor $u_j$. Either way the
new coefficient is the old one times
$u_j^{(-1)^{1 - \beta_{j-1}}}$, which is the claim for $j - 1$. At
$j = 0$ every $G_m$ sits in the single entry $G^{(0)}$ with coefficient
$s_m$, which is the first half of~\eqref{eq:ipa-closed}. The vector
$\mathbf b$ is folded with the same weights, so the same argument gives
$b^{(0)} = \sum_m s_m\,b_m = \ip{\mathbf s}{\mathbf b(x)}$.

For the closed form, expand the product~\eqref{eq:ipa-g}: choosing from
the $\ell$-th factor either $u_\ell^{-1}$ (when $\beta_{\ell-1} = 0$) or
$u_\ell X^{2^{\ell-1}}$ (when $\beta_{\ell-1} = 1$) produces, for each
$m$, the monomial
$s_m X^{\sum_{\ell=1}^{k} \beta_{\ell-1} 2^{\ell-1}} = s_m X^{m}$, and
every $m \in [0, d)$ arises from exactly one choice. Hence
$g(X) = \sum_m s_m X^m$, whose value at $x$ is
$\ip{\mathbf s}{\mathbf b(x)}$, and whose degree is $d - 1$ since the
top monomial has coefficient $s_{d-1} = \prod_\ell u_\ell \ne 0$.
\end{proof}

% ipa_toy.py: verifier: s = (u1^-1 u2^-1, u1 u2^-1, u1^-1 u2, u1 u2)
%   g(z; u1, u2) = (u1^-1 + u1 z)(u2^-1 + u2 z^2)
At $d = 4$ the scalars are $s_0 = u_1^{-1}u_2^{-1}$,
$s_1 = u_1 u_2^{-1}$, $s_2 = u_1^{-1}u_2$ and $s_3 = u_1 u_2$, and
$g(x; u_1, u_2) = (u_1^{-1} + u_1 x)(u_2^{-1} + u_2 x^2)$: the digit of
weight $1$ selects the exponent of $u_1$, the round that folded pairs of
neighbours, and the digit of weight $2$ selects the exponent of $u_2$,
the round that folded the two halves of the whole vector.

\paragraph{The final equation, and why it checks.}
The verifier's closing work is now explicit. He evaluates
$b^{(0)} = g(x; \mathbf u)$ from the challenges; he computes
$G^{(0)} = \ip{\mathbf s}{\mathbf G}$, one multi-scalar multiplication
of length $d$; he adds up the left-hand side of~\eqref{eq:ipa-final}
from $P$, $U$, the $2k$ received points and the challenges, $2k + 1$
scalar multiplications; and he compares it with the right-hand side,
three more scalar multiplications. For the honest prover the two sides
agree by the invariant~\eqref{eq:ipa-invariant}. That a dishonest prover
cannot make them agree except by knowing an opening is
\S\ref{sec:ipa-binding}.

% orchard_params.py:
% g(x) in the deployed form: 11 products u_j x^(2^i), 10 squarings of x,
%   10 factor multiplications = 3k - 2 = 31 field multiplications
% against the MSM G^(0) = <s, G>: 2048 scalar multiplications
\paragraph{The cost of the final check.}
Evaluating $g$ at $x$ costs $3k - 2$ field multiplications: $k$
products $u_\ell\,x^{2^{\ell-1}}$, $k - 1$ squarings to produce the
powers $x, x^2, x^4, \dots$, and $k - 1$ multiplications of the factors
together; at $k = 2$ that is four multiplications, and at Orchard's
$k = 11$ it is $31$. But $G^{(0)}$ is a linear combination of \emph{all}
$d$ generators, and computing it is a multi-scalar multiplication of
length $d$, at Orchard's $d = 2^{11}$ some $2048$ scalar
multiplications: the one part of the verifier's work that is linear in
the size of the committed vector, and the check ``linear in $n$'' that
\S\ref{sec:arith-perm} deferred to this subsection. Everything else the
verifier does is logarithmic. The asymmetry has a name in the closed
form: $G^{(0)} = \ip{\mathbf s}{\mathbf G}$ is nothing but
$\Comm(g;\, 0)$, the unblinded commitment to the polynomial $g$ whose
value the verifier just computed in $O(k)$ operations. Checking a
\emph{value} of $g$ is cheap; checking its \emph{commitment} is not.
This asymmetry is the seed of the accumulation scheme of
\S\ref{sec:pcd}, and of the deferral described at the end of this
section.

\subsection{Why it is binding: extraction from three transcripts}
\label{sec:ipa-binding}

This subsection shows that \emph{any} prover that makes the verifier of
Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit} accept knows an opening
$(\mathbf a, r)$ of $P$ with $\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b(x)} = v$, in the
operational sense of the \emph{Crypto Guide} (\S``Knowledge soundness
and extractors''): an extractor that runs the prover as a subroutine,
rewinds it to an earlier state and feeds it fresh challenges computes
the pair from its answers. The mechanism is the one that makes
Schnorr's identification protocol a proof of knowledge (\emph{Crypto
Guide}, \S``The Schnorr identification protocol''): a single transcript
reveals nothing, but two accepting transcripts with the same first
message and distinct challenges are two linear equations, and
subtracting them cancels the unknown commitment and leaves the witness.
Here each round has three unknown group elements instead of one, so
three transcripts are needed per round, and the $k$ rounds stack the
three-way branching into a tree, the tree special soundness of the
\emph{Crypto Guide} (\S``Sigma-protocols''). The single-round step is
pure linear algebra and is proved in full; the tree is an induction on
it; the discrete-logarithm assumption enters once, at the root.

\paragraph{Representations.}
Fix a level $j$ with generator vector $\mathbf G^{(j)}$ of length
$2^{j}$. A \emph{representation} of an element $Q \in \G$ over
$(\mathbf G^{(j)}, H, U)$ is a triple $(\mathbf x; y; w) \in \F^{2^{j}}
\times \F \times \F$ with
\[
Q = \ip{\mathbf x}{\mathbf G^{(j)}} + [y]H + [w]U .
\]
Call the representation \emph{well formed} at level $j$ if its
$U$-coordinate is the inner product of its $\mathbf G$-coordinates with
the round's evaluation vector, $w = \ip{\mathbf x}{\mathbf b^{(j)}}$.
The invariant~\eqref{eq:ipa-invariant} says exactly that the honest
prover holds a well-formed representation
$(\mathbf a^{(j)}; r^{(j)}; \ip{\mathbf a^{(j)}}{\mathbf b^{(j)}})$ of
$C^{(j)}$ at every level. A representation over folded generators
lifts to one over the generators of the level above: if
$Q = \ip{\mathbf a'}{\mathbf G'} + [y]H + [w]U$ with
$\mathbf G' = u^{-1}\mathbf G_{\mathrm{lo}} + u\,\mathbf G_{\mathrm{hi}}$,
then by bilinearity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ipa-lift}
Q = \ip{(u^{-1}\mathbf a',\; u\,\mathbf a')}{\mathbf G} + [y]H + [w]U ,
\end{equation}
and the lift preserves well-formedness, since
$\ip{(u^{-1}\mathbf a', u\mathbf a')}{\mathbf b}
 = \ip{\mathbf a'}{u^{-1}\mathbf b_{\mathrm{lo}} + u\,\mathbf b_{\mathrm{hi}}}
 = \ip{\mathbf a'}{\mathbf b'}$. Under the discrete-logarithm
assumption a prover can know at most one representation of any $Q$:
two of them differ by a nontrivial relation among $\mathbf G$, $H$ and
$U$, which is the binding argument of the \emph{Crypto Guide}
(\S``Pedersen vector commitments''). Nothing below uses this until the
root is reached.

\begin{lemma}[Single-round recovery]\label{lem:ipa-single-round}
Fix one round of Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit} at level $j$: the
verifier's element $C := C^{(j)}$, the generators $\mathbf G :=
\mathbf G^{(j)}$ and evaluation vector $\mathbf b := \mathbf b^{(j)}$,
and the prover's cross terms $L$, $R$. Let $u, u', u''$ be three
challenges for this round, and suppose that for each
$\nu \in \{u, u', u''\}$ a representation $(\mathbf x_\nu; y_\nu; w_\nu)$
of the folded element $C + [\nu^{2}]L + [\nu^{-2}]R$ over
$(\mathbf G, H, U)$ is known. If the squares $u^{2}, u'^{2}, u''^{2}$ are
pairwise distinct, then representations $(\mathbf x_L; y_L; w_L)$,
$(\mathbf x_C; y_C; w_C)$ and $(\mathbf x_R; y_R; w_R)$ of $L$, $C$
and $R$ over $(\mathbf G, H, U)$ are obtained by solving one
$3 \times 3$ linear system with rows $(\nu^{2}, 1, \nu^{-2})$, and
they satisfy, for each $\nu$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ipa-rep-identity}
\mathbf x_\nu = \nu^{2}\mathbf x_L + \mathbf x_C + \nu^{-2}\mathbf x_R,
\qquad
y_\nu = \nu^{2} y_L + y_C + \nu^{-2} y_R,
\qquad
w_\nu = \nu^{2} w_L + w_C + \nu^{-2} w_R .
\end{equation}
If the three given representations are well formed at level $j$, so
is the recovered representation of $C$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write the three known representations as vectors
$\mathbf Y_\nu := (\mathbf x_\nu, y_\nu, w_\nu) \in \F^{2^{j} + 2}$ and
the three unknown ones as $\mathbf X_L, \mathbf X_C, \mathbf X_R$. The
three group equations
\[
[\nu^{2}]L + C + [\nu^{-2}]R
 = \ip{\mathbf x_\nu}{\mathbf G} + [y_\nu]H + [w_\nu]U,
\qquad \nu \in \{u, u', u''\},
\]
suggest the linear system
\[
M \begin{pmatrix} \mathbf X_L \\ \mathbf X_C \\ \mathbf X_R \end{pmatrix}
 = \begin{pmatrix} \mathbf Y_u \\ \mathbf Y_{u'} \\ \mathbf Y_{u''}
   \end{pmatrix},
\qquad
M := \begin{pmatrix}
  u^{2}   & 1 & u^{-2} \\
  u'^{2}  & 1 & u'^{-2} \\
  u''^{2} & 1 & u''^{-2}
\end{pmatrix},
\]
to be solved coordinate by coordinate. Multiplying the row of $\nu$ by
the nonzero scalar $\nu^{2}$ turns it into $(\nu^{4}, \nu^{2}, 1)$, which
does not change the kernel. If $M(c_0, c_1, c_2)^{\top} = 0$, the
polynomial $c_0 W^{2} + c_1 W + c_2$ of degree at most $2$ vanishes at
$W = u^{2}, u'^{2}, u''^{2}$; when these squares are pairwise distinct it
is zero (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Roots and the factor theorem'', Theorem
``At most $d$ roots''), so $M$ is invertible. When two squares coincide,
two rows of $M$ coincide and $M$ is singular. Define
$(\mathbf X_L, \mathbf X_C, \mathbf X_R)$ as the unique solution.
Because it solves the system exactly,
$\mathbf Y_\nu = \nu^{2}\mathbf X_L + \mathbf X_C + \nu^{-2}\mathbf X_R$
for each $\nu$, which is~\eqref{eq:ipa-rep-identity} read coordinate by
coordinate. That the solutions are representations of $L$, $C$ and $R$
follows by taking the same linear combinations of the group equations:
let $(\alpha, \alpha', \alpha'')$ be the middle row of $M^{-1}$, so
that $\alpha(u^{2}, 1, u^{-2}) + \alpha'(u'^{2}, 1, u'^{-2})
+ \alpha''(u''^{2}, 1, u''^{-2}) = (0, 1, 0)$ and $\mathbf X_C = \alpha
\mathbf Y_u + \alpha'\mathbf Y_{u'} + \alpha''\mathbf Y_{u''}$. Adding
$\alpha$ times the first group equation, $\alpha'$ times the second and
$\alpha''$ times the third gives, on the left, $[0]L + [1]C + [0]R =
C$, and on the right, by bilinearity, $\ip{\mathbf x_C}{\mathbf G} +
[y_C]H + [w_C]U$. The first and third rows of $M^{-1}$ do the same for
$L$ and $R$. For well-formedness, set $\delta_\nu := w_\nu -
\ip{\mathbf x_\nu}{\mathbf b}$ and $\delta_L := w_L - \ip{\mathbf x_L}
{\mathbf b}$, $\delta_C := w_C - \ip{\mathbf x_C}{\mathbf b}$,
$\delta_R := w_R - \ip{\mathbf x_R}{\mathbf b}$. Subtracting the inner
product of the first identity of~\eqref{eq:ipa-rep-identity} with
$\mathbf b$ from the third gives $\delta_\nu = \nu^{2}\delta_L +
\delta_C + \nu^{-2}\delta_R$ for each $\nu$, that is,
$M(\delta_L, \delta_C, \delta_R)^{\top} = (\delta_u, \delta_{u'},
\delta_{u''})^{\top}$. If the given representations are well formed the
right-hand side is zero, and $M$ invertible forces $\delta_L = \delta_C
= \delta_R = 0$; in particular $w_C = \ip{\mathbf x_C}{\mathbf b}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Knowledge soundness of the opening protocol]
\label{thm:ipa-ks}
Let $\G$ be a group of prime order in which the discrete logarithm
problem is hard, and let $\mathbf G$ and $H$ be uniform in
$\G \setminus \{\mathcal O\}$ as in Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit}
(for hashed generators: hash-to-curve modelled as a random oracle into
$\G$). The opening protocol of Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit} is
knowledge sound for $\Rel_{\mathrm{IPA}}$: there is an extractor which,
given rewindable access to any prover that makes the verifier accept
with non-negligible probability, runs in expected polynomial time and
outputs either a witness $(\mathbf a, r)$ with $P = \ip{\mathbf a}
{\mathbf G} + [r]H$ and $\ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf b(x)} = v$, or a
nontrivial discrete-logarithm relation among $\mathbf G$, $H$ and the
verifier's generators $U$, $U'$. Its knowledge error is
$O(\log d/|\F|)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The proof is given at the level of this volume: the single-round step
is Lemma~\ref{lem:ipa-single-round}, the tree is an induction on it,
and the rewinding that supplies the sibling transcripts is the
\emph{Crypto Guide}'s (\S``The Schnorr identification protocol'', the
remark on extraction by rewinding, and \S``Sigma-protocols'', the
tree-extraction theorem cited there, which also gives the expected
running time). The trees it assembles have children with pairwise
distinct challenges (the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s Definition ``Tree special
soundness''), while
Lemma~\ref{lem:ipa-single-round} needs pairwise distinct squares.
A square has at most two square roots, so any five pairwise distinct
challenges contain three with pairwise distinct squares: the rewinding
assembles five children per node, and the extractor uses three of them.
From a tree the extractor
computes a witness or, at the root, a discrete-logarithm relation; that
definition asks for a witness alone, and the difference is where the
assumption enters.

\emph{Leaves.} Rewind the prover to the moment after it sent $(L_1,
R_1)$ and obtain three accepting final messages $(a, r^{*})$ for three
challenges $u_1$ with pairwise distinct squares. An accepting final
message is, by~\eqref{eq:ipa-final}, a representation of $C^{(0)}$
over $(G^{(0)}, H, U)$, namely $(a; r^{*}; a\,b^{(0)})$, and it is well
formed at level $0$: its $U$-coordinate is the inner product of $a$
with the length-one vector $b^{(0)}$. Lift it to level $1$
by~\eqref{eq:ipa-lift}: $\mathbf x = (u_1^{-1}a, u_1 a)$.

\emph{One node.} The three lifted leaves are well-formed
representations of the three folded elements $C^{(1)} + [u_1^{2}]L_1 +
[u_1^{-2}]R_1$ over $(\mathbf G^{(1)}, H, U)$. Lemma~%
\ref{lem:ipa-single-round} at the round-$1$ node returns a
well-formed representation of $C^{(1)}$ (and representations of $L_1$
and $R_1$, which are not needed further).

\emph{Induction.} Rewind to the moment after $(L_2, R_2)$, obtain three
challenges $u_2$ with pairwise distinct squares, and under each of them
repeat the two steps above. That yields, after lifting to level $2$,
three well-formed representations of the elements $C^{(2)} +
[u_2^{2}]L_2 + [u_2^{-2}]R_2$, and the lemma at the round-$2$ node
returns a well-formed representation of $C^{(2)}$. Continuing upward,
after $k$ levels the extractor holds a well-formed representation
$(\mathbf x; y; w)$ of $C^{(k)} = P + [v]U$ over $(\mathbf G, H, U)$,
with $w = \ip{\mathbf x}{\mathbf b(x)}$. The tree of transcripts
consumed has three children at every node and depth $k$, hence $3^{k} =
d^{\log_2 3}$ leaves (Figure~\ref{fig:ipa-tree}).

\emph{The root, where the assumption enters.} The root representation
says $P = \ip{\mathbf x}{\mathbf G} + [y]H + [w - v]U$. If $w = v$ then
$(\mathbf x, y)$ is a witness: $P = \ip{\mathbf x}{\mathbf G} + [y]H$
and $\ip{\mathbf x}{\mathbf b(x)} = w = v$. If $w \ne v$, the extractor
rewinds once more, to the setup step at which the verifier sampled
$U$, and repeats the whole extraction under an independent sample
$U'$, obtaining $P = \ip{\mathbf x'}{\mathbf G} + [y']H + [w' - v]U'$.
If $w' = v$ the witness is $(\mathbf x', y')$. Otherwise subtracting the
two expressions for $P$ gives
\[
[w - v]\,U - [w' - v]\,U' + \ip{\mathbf x - \mathbf x'}{\mathbf G}
 + [y - y']H = \mathcal O
\]
with the nonzero coefficient $w - v$ on $U$: a nontrivial relation
among the independent uniform generators $\mathbf G$, $H$, $U$, $U'$,
which the every-generator embedding of the \emph{Crypto Guide}
(\S``Pedersen vector commitments'', the proof of binding) turns into a
discrete logarithm, the reduction embedding its challenge in the public
generators and in the verifier's samples alike. This is the only place
the assumption is used.

\emph{The error.} The figure does not come from a union bound over the
tree. At each node the extractor needs three siblings whose challenges
avoid $O(1)$ values: $\pm\nu$ for each earlier sibling's challenge
$\nu$, so that the squares stay pairwise distinct. Each of the
$k = \log_2 d$ rounds therefore contributes $O(1/|\F|)$, and the rounds
together with the resampling of $U$ give $O(\log d / |\F|)$, the
theorem's figure. Collisions
among the challenges resampled inside the $3^{k}$-leaf tree cost the
extractor a rewind and a fresh sample; they affect its expected running
time, not the knowledge error. A naive union bound over the roughly
$3^{k}$ sibling draws would bound the tree-wide collision probability
by $O(d^{\log_2 3}/|\F|)$, still negligible, but that is not the source
of the figure. The weaker $O(d/|\F|)$ sometimes quoted for this
argument is implied by the proved bound; a larger protocol whose own
error is already linear in $d$ loses nothing by quoting it.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{What the theorem does not say.}
The theorem concerns the interactive protocol; the compiled argument is
treated in \S\ref{sec:ks}. In the algebraic group model the extractor
does not rewind for the representations at all: an algebraic prover
hands them over with every group element it sends, and the tree
collapses to a single transcript (\S\ref{sec:ks}, ``The algebraic group
model''). After the Fiat--Shamir transform has replaced the verifier's
challenges by hashes, the rewinding above must be re-proved against a
prover who can query the hash adaptively and resume from any earlier
transcript state (for one round, the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s forking
lemma, \S``Security in the random oracle model and the forking lemma'';
the multi-round case in \S\ref{sec:ks}), and its cost cannot be
obtained by multiplying every node of the tree by an independent
$1/\varepsilon$ factor. Nor does the theorem restate the binding of the
commitment itself: that a single $P$ cannot be opened as two vectors is
the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s theorem, cited in \S\ref{sec:arith-commit}
and not reproved here.

\paragraph{The assumption, and what breaks it.}
The extractor's alternative output is a discrete-logarithm relation
among $\mathbf G$, $H$ and the verifier's $U$, $U'$, so the argument is
binding as long as discrete logarithms are hard in $\G$ and the
generators are uniform; hashed generators are uniform when hash-to-curve
is modelled as a random oracle into $\G$ (\emph{Crypto Guide},
\S``Hashing to a curve point'', Remark ``Why NUMS generators are
binding-safe''), a hypothesis of Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks}. For the
deployed instance
the group is the Vesta curve (\S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}), and the
assumption that carries the argument is the discrete logarithm on
Vesta, the first of the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s standing assumptions
(\S``Standing assumptions''), against which the generic attacks cost
about $2^{126}$ operations (\S``Instantiation on elliptic curves; the
Pasta curves''); a quantum adversary running Shor's algorithm lies
outside those assumptions, which fix a classical adversary. Suppose the
assumption fails: an algorithm computes discrete logarithms on Vesta.
Then it computes the logarithms $\eta_0, \dots, \eta_{d-1}, \eta_H$ of the
hashed generators to one base, and every commitment
$P = [\pi]\,\mathrm{base}$ opens to every vector: for any
$\tilde{\mathbf a}$ whatsoever, the blinder
$\tilde r := (\pi - \ip{\tilde{\mathbf a}}{\boldsymbol\eta})/\eta_H$
satisfies $\ip{\tilde{\mathbf a}}{\mathbf G} + [\tilde r]H = P$, and the
honest protocol run on $(\tilde{\mathbf a}, \tilde r)$ convinces the
verifier of $\ip{\tilde{\mathbf a}}{\mathbf b(x)}$, a value the cheat
chose. One
commitment then opens to two vectors and to two values at one point;
binding and evaluation binding fall together, and with them the premise
of Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-random} that the polynomials were fixed
before $z$ was drawn.

\begin{example}[The extractor run on the toy]
\label{ex:ipa-extract}
% ipa_extract.py:
% a = [90, 61, 22, 24], z = 20, v = 59, r = 88, P = (51, 31), P + [v]U = (43, 6)
% == sym fold: rows (u^2, 1, u^-2) on the unknowns (L, C, R) ==
% node u_2 = 18: three u_1 = [85, 19, 84], det = 67; recovered a^(1) = [80, 0], blinder 3, U-coeff 40 = <a^(1), b^(1)>: True
% node u_2 = 81: three u_1 = [37, 43, 46], det = 49; recovered a^(1) = [50, 41], blinder 87, U-coeff 73 = <a^(1), b^(1)>: True
% node u_2 = 21: three u_1 = [38, 43, 93], det = 6; recovered a^(1) = [85, 35], blinder 75, U-coeff 79 = <a^(1), b^(1)>: True
% root: three u_2 = [18, 81, 21], det = 75; recovered a = [90, 61, 22, 24], r = 88, U-coeff = 59
The extractor is run against an honest prover for the running example's
vector $\mathbf a = (90, 61, 22, 24)$ opened at $z = 20$ with $v = 59$,
on the prime-order-$97$ curve and generators of \S\ref{sec:ipa-toy},
with the blinder $r = 88$, so $P = (51, 31)$ and
$C^{(2)} = P + [59]U = (43, 6)$. It fixes
$(L_2, R_2)$, branches over three round-$2$ challenges
$u_2 \in \{18, 81, 21\}$, and under each fixes $(L_1, R_1)$ and
branches over three round-$1$ challenges: $\{85, 19, 84\}$,
$\{37, 43, 46\}$ and $\{38, 43, 93\}$, nine leaves in all. At the node
$u_2 = 18$ the $3 \times 3$ system has determinant $67$ and returns the
representation $\mathbf a^{(1)} = (80, 0)$ with blinder $3$ and
$U$-coordinate $40 = \ip{\mathbf a^{(1)}}{\mathbf b^{(1)}}$, well
formed as the lemma promises; the other two nodes return $(50, 41)$
and $(85, 35)$ likewise. At the root the system in $u_2$ has
determinant $75$ and returns $\mathbf a = (90, 61, 22, 24)$, $r = 88$
and $U$-coordinate $59 = v$: the extracted witness is the committed
vector, its blinder, and the true value.
Figure~\ref{fig:ipa-tree} draws the tree with the node
$u_2 = 18$ called out.
\end{example}
