\subsubsection{The ``Halo'' in Halo 2: deferring the expensive check}
\label{sec:ipa-halo-trick}

The verifier of this section is logarithmic everywhere but in one
place. He reads $2k + 1$ points and two scalars, evaluates $g$ in
$3k - 2$ field multiplications, and forms the left-hand side
of~\eqref{eq:ipa-dep-final} with $2k + 2$ scalar multiplications
($[\xi]S$, $[v]G_0$ and the $2k$ round terms); then he needs
$G^{(0)} = \ip{\mathbf s}{\mathbf G}$, one combination of \emph{all}
$2048$ generators, group arithmetic proportional to the size of the
whole circuit (\S\ref{sec:ipa-verifier}). Two ways of amortising that cost
follow, batch verification and accumulation, the second not deployed by
Orchard; between them, the paragraph ``Transparency: no trusted setup''
records why the linear cost is accepted.

\paragraph{Batch verification.}
% orchard_params.py: against the MSM G^(0) = <s, G>: 2048 scalar multiplications
% proof_bytes.py: shared block 33 points, per-Action block 22 points
The consensus rules require each Action proof to be valid for the
verifying key of the Orchard circuit (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actiondesc}{\S~4.6},
``Action Descriptions''); validity includes the
check~\eqref{eq:ipa-dep-final} with its multi-scalar multiplication of
length $d$, and the specification composes no proofs, so the verifier
pays that multiplication itself rather than passing it on. Several
proofs can share it (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Polynomial commitment
schemes'', Remark ``KZG versus IPA, and the Halo lineage''). Written as
one multi-scalar multiplication that must evaluate to $\mathcal O$,
equation~\eqref{eq:ipa-dep-final} has $d + 2k + 4$ terms: the
generators $G_0, \dots, G_{d-1}$, $U$, $H$, $P$, $S$ and the $2k$
points $L_j$, $R_j$. The equations $E_1, \dots, E_b$ of $b$ proofs are
checked together by testing $\sum_i [\mu_i]E_i = \mathcal O$ for
fresh uniform $\mu_i \in \F$ drawn by the verifier.

\begin{lemma}[Randomised batch check]\label{lem:ipa-batch}
Let $\G$ have prime order, let $E_1, \dots, E_b \in \G$, and let
$\mu_1, \dots, \mu_b \in \F$ be independent and uniform. If some
$E_i \ne \mathcal O$, then
$\Pr\bigl[\sum_{j} [\mu_j]E_j = \mathcal O\bigr] = 1/|\F|$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Fix $i$ with $E_i \ne \mathcal O$ and condition on every $\mu_j$ with
$j \ne i$. Since $\G$ has prime order, $E_i$ generates it, so
$\mu \mapsto [\mu]E_i$ is a bijection $\F \to \G$, and the sum equals
$\mathcal O$ for exactly one value of $\mu_i$.
\end{proof}

Because $\mathbf G$, $U$ and $H$ are shared, their terms merge, and $b$
equations combine into one multi-scalar multiplication of
$d + 2 + b(2k + 2)$ terms, against $b(d + 2k + 4)$ when checked
separately. In the complete protocol $P$ is itself a combination of
commitments (\S\ref{sec:halo2}); those of the verifying key are shared
as well, and each further proof adds its own points, the $22a + 33$ of
Table~\ref{tab:proof-bytes}, and the instance commitments computed from
its public inputs, one per Action (\S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}). The $d = 2048$
generator terms are paid once per batch. A batch check is not
accumulation: it emits nothing that a later proof could consume, and it
verifies the batch and stops. Both take linear combinations of deferred
equations; their interfaces and guarantees differ, and the accumulation
scheme of B\"unz, Chiesa, Mishra and Spooner, sketched below under
``Accumulation (not deployed by Orchard)'', is not part of the specified
protocol.

\paragraph{Transparency: no trusted setup.}
The generators are outputs of a public hash-to-curve map on fixed inputs
(this subsection, ``Generators from a hash''); the scheme has no trapdoor
and requires no trusted setup, unlike KZG, whose trapdoor permits
forgeries (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Polynomial commitment schemes'', under
``KZG: evaluation in the exponent''). The price is the $2k + 1$ points of
Table~\ref{tab:proof-bytes}, against the constant-size opening proof of
KZG, and the linear verifier. ZIP~224's motivation names the generation of
the structured reference string of Zcash's earlier proving system a point
of risk within the protocol and asks for a proving system that does not
require one
(\href{https://zips.z.cash/zip-0224\#motivation}{ZIP~224, Motivation}).

\paragraph{Accumulation (not deployed by Orchard).}
In a recursive verifier, a circuit that verifies a proof, the
multi-scalar multiplication $G^{(0)} = \ip{\mathbf s}{\mathbf G} =
\Comm(g;\, 0)$ of \S\ref{sec:ipa-verifier} would cost $d$ scalar
multiplications inside the circuit. Instead the claim is combined with
a running claim of the same shape, $G'^{(0)} = \Comm(g';\, 0)$, by a
random linear combination with a challenge $\rho$ drawn after both (a
verifier challenge, not a round blinder $\rho_j$ of this section;
\S\ref{sec:pcd} keeps the letter):
$G^{(0)} + [\rho]\,G'^{(0)} = \Comm(g + \rho\,g';\, 0)$ holds for all
$\rho$ when both inputs hold and for at most one $\rho$ otherwise. The
combined claim, returned to a single tuple by a prover-assisted opening,
is the \emph{accumulator} carried along the chain of proofs, and a
single multi-scalar multiplication outside any circuit at the end
discharges all combined claims. The soundness of an accumulation scheme
for a closely related inner-product commitment is cited in
\S\ref{sec:pcd} from B\"unz, Chiesa, Mishra and Spooner, under the
extractability of its openings, which they conjecture from the binding
of the Pedersen commitment; the cycle of curves that
keeps the recursive arithmetic in the right fields is the other half of
Pasta. Both are developed in \S\ref{sec:pcd}, under ``Accumulation and
the Halo trick'' and ``A cycle of curves for efficient native recursion'',
where recursion and accumulation over the cycle are classified
designed-but-unspecified in the sense of \S\ref{sec:intro-claim}: the
protocol specification uses Halo~2 only to prove and verify Action
statements and composes no proofs.

% 04-halo2.tex -- sec:halo2. Every number in this section is read from
% compute/OUTPUT.md, blocks protocol_params.py, orchard_params.py,
% proof_bytes.py, gadget_checks.py, pallas_constants.py, blinding_rows.py,
% halo2_counts.py and toy_trace.py; the relevant lines are
% repeated as comments above each passage that uses them.

\section{The Halo 2 proof system}
\label{sec:halo2}

This section defines the Halo~2 proof system as deployed for the Orchard
Action statement: the Action circuit, the instance column, the transcript,
the multipoint opening, the protocol, and its zero knowledge. It combines
the arithmetisation of \S\ref{sec:arith}, which reduces a circuit to
committed polynomials and identities among them (the gates, the
permutation argument for the wiring, the lookup argument for table
membership), with the polynomial commitment of \S\ref{sec:ipa}, which
opens a committed polynomial at a point in logarithmically many group
elements and is binding under the discrete logarithm on Vesta. The
abstractions the \emph{Crypto Guide} owns, the non-interactive argument
and the SNARK, the polynomial interactive oracle proof, the Fiat--Shamir
transform and the polynomial-commitment interface, are recalled at the
point of use. Halo~2 is a \emph{transparent} PLONKish argument: the
parameters of its commitment scheme are derived by hashing to the curve
and carry no trapdoor (\S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}), and for the Orchard Action
circuit its polynomial commitment works over Vesta. The protocol
specification defines Action proofs as proofs of the Halo~2 proving
system described in the halo2 book (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#halo2}{\S~5.4.10.3},
``Halo 2''); this section cites the book, by chapter, as the
specification of the protocol, and names an implementation only for a
behaviour that neither document fixes.

The subsections treat, in order, the Action circuit as a PLONKish circuit
(its gates, one gate in full, its lookup arguments and its column
polynomials); the instance column; the Fiat--Shamir transform and the
deployed transcript; the multipoint opening argument; the complete
protocol in seven phases; its zero knowledge; and the
compiled-polynomial-IOP pattern the protocol instantiates. Recursion is
not deployed by Orchard and is treated in \S\ref{sec:pcd}.

\paragraph{Notation.}
The constraint degree is $d_{\max}$, as Definition~\ref{def:plonkish} fixed
it; the letter $d$ is the committed vector length of \S\ref{sec:ipa}, which
equals the row count $n$ throughout this section. The letter $H$ carries
three meanings; two come from the sections this one joins:
\S\ref{sec:arith} writes $H$ for the evaluation domain and \S\ref{sec:ipa}
for the blinding generator of the Pedersen commitment. Here the domain
appears only through its vanishing polynomial $\ZH(X) = X^n - 1$, its
Lagrange basis $\ell_j$ and its rows $\omega^j$; a bare $H$ inside a
commitment formula is the blinding generator, as in \S\ref{sec:ipa}.
In \S\ref{sec:halo2-fs} and the security statements the letter $H$ also
names the transcript hash, modelled as a random oracle, as in the
\emph{Crypto Guide}; there it is applied to arguments, $H(\cdot)$, or
called the hash or the oracle. The letter $x$ is the evaluation challenge
of phase~5; the running example's witness is written by its value $3$
here and in \S\ref{sec:verdict}.

\subsection{From statement to circuit: arithmetisation in practice}
\label{sec:halo2-practice}

% pinned circuit description of the deployed Action circuit: k: 11,
%   num_fixed_columns: 29, num_advice_columns: 10, num_instance_columns: 1;
%   three lookup arguments; protocol_params.py: D = 9
Definition~\ref{def:plonkish} says what a PLONKish circuit is; the deployed
instance is the Orchard Action circuit. The protocol specification fixes the
statement it proves, the Action statement (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actionstatement}{\S~4.18.4},
``Action Statement (Orchard)''), and requires every Action proof to verify under
the verifying key identified by the current Orchard circuit version (protocol
specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actiondesc}{\S~4.6}, ``Action
Descriptions'', consensus rules). No specification or ZIP fixes
the parameters of the circuit behind that key; they are those of its
implementation, \texttt{orchard}. As a PLONKish circuit in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:plonkish} it has the field $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$,
$n = 2^{11}$ rows, ten advice columns, one instance column and
twenty-nine fixed columns, among them its selector columns and the three
columns of its lookup table; the constraint-degree bound $d_{\max} = 9$;
and three lookup arguments into that table, of $2^{10}$ rows. Its custom
gates encode the conditions of the Action statement, using among others
complete and incomplete addition on Pallas, the distinction the
\emph{Math Guide} draws in \S``Explicit affine formulas'' (its remark on
complete versus incomplete addition). This subsection gives the form of
the gates, one gate in full, the three lookup arguments, and the column
polynomials on which every argument of \S\ref{sec:arith} operates.

\paragraph{Key and witness.}
The verifying key is a deterministic function of the commitment
parameters and the circuit description: the values of the fixed columns,
the gates, the lookup arguments and the permutation $\sigma$ of
Definition~\ref{def:plonkish}. For the Action circuit it is a fixed
consensus parameter, the key the consensus rule above identifies. The
prover's witness is the content of the advice columns. A copy constraint
places two cells in one cycle of $\sigma$
(Construction~\ref{con:arith-perm}), and the public inputs are bound to
advice cells in this way (\S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}).

\subsubsection{Custom gates}
\label{sec:halo2-gates}

Every custom gate of the deployed circuit, a gate in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:plonkish}, has the form
\[
q \cdot c\bigl(v_1(\omega^{r_1}X), \dots, v_s(\omega^{r_s}X)\bigr) = 0,
\]
where $v_1, \dots, v_s$ are columns read at rotations $r_1, \dots, r_s$,
$c$ is a polynomial, and the \emph{selector} $q$ is a polynomial in the
fixed columns that is zero on every row where the gate does not apply
(the halo2 book's \emph{PLONKish Arithmetization} chapter). The gate is
required to vanish at every row; on the rows where $q$ is zero, the
blinding rows among them, it holds whatever the cells contain. The
universal gate of vanilla PLONK is the special case of
\S\ref{sec:arith-plonkish}.

\subsubsection{The incomplete-addition gate}
\label{sec:halo2-addinc}

The incomplete-addition gate of the Action circuit constrains $R = P + Q$
for affine points $P = (x_p, y_p)$ and $Q = (x_q, y_q)$ of Pallas with
$x_p \ne x_q$. It has one selector $q_{\mathrm{add}}$ and four
equality-enabled advice columns $x_p$, $y_p$, $x_{qr}$ and $y_{qr}$; it
reads $x_p, y_p, x_q, y_q$ at the current row and $x_r, y_r$ at the next
row of the \emph{same} two columns $x_{qr}$, $y_{qr}$, so that $Q$
occupies the selector row and $R$ the row below it. Its two constraints
are
\begin{align}
q_{\mathrm{add}}\cdot\bigl((x_r + x_q + x_p)(x_p - x_q)^2
  - (y_p - y_q)^2\bigr) &= 0, \label{eq:halo2-addinc-xr}\\
q_{\mathrm{add}}\cdot\bigl((y_r + y_q)(x_p - x_q)
  - (y_p - y_q)(x_q - x_r)\bigr) &= 0 \label{eq:halo2-addinc-yr}
\end{align}
(the halo2 book's \emph{Incomplete and complete addition} chapter).

\begin{theorem}[The gate is the chord formula with the slope eliminated]
\label{thm:halo2-addinc}
Let $P = (x_p, y_p)$ and $Q = (x_q, y_q)$ be affine points of a short
Weierstrass curve with $x_p \ne x_q$, and set
$\lambda := (y_p - y_q)/(x_p - x_q)$. For a pair $(x_r, y_r) \in \F^2$ the
following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=1pt]
\item the bracketed polynomials of~\eqref{eq:halo2-addinc-xr}
  and~\eqref{eq:halo2-addinc-yr} both vanish;
\item $x_r + x_q + x_p = \lambda^2$ and $y_r + y_q = \lambda\,(x_q - x_r)$;
\item $(x_r, y_r) = P + Q$ by the chord formulas of the \emph{Math Guide}
  (\S``Explicit affine formulas''), $x_r = \lambda^2 - x_p - x_q$ and
  $y_r = \lambda\,(x_p - x_r) - y_p$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(i)$\iff$(ii). Since $x_p - x_q \ne 0$, dividing the first bracket by
$(x_p - x_q)^2$ and the second by $(x_p - x_q)$ are invertible operations:
the first bracket vanishes exactly when
$x_r + x_q + x_p = (y_p - y_q)^2/(x_p - x_q)^2 = \lambda^2$, and the second
exactly when $y_r + y_q = \lambda\,(x_q - x_r)$. The gate stores the cleared
forms because a constraint must be a polynomial in the cells, and a
division is not.

(ii)$\iff$(iii). The $x$-equations are the same equation rearranged. For
the $y$-equations, the chord formula uses the slope of the line through
$P$ and $Q$, which is $\lambda$ whichever endpoint is subtracted from
which, and the identity $\lambda\,(x_p - x_q) = y_p - y_q$ gives
$\lambda x_p - y_p = \lambda x_q - y_q$; hence
$\lambda\,(x_p - x_r) - y_p = \lambda x_q - y_q - \lambda x_r
 = \lambda\,(x_q - x_r) - y_q$, which is (ii)'s $y$-equation with $y_q$
moved across.
\end{proof}

% gadget_checks.py:
% toy curve over F_79: P = (12, 25), Q = (71, 53), chord P + Q = (58, 71);
%   constraints evaluate to (0, 0)
% Pallas (P = (-1, 2), Q = [2]P): ... chord P + Q = (...); constraints
%   evaluate to (0, 0)
On the $97$-point toy curve of \S\ref{sec:ipa-toy}, $y^2 = x^3 + 3$ over
$\F_{79}$, the chord formulas give $P + Q = (58, 71)$ for $P = (12, 25)$
and $Q = (71, 53)$, and both brackets are $0$ at
$(x_r, y_r) = (58, 71)$. On Pallas, with $P = (-1, 2)$ and $Q = [2]P$,
both brackets are again $0$ at the chord sum.

\paragraph{Degrees.}
% gadget_checks.py: degrees: x_r constraint 3, y_r constraint 2; with the
%   selector factor: 4, 3
The bracket of~\eqref{eq:halo2-addinc-xr} has degree three in the cells
and that of~\eqref{eq:halo2-addinc-yr} degree two; with a selector column
taking values in $\{0, 1\}$ the constraints have degrees four and three.
The deployed key combines selectors into shared fixed columns (the halo2
book's \emph{Selector combining} chapter), which can raise both degrees,
keeping them within the deployed bound $d_{\max} = 9$.

\paragraph{Layout.}
The selector $q_{\mathrm{add}}$ is $1$ on row $i$, which holds $P$ and
$Q$, each coordinate copy-constrained to the cell it came from; row
$i + 1$ holds $R$ (Table~\ref{tab:halo2-addinc-region}). The slope
$\lambda$ occupies no cell: the
identities~\eqref{eq:halo2-addinc-xr}--\eqref{eq:halo2-addinc-yr} verify
the sum without it, which is what Theorem~\ref{thm:halo2-addinc} says. By
that theorem the two constraints force $(x_r, y_r) = P + Q$, a point of
the curve, and $R \ne \mathcal O$ because $x_p \ne x_q$ excludes
$Q = -P$.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{l cccc c}
\toprule
row & $x_p$ & $y_p$ & $x_{qr}$ & $y_{qr}$ & $q_{\mathrm{add}}$ \\
\midrule
$i$     & $x_p$ & $y_p$ & $x_q$ & $y_q$ & $1$ \\
$i + 1$ & --    & --    & $x_r$ & $y_r$ & -- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The cells of one incomplete addition: two rows of four advice
columns and the selector. The four input cells $x_p, y_p, x_q, y_q$ on
the selector row $i$ are copy-constrained to the cells they came from;
the result cells $x_r, y_r$ occupy the $x_{qr}, y_{qr}$ columns of row
$i + 1$, where the gate's next-rotation queries find them. Dashes mark
cells the gate does not use. The gate does not read $q_{\mathrm{add}}$ on
row $i + 1$. When additions are chained, as in fixed-base scalar
multiplication, row $i + 1$ is the selector row of the next addition, and
$R$ is that addition's $Q$.}
\label{tab:halo2-addinc-region}
\end{table}

\paragraph{The excluded inputs.}
% gadget_checks.py, block "excluded cases (toy curve)":
% Q = -P = (12, 54): x_r constraint = 28 = -(2 y_p)^2 = 28 for any (x_r,
%   y_r): unsatisfiable
% Q = P: both constraints vanish for every (x_r, y_r) in F_79^2: True (the
%   output is unconstrained)
If $Q = P$, both brackets vanish identically, since every term carries a
factor $x_p - x_q$ or $y_p - y_q$, and $(x_r, y_r)$ is unconstrained. If
$Q = -P \ne \mathcal O$, the bracket of~\eqref{eq:halo2-addinc-xr} reduces
to $-(2y_p)^2$, which is nonzero because Pallas, of prime order, has no
point of order two, and no assignment satisfies the gate. The Action
circuit applies this gate only to non-identity inputs with
$x_p \ne x_q$: it sums the window multiples of fixed-base scalar
multiplication for every window but the last, an offset in the window
table excluding the identity and the doubling case there, and adds the
last window, whose sum can be the identity or a doubling, with the
complete-addition gate (the halo2 book's \emph{Fixed-base scalar
multiplication} chapter).

\subsubsection{Lookup arguments of the Action circuit}
\label{sec:halo2-lookups}

A gate asserts only polynomial identities. ``This value has ten bits'' and
``this point is the $j$-th Sinsemilla generator'' are set memberships, and
\S\ref{sec:arith-lookups} showed why no low-degree gate on the cell alone states
them; the circuit states them as lookup arguments. Each is one instance of
Construction~\ref{con:arith-lookup}, the input expressions playing $A_0, \dots,
A_{m-1}$ and the table columns $S_0, \dots, S_{m-1}$, compressed by the
challenge $\theta$ as in~\eqref{eq:arith-theta} whenever $m > 1$.

\paragraph{The table.}
% pinned circuit description of the deployed Action circuit: three lookup
%   arguments
The Action circuit has three lookup arguments, one range check and two
Sinsemilla generator lookups, and all three use one table. It holds, for
$0 \le j < 2^{10}$,
\[
\text{row } j:\quad \bigl(j,\; X(S[j]),\; Y(S[j])\bigr), \qquad
S[j] := \GroupHash\bigl(\texttt{"z.cash:SinsemillaS"},\,
  \mathrm{LE}_{32}(j)\bigr),
\]
in three table columns $(t_{\mathrm{idx}}, t_x, t_y)$, where
$\mathrm{LE}_{\ell}(v)$ is the $\ell$-bit little-endian encoding of the
integer $v$, the generator table $S$ is defined by the protocol
specification
(\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#concretesinsemillahash}%
{\S~5.4.1.9}, ``Sinsemilla Hash Function''), and the hash $\GroupHash$
that derives it is the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s (\S``Sinsemilla: an
algebraic hash-based commitment''; the hash-to-curve pipeline is
\S``Hashing to a curve point'').
% pallas_constants.py, Sinsemilla generator table rows
%   S[j] = GroupHash("z.cash:SinsemillaS", LE_32(j)):
% j = 0: X = 0x0db5218be6881f0f1431d4ea7d4afc7b29a05bafbede62b55a91eb912044ea5f
%        Y = 0x2f0f40c2f152a01c9caf66298493d5d0944a041c2e65ba0117c24f76bf8e6483
% j = 1: X = 0x211112b4b3e19518c8fd33eb39175404e67700ca24649b8fce4ae33ea108af91
%        Y = 0x06c5993993adb03ba38a3e79cd5a35feb43c744a670e19bc1d83c293f810c5ee
%   (recomputed from the hash)
The first two rows are:
\begin{center}
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{l l}
\toprule
row & coordinates of $S[j]$, hexadecimal \\
\midrule
$j = 0$ & $X = \mathtt{0db5218b\,e6881f0f\,1431d4ea\,7d4afc7b\,
                        29a05baf\,bede62b5\,5a91eb91\,2044ea5f}$ \\
        & $Y = \mathtt{2f0f40c2\,f152a01c\,9caf6629\,8493d5d0\,
                        944a041c\,2e65ba01\,17c24f76\,bf8e6483}$ \\
$j = 1$ & $X = \mathtt{211112b4\,b3e19518\,c8fd33eb\,39175404\,
                        e67700ca\,24649b8f\,ce4ae33e\,a108af91}$ \\
        & $Y = \mathtt{06c59939\,93adb03b\,a38a3e79\,cd5a35fe\,
                        b43c744a\,670e19bc\,1d83c293\,f810c5ee}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Unused table rows.}
On every active row $j \ge 2^{10}$ (Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows}), each
of $t_{\mathrm{idx}}$, $t_x$ and $t_y$ holds its row-$0$ value, so that
every active row of the table is a genuine entry, $(0, X(S[0]), Y(S[0]))$
or $(j, X(S[j]), Y(S[j]))$. Rows left at $0$ would add the tuple
$(0, 0, 0)$, which is no entry, since row~$0$ is the only entry with
index $0$ and $X(S[0]) \ne 0$, and which an input tuple could then
match.

\paragraph{The range check.}
The range-check lookup is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:halo2-lookup-range}
q_{\mathrm{lookup}} \cdot \Bigl(
  q_{\mathrm{running}} \cdot \bigl(z_i - 2^{10} z_{i+1}\bigr)
  + (1 - q_{\mathrm{running}}) \cdot z_i \Bigr)
\;\longmapsto\; t_{\mathrm{idx}},
\end{equation}
with $z_i$ and $z_{i+1}$ the running-sum advice column at the current and
next rotations. With $q_{\mathrm{running}} = 1$ the input is a running-sum
step: the prover witnesses $z_{i+1} = (z_i - a_i) \cdot 2^{-10}$ on
successive rows, so the expression recovers the limb
$a_i = z_i - 2^{10} z_{i+1}$, and membership in
$t_{\mathrm{idx}} = \{0, \dots, 2^{10} - 1\}$ asserts that each limb has ten
bits. With $q_{\mathrm{running}} = 0$ the cell itself is looked up.
Every range check of the Action circuit is either an instance of this
lookup, a limb of fewer than ten bits being looked up together with its
shift to ten bits, or, for a range of $R \le 8$ values, the gate
$q \cdot w(w - 1)\cdots(w - (R - 1)) = 0$; the three-bit windows of
fixed-base scalar multiplication and the single bits use the gate (the
halo2 book's \emph{Decomposition} chapter).

\paragraph{The Sinsemilla generator lookup.}
The Sinsemilla lookup is the three-column instance
\begin{equation}\label{eq:halo2-lookup-sinsemilla}
\Bigl(\;
q_{S1} \cdot m_{i+1},\;\;
q_{S1} \cdot x_p + (1 - q_{S1}) \cdot X(S[0]),\;\;
q_{S1} \cdot y_p + (1 - q_{S1}) \cdot Y(S[0])
\;\Bigr)
\;\longmapsto\;
(t_{\mathrm{idx}},\, t_x,\, t_y),
\end{equation}
where $m_{i+1} = z_i - 2^{10} \cdot q_{\mathrm{run}} \cdot z_{i+1}$ recovers
the ten-bit message word from the hash's own running-sum decomposition of its
message, and $q_{\mathrm{run}} := q_{S2} - q_{S2}(q_{S2} - 1)
= q_{S2}(2 - q_{S2})$ is an expression in a fixed column
$q_{S2} \in \{0, 1, 2\}$ that evaluates to $1$ on the interior rows of a
message piece and to $0$ on its final row, where the trailing running sum
is implicitly zero. The coordinate $y_p$ is not a cell but a polynomial
expression in the queried cells equal to the $y$-coordinate of the
generator added (the halo2 book's \emph{Sinsemilla} chapter).

\paragraph{Expression-valued inputs.}
Lookup inputs may be arbitrary expressions over queried cells, not merely
cells. The Sinsemilla lookup asserts that the generator added is
$S[m_{i+1}]$ with no advice cell holding that generator's $y$-coordinate.
The $(1 - q_{S1})$ branches set the input tuple of every row with
$q_{S1} = 0$ to table row $0$, so the assertion of
Construction~\ref{con:arith-lookup}, that every active row's input tuple
appears in the table, holds with nothing to exempt.

\subsubsection{Column polynomials and the quotient}
\label{sec:halo2-handoff}

Every column, advice, fixed and instance, is a vector of $n = 2^{k}$
field elements, and its polynomial is its interpolant over the rows, of
degree $< n$: the column polynomials of \S\ref{sec:arith-plonkish}. The
trailing rows of each advice column hold uniformly random field
elements, the blinding rows of \S\ref{sec:halo2-zk}, on which every
selector is zero and which Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows} exempts from the
permutation and lookup arguments.

\paragraph{The quotient.}
Each gate polynomial is evaluated with the committed column polynomials
substituted for the queried cells, a query at rotation $r$ reading
$v(\omega^{r}X)$, and the resulting polynomials, together with the
permutation identities~\eqref{eq:arith-perm-rules} and the lookup
identities~\eqref{eq:arith-lookup-rules}, are folded with powers of a
challenge $y$ and divided by $\ZH(X) = X^{n} - 1$. The result is the
quotient $h(X)$ of phase~4 of ``The complete Halo 2 protocol''
(\S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol}), where the folding and the division are
written out (the halo2 book's \emph{Vanishing argument} chapter).

\paragraph{The running example.}
% toy_trace.py: G(X) = 36 + 27X + ... + 46X^9, deg G = 9; G on H = [0, 0, 0,
%   0]; t(X) = G / Z_H = 61 + 70X + 43X^2 + 47X^3 + 81X^4 + 46X^5, deg t = 5
The running example of \S\ref{sec:arith-polys} stands at the same point:
it interpolated a four-row trace into $a, b, c$, the five selector
polynomials and $\mathrm{PI}$, and assembled the one polynomial $G(X)$ of
degree $9$ whose divisibility by $\ZH = X^{4} - 1$, with the quotient $t$
of degree $5$, the rest of the protocol certifies.

\paragraph{The deployed parameters.}
% protocol_params.py: D = 9, n = 2^11 = 2048: quotient chunks D - 1 = 8,
%   each of degree < n
For the Action circuit $k = 11$, $n = 2^{11} = 2048$ and $d_{\max} = 9$,
the bound holding for its gates and for its permutation and lookup
identities. The quotient therefore splits into $d_{\max} - 1 = 8$ chunks
of degree below $n$, derived in \S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol}. The two
arguments this construction leans on are the ones \S\ref{sec:arith}
proved: the permutation argument of \S\ref{sec:arith-perm} stands behind
every copy constraint, and the lookup argument of
\S\ref{sec:arith-lookup-arg} behind the
lookups~\eqref{eq:halo2-lookup-range}
and~\eqref{eq:halo2-lookup-sinsemilla}.

\subsection{Instance columns and binding the public statement}
\label{sec:halo2-instance}

Definition~\ref{def:plonkish} lists instance columns beside fixed and
advice columns, and the arguments of \S\ref{sec:arith} treat them like
any other column. This subsection states how the instance column binds a
proof to its public statement.

\paragraph{The public input of the running example.}
% toy_trace.py: row 3 (omega^3 = 75): ... PI = -35
% PI  (X) = 64 + 50X + 33X^2 + 47X^3;  on H: [0, 0, 0, 62]
In the running example the target $35$ is public, so it is not advice but
\emph{instance} data: it sits in the public-input polynomial
$\mathrm{PI}(X)$ of~\eqref{eq:arith-Gpoly}, which the verifier builds
himself from the column $(0, 0, 0, -35)$; its interpolant
$64 + 50X + 33X^2 + 47X^3$ of \S\ref{sec:arith-polys} takes the value
$-35 = 62$ on row $3$ and $0$ elsewhere. A prover who claimed that
$X^3 + X + 5 = 36$ has a root would face a \emph{different} $G$, one with $-36$
in place of $-35$, and her honest trace would no longer vanish on the
rows: the fourth gate would read $30 + 5 - 36 \ne 0$. The public input
enters the polynomial $G$ whose divisibility
Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-random} tests, and an accepted proof attests the
statement for the verifier's public values.

\begin{definition}[Instance polynomial]\label{def:halo2-instance-poly}
An \emph{instance column} is a public-input column: its $n$ entries
$\boldsymbol\pi = (\pi_0, \dots, \pi_{n-1}) \in \F^{n}$ are not part of
the witness, and both parties agree on them before verification. Like
every other column it is interpolated over the rows in the Lagrange basis,
\[
\pi(X) := \sum_{j=0}^{n-1} \pi_j\,\ell_j(X) \in \F[X], \qquad
\deg \pi < n,
\]
with $\ell_j(\omega^{j}) = 1$ and $\ell_j(\omega^{j'}) = 0$ for $j' \ne j$
(\S\ref{sec:arith-plonkish}; the \emph{Math Guide}, \S``The Lagrange basis
on $H$''). The public values occupy designated rows, and every other
$\pi_j$ is $0$.
\end{definition}

\paragraph{Binding by copy constraints.}
The deployed circuit binds the public values through the permutation
argument of \S\ref{sec:arith-perm}, not through a gate. The instance
column is equality-enabled, so it participates in the permutation
$\sigma$; it is one of the fifteen columns counted under ``The deployed
chunking'' there, beside the ten advice and four fixed ones. Each
designated instance cell is copy-constrained to an advice cell that the
circuit's constraints read (the halo2 book's \emph{Permutation argument}
chapter for the mechanism), so a satisfying assignment exists only when
those advice cells hold the public values the verifier supplied
(Figure~\ref{fig:halo2-instance}). The alternative, a dedicated gate
$q(X)\bigl(a(X) - \pi(X)\bigr) \equiv 0 \pmod{\ZH(X)}$ with a selector
$q$ active exactly on the designated rows, is not used: the copy
constraint reuses the permutation argument, at no extra gate degree.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small, x=1.15cm, y=-0.62cm,
  cell/.style={draw, minimum width=1.05cm, minimum height=0.5cm,
    inner sep=1pt, font=\scriptsize},
  icell/.style={cell, minimum width=2.3cm, fill=tierrecv!12},
  cp/.style={{Latex[length=1.6mm]}-{Latex[length=1.6mm]}, tierrecv,
    line width=0.9pt},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]}, line width=0.7pt, black!65}]
  \node[tierrecv!80!black] at (0,-1.1) {instance $\boldsymbol\pi$};
  \node[tierin!80!black] at (3.2,-1.1) {advice columns};
  \foreach \r/\lab in {0/{$\mathrm{rt}$},
      1/{$x(\cvsym^{\mathrm{net}})$}, 2/{$y(\cvsym^{\mathrm{net}})$},
      3/{$\nfsym$}, 4/{$x(\rk)$}, 5/{$y(\rk)$}, 6/{$\cmx$},
      7/{\tiny$\mathsf{enableSpends}$},
      8/{\tiny$\mathsf{enableOutputs}$}, 9/{}} {
    \node[icell] (I\r) at (0,\r) {\lab};
  }
  \node at (0,10.4) {$\vdots$};
  \node[icell] (Ilast) at (0,11.3) {$0$};
  \foreach \c in {2.2,3.2,4.2} {
    \foreach \r in {0,...,9} { \node[cell, fill=tierin!10] at (\c,\r) {}; }
    \node at (\c,10.4) {$\vdots$};
    \node[cell, fill=tierin!10] at (\c,11.3) {};
  }
  \node[cell, fill=tierin!25] (A0) at (3.2,2) {$\mathrm{rt}$};
  \node[cell, fill=tierin!25] (R0) at (4.2,2) {root};
  \node[font=\scriptsize, anchor=west] at (4.7,2)
    {$v^{\mathrm{old}} \cdot (\mathrm{root} - \mathrm{rt}) = 0$};
  \node[cell, fill=tierin!25] (A1) at (4.2,5) {$\nfsym$};
  \node[cell, fill=tierin!25] (A2) at (2.2,7) {$\cmx$};
  \draw[cp] (I0.east) -- (A0.west);
  \draw[cp] (I3.east) -- (A1.west);
  \draw[cp] (I6.east) -- (A2.west);
  \node[draw=tierrecv, rounded corners=2pt, fill=tierrecv!6, align=flush left,
    text width=4.4cm, inner sep=4pt] (comm) at (8.99,1.0)
    {\textbf{verifier's own commitment}\\
     $\Comm(\pi;\,1)$ from the public\\values, absorbed before\\any
     challenge};
  \node[draw=tierin, rounded corners=2pt, fill=tierin!6, align=flush left,
    text width=4.4cm, inner sep=4pt] (open) at (8.99,8.0)
    {\textbf{opened like any column}\\
     $\pi(x)$ sent in phase 5, bound\\to the verifier's commitment\\in
     phase 6, entering the\\permutation identities};
  \draw[arr] (comm) -- (open);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The instance column of the Action circuit. Rows $0$ to $8$ hold
the primary input in the encoding of the protocol specification
(\S~4.18.4), the curve points $\cvsym^{\mathrm{net}}$ and $\rk$ as two
coordinates each; the further input $\mathsf{disableCrossAddress}$ of
\S~4.6 occupies a row that no normative text fixes, drawn empty; every
other row is $0$. Each
designated cell is copy-constrained to an advice cell the circuit's
constraints read; three such constraints are drawn (green double
arrows): $\nfsym$ and $\cmx$ are copied to the cells where the circuit
computes them, $\mathrm{rt}$ to a cell that a gate ties to the computed
Merkle root. The verifier commits to the column himself, with the fixed
blinding factor $1$, and absorbs that commitment before the first
challenge.}
\label{fig:halo2-instance}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The instance commitment.}
The verifier does not take $\pi(X)$ from the prover. \emph{Both} parties
compute the commitment to each instance polynomial from the instance
values (the halo2 book's \emph{Circuit commitments} chapter), with the
public blinding factor $1$ in place of a random one so that the two
computations agree, and absorb it into the transcript before the first
challenge is drawn, in phase~1 of \S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol}; every
Fiat--Shamir challenge thereby depends on the public input. The book
fixes the factor $1$ for the fixed columns only; for the instance columns
it is the choice of the implementation, \texttt{halo2\_proofs}.
Thereafter the instance column is treated like any other: its claimed
evaluation $\pi(x)$ appears among the phase-5 evaluations, enters the
permutation identities as one of the $v_i$ of
Construction~\ref{con:arith-perm}, and the multipoint opening of phase~6
binds it to the instance commitment. Because the verifier computed that
commitment himself, from the instance values he verifies against, the
prover has no freedom over $\pi$: an evaluation inconsistent with the
verifier's own values fails the opening argument, by the evaluation
binding recalled in \S\ref{sec:arith-commit}.

\paragraph{The public input of the Action statement.}
The Action statement's public input is the tuple
$(\mathrm{rt},\allowbreak \cvsym^{\mathrm{net}},\allowbreak \nfsym,\allowbreak
\rk,\allowbreak \cmx,\allowbreak \mathsf{enableSpends},\allowbreak
\mathsf{enableOutputs})$ (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actionstatement}{\S~4.18.4},
``Action Statement (Orchard)''): the anchor $\mathrm{rt}$, the root of the
note-commitment tree the spent note is proved against (the \emph{Crypto
Guide}, \S``Append-only and incrementally updatable trees''); the net
value commitment $\cvsym^{\mathrm{net}}$; the nullifier $\nfsym$; the
randomised verification key $\rk$; the extracted commitment
$\cmx = \Extract_{\PP}(\cmsym)$ of the output note (the \emph{Crypto
Guide}, \S``A shielded spend, end to end''); and the flags
$\mathsf{enableSpends}$ and $\mathsf{enableOutputs}$, which permit
non-zero-valued spends and outputs. The primary-input note of the same
section encodes the tuple as the sequence
\[
\bigl[\mathrm{rt},\, x(\cvsym^{\mathrm{net}}),\,
  y(\cvsym^{\mathrm{net}}),\, \nfsym,\, x(\rk),\, y(\rk),\, \cmx,\,
  \mathsf{enableSpends},\, \mathsf{enableOutputs}\bigr] \in \F^{9},
\]
each integer read as an element of $\F = \F_{p_{\Pallas}}$. The Action
description specifies the proof with an eighth component,
$\mathsf{disableCrossAddress} := 1 - \mathsf{enableCrossAddress}$, where
$\mathsf{enableCrossAddress}$ permits the output note's receiver to
differ from the input note's (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actiondesc}{\S~4.6},
``Action Descriptions''); from NU6.3 the proof of every Action of either
pool is verified with this further public input, which is $1$ for every
Orchard-pool Action (\href{https://zips.z.cash/zip-0258}{ZIP~258}).
Rows~$0$ to~$8$ of the instance column hold the nine values above; no
normative text fixes the row of the further input, and every other row is
$0$ (Figure~\ref{fig:halo2-instance}). Each is copy-constrained to an advice
cell the circuit's constraints read: $\cvsym^{\mathrm{net}}$, $\nfsym$,
$\rk$ and $\cmx$ to the cells where the circuit computes them;
$\mathrm{rt}$ to a cell tied to the computed Merkle root by the gate
$v^{\mathrm{old}}(\mathrm{root} - \mathrm{rt}) = 0$, which encodes the
statement's condition that $v^{\mathrm{old}} = 0$ or the Merkle path is
valid; the two enable flags to cells read by the gates
$v^{\mathrm{old}}(1 - \mathsf{enableSpends}) = 0$ and
$v^{\mathrm{new}}(1 - \mathsf{enableOutputs}) = 0$, which encode, for
flags in $\{0, 1\}$, the conditions $v^{\mathrm{old}} = 0$ or
$\mathsf{enableSpends} = 1$, and $v^{\mathrm{new}} = 0$ or
$\mathsf{enableOutputs} = 1$; and $\mathsf{disableCrossAddress}$ to a cell
read by a gate that, when it is nonzero, equates the coordinates of the
receivers $(\gd, \pkd)$ of the two notes. That condition is the meaning
\S~4.6 gives the flag; the list of conditions in \S~4.18.4 does not yet
include it.

\paragraph{Binding to the instance.}
The verifier computes the instance commitment from the public values
$\boldsymbol\pi$ itself, and the copy constraints tie the designated
cells to the advice cells the constraints read; so a witness extracted
from an accepting proof satisfies the relation at $\boldsymbol\pi$, not at
other public values $\boldsymbol\pi'$ the prover may target. The
probabilistic conclusion, that a prover who knows no witness for
$\boldsymbol\pi$, whatever witnesses she holds for other instances,
convinces the verifier with at most negligible probability, holds under
the knowledge soundness whose status \S\ref{sec:ks} classifies:
designed-but-unspecified for the deployed transcript, resting on the
discrete logarithm on Vesta or on the algebraic group model, with the
transcript hash modelled as a random oracle. Under that hypothesis an
Action proof that verifies against an anchor, a nullifier and a value
commitment certifies knowledge of a witness consistent with those
values.

\subsection{Removing the verifier: the Fiat--Shamir transform}
\label{sec:halo2-fs}

The protocols of \S\S\ref{sec:arith}--\ref{sec:ipa} are public-coin:
every verifier message is uniformly random and independent of the
prover's messages, a field element (the point $z$ of the random-point
check, the pair $\beta, \gamma$ of the permutation and lookup arguments,
the challenges $u_j$ of each opening round) or, in
Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit}, the group element $U$; and the
verifier's decision is a deterministic function of the
public input and the transcript. Their soundness requires only that each
challenge be drawn after the commitments it tests are fixed.

\paragraph{The transform.}
The Fiat--Shamir transform replaces the $i$-th challenge $c_i$ by
\[
c_i := H\bigl(\mathsf{x}, m_1, c_1, \dots, c_{i-1}, m_i\bigr),
\]
the hash of the public statement $\mathsf{x}$ and of the transcript
prefix, the prover's messages $m_1, \dots, m_i$ and the earlier
challenges, under a fixed hash function $H$ (the \emph{Crypto Guide},
\S``The Fiat--Shamir transform: from interactive to non-interactive'').
The ordering of \S\ref{sec:arith-commit} is enforced because each
commitment is an input to the hash that derives the challenge testing
it. The toy of \S\ref{sec:arith-random} drew $z = 20$ as an interactive
challenge; the deployed prover derives every challenge from a hash. The
two kinds of challenge are kept apart in what follows: the interactive
one is uniform by assumption, the hashed one only in the model in which
the hash is a random oracle.

\paragraph{Grinding.}
A prover against the compiled argument may vary the inputs of $H$ and
inspect the resulting challenges. The bound on that freedom uses
round-by-round soundness (Canetti, Chen, Holmgren, Lombardi, Rothblum,
Rothblum and Wichs, \emph{Fiat--Shamir: From Practice to Theory}), which
Remark~\ref{rem:halo2-compile} recalls.

\begin{proposition}[Fiat--Shamir of a round-by-round sound protocol]
\label{prop:halo2-grinding}
Let $\Pi$ be a public-coin interactive protocol whose partial
transcripts for a false statement carry a \emph{doomed} labelling with
error $\varepsilon$: the empty transcript is doomed, a doomed complete
transcript is rejected, and from a doomed partial transcript, whatever
the prover's next message, the next challenge leaves it doomed except
with probability at most $\varepsilon$. With $H$ modelled as a random
oracle, a prover making at most $Q$ distinct oracle queries, the
verifier's recomputations counted among them, makes the verifier of the
Fiat--Shamir transform of $\Pi$ accept with probability at most
$Q\varepsilon$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Each query is a transcript prefix followed by a prover message, and its
answer is uniform and independent of everything before the query. The
verifier accepts only a transcript that is not doomed, and the empty
transcript is doomed, so acceptance requires some query on a doomed
prefix whose answer leaves the doomed set; each query does so with
probability at most $\varepsilon$, and the union bound over the at most
$Q$ queries gives $Q\varepsilon$.
\end{proof}

The proposition has the form of the halo2 book's extraction theorem
(\emph{Protocol Description} chapter, ``Witness-extended Emulation'', its
term $q\epsilon$) and of the state-restoration analysis of Ghoshal and
Tessaro that \S\ref{sec:ks} cites, where its application to the
deployed transcript is classified.
% toy_trace.py, block "grinding bound Q * epsilon":
% toy: epsilon = 11/97, Q = 1: Q epsilon = 0.1134
% toy: epsilon = 11/97, Q = 10: Q epsilon = 1.0000
% toy: epsilon = 11/97, Q = 100: Q epsilon = 1.0000
% Orchard scale: epsilon = 2^-240, Q = 2^40: Q epsilon = 2^-200
% Orchard scale: epsilon = 2^-240, Q = 2^64: Q epsilon = 2^-176
% Orchard scale: epsilon = 2^-240, Q = 2^80: Q epsilon = 2^-160
% Orchard scale: permutation term 3N/p with N = 15 * 2042 = 30630: 2^(-237.5) > identity check 18431/p = 2^(-239.8)
For the toy's identity check, a single challenge with error $11/97$
(\S\ref{sec:arith-random}), ten queries exhaust the bound. This volume
establishes no round-by-round error for Halo~2, whose binding is computational.
At Orchard scale the identity check's term is about $2^{-240}$ (one
Schwartz--Zippel term, in the sense \S\ref{sec:intro-claim} fixed), and budgets
of $2^{40}$, $2^{64}$ and $2^{80}$ queries multiply that term alone to
$2^{-200}$, $2^{-176}$ and $2^{-160}$; these agree with the
$Q\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bad}}$ of the halo2 book's algebraic-group-model bound
quoted in \S\ref{sec:ks-agm}, proved for the abstract protocol, whose transfer
to the deployed transcript \S\ref{sec:ks} classifies. The permutation argument's
term over $(\beta, \gamma)$, $3N/|\F|$ with $N = 15 \cdot 2042$ participating
cells (Corollary~\ref{cor:arith-perm-deployed}), is larger, about $2^{-237.5}$.

The hash must take the whole transcript prefix as input: a statement or
commitment left out can be chosen after the challenge is seen, the
``weak Fiat--Shamir'' failure the \emph{Crypto Guide} records in
\S``The Fiat--Shamir transform: from interactive to non-interactive''.
The bound holds
with $H$ modelled as a random oracle, a heuristic. The \emph{Crypto
Guide} proves the one-round theorem, with its forking-lemma extractor
and its programmed-oracle simulator, and states that a multi-round
protocol such as this one needs a multi-round theorem whose hypotheses
match the protocol at hand; \S\ref{sec:ks} weighs that theorem against
the deployed transcript.

\paragraph{Non-interactivity.}
The compiled argument is non-interactive: the proof is one static
object, commitments, evaluations and the opening's scalars, and
verification recomputes the challenges by the same hash and runs the
same checks. The protocol specification defines a Halo~2 proof as a
byte sequence (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#halo2}{\S~5.4.10.3},
``Halo 2'', and its ``Encoding of Halo 2 Proofs''), so a full node
verifies an Action proof from the transaction alone.

\paragraph{The deployed transcript.}
Neither the protocol specification nor the halo2 book fixes the
transcript that instantiates the transform; it is that of the
implementation, \texttt{halo2\_proofs}, and is as follows. The state is
a BLAKE2b-512 hash state with the sixteen-byte personalisation
\texttt{Halo2-Transcript} (the \emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Domain
separation and personalisation''). Absorbing a point
$P = (x, y) \ne \mathcal O$ of Vesta appends the bytes
$\mathtt{0x01} \,\|\, \mathrm{LE}_{256}(x) \,\|\, \mathrm{LE}_{256}(y)$;
the identity is not absorbable. Absorbing a scalar $s \in \F$ appends
$\mathtt{0x02} \,\|\, \mathrm{LE}_{256}(s)$. Squeezing a challenge
appends $\mathtt{0x00}$ to the state, which keeps it, and returns the
$64$-byte digest of the state read as a little-endian integer and
reduced modulo $p_{\Pallas}$, with no rejection of any value
(\S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}). The proof carries each point as its $32$-byte
compressed encoding, the $x$-coordinate with the parity of $y$ in the
top bit (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#pallasandvesta}{\S~5.4.9.6},
``Pallas and Vesta''), and each scalar as $\mathrm{LE}_{256}(s)$. Before
any prover message both parties absorb, as a scalar, a digest of the
verifying key, its BLAKE2b hash reduced into $\F$, and then the
instance commitments of \S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}, which therefore
never appear in the proof. Every prover message is then
absorbed in the order the protocol fixes, and each challenge is squeezed
at its prescribed point, after everything it must depend on. The proof
is the sequence of the prover's messages in the phase order of
Table~\ref{tab:halo2-phases}, counted by kind in
Table~\ref{tab:proof-bytes}; the verifier replays the absorptions and
recomputes every challenge. Figure~\ref{fig:halo2-phases} in
\S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol} draws the timeline.

\subsection{The multipoint opening argument}
\label{sec:halo2-multipoint}

% halo2_counts.py: claims = 49 a + 45 sent evaluations + 1 quotient value
%   = 49 a + 46; a = 1: 95; committed polynomials opened = 23 a + 46;
%   a = 1: 69; separate openings would cost 2k + 1 = 23 points per claim
When the verifier has drawn his evaluation point $x$, the prover has
committed to several dozen polynomials and claimed their values at $x$
and at rotations of $x$: a running product at $x$ and $\omega x$, a
permuted lookup input at $x$ and $\omega^{-1}x$, an advice column at
every rotation its gates read. Opening each claim with the argument of
\S\ref{sec:ipa} would cost $2k + 1 = 23$ points per claim, and a
one-Action proof makes $95$ claims about $69$ committed polynomials, the
$94$ evaluations at $x$ and its rotations in Table~\ref{tab:proof-bytes}
and the quotient's ($49a + 46$ claims for $a$ Actions). Halo~2 reduces
all of them to a single opening of a single polynomial at a single
point. The reduction
combines random linear combinations, division by vanishing polynomials
and one random-point check. The letters $q$ and $f$ below follow the
halo2 book's \emph{Multipoint opening argument} chapter and are unrelated
to the selector polynomials $q_\bullet$ of \S\ref{sec:arith}, which are
among the polynomials being folded; nor is this $f$ the final blinder $f$
of the deployed opening (\S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}), which phase~7 sends. The
book's \emph{Protocol
Description} chapter writes the values $q_i(x_3)$ as $u$, the letter this
volume reserves for the round challenges of the inner-product argument;
they are written $\bar q_i$ here. The index $i$ ranges over sets of query
points, not over columns.

\begin{construction}[Multipoint opening]\label{con:halo2-multipoint}
Let $p_1, \dots, p_N$ be polynomials of degree $< n$ with commitments
$C_1, \dots, C_N$ under Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit}, each $p_\nu$
queried at a finite set $T_\nu \subset \F$ of points, and let
$\hat p_\nu(t)$ for $t \in T_\nu$ be the values the prover has claimed.
Let $T^{(1)}, \dots, T^{(m)}$ be the distinct sets among the $T_\nu$ and
$I_i := \{\nu : T_\nu = T^{(i)}\}$ the $i$-th group, enumerated as
$\nu_{i,0}, \nu_{i,1}, \dots$; write
$Z_i(X) := \prod_{t \in T^{(i)}}(X - t)$.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Fold each group ($x_1$).} The verifier sends $x_1 \in \F$.
  For each group $i$ the prover forms
  $q_i(X) := \sum_{e} x_1^{\,e}\,p_{\nu_{i,e}}(X)$, the verifier forms the
  same combination of commitments, $Q_i := \sum_e [x_1^{e}]\,C_{\nu_{i,e}}$,
  which commits to $q_i$ by linearity (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Pedersen
  vector commitments''), and both form the combined claimed values
  $\hat q_i(t) := \sum_e x_1^{e}\,\hat p_{\nu_{i,e}}(t)$ for
  $t \in T^{(i)}$. Both interpolate $r_i(X)$, the unique polynomial of
  degree $< |T^{(i)}|$ with $r_i(t) = \hat q_i(t)$ on $T^{(i)}$ (the
  \emph{Math Guide}, \S``Lagrange interpolation''); the verifier can do
  this from the claimed values alone.
\item \textbf{Divide out the points ($x_2$).} The verifier sends
  $x_2 \in \F$. The prover forms the quotients
  $f_i(X) := \bigl(q_i(X) - r_i(X)\bigr)/Z_i(X)$, which are polynomials
  exactly when the combined values $\hat q_i(t)$ equal $q_i(t)$ for every
  $t \in T^{(i)}$, in particular when every claimed value is true, and
  combines them into
  \[
  f(X) := \sum_{i=1}^{m} x_2^{\,i-1}\,f_i(X),
  \]
  samples a fresh blinder $r_f$ and sends $F := \Comm(f;\, r_f)$.
\item \textbf{A fresh point ($x_3$).} The verifier sends $x_3 \in \F$. The
  prover sends $\bar q_i := q_i(x_3)$ for $i = 1, \dots, m$.
\item \textbf{The value $f$ must take.} The verifier computes
  \[
  v_f := \sum_{i=1}^{m} x_2^{\,i-1}\,\frac{\bar q_i - r_i(x_3)}{Z_i(x_3)},
  \]
  the value of $f(x_3)$ if every $f_i$ is a polynomial and every
  $\bar q_i$ is honest; he can, since $r_i$ and $Z_i$ are his to evaluate,
  and he rejects if some $Z_i(x_3)$ is $0$.
\item \textbf{One polynomial ($x_4$).} The verifier sends $x_4 \in \F$.
  Both parties form
  \[
  f^{*}(X) := f(X) + \sum_{i=1}^{m} x_4^{\,i}\,q_i(X),
  \qquad
  F^{*} := F + \sum_{i=1}^{m} [x_4^{\,i}]\,Q_i,
  \qquad
  v^{*} := v_f + \sum_{i=1}^{m} x_4^{\,i}\,\bar q_i,
  \]
  and run one opening of Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit} on the
  statement $(F^{*}, x_3, v^{*})$, the prover's witness being the
  coefficient vector of $f^{*}$ and the blinder $r_f + \sum_i x_4^{i}\,
  r_{Q_i}$, where $r_{Q_i}$ is the blinder of $Q_i$, the $x_1$-combination
  of the blinders of the polynomials in group $i$. The verifier accepts
  the original claims if and only if that opening accepts.
\end{enumerate}
\end{construction}

Two remarks on the shape. Nothing is sent between $x_1$ and $x_2$, so the
deployed transcript squeezes them back to back; they are two challenges
because they play two roles, one keeping the polynomials of a group
linearly independent and one keeping the quotients of different groups
independent. And the reduction is free for the verifier in group
operations except for the combinations of commitments, which are a few
scalar multiplications per committed polynomial; the one expensive step,
the final multi-scalar multiplication, is paid once, inside the single
opening. Figure~\ref{fig:halo2-multipoint} draws the data flow.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small,
  grp/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, inner sep=3pt, align=flush center,
    fill=tierin!8, draw=tierin, minimum height=7mm},
  stg/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, inner sep=3pt, align=flush center,
    fill=tierrecv!10, draw=tierrecv, minimum height=7mm},
  chal/.style={draw, circle, inner sep=1.5pt, fill=tierfull!15,
    draw=tierfull, font=\small},
  fin/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, inner sep=3pt, align=flush center,
    fill=tierspend!8, draw=tierspend, minimum height=7mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]}, line width=0.7pt, black!65}]
  \node[grp, text width=2.5cm] (g1) at (0,2.0)
    {group $1$: queried\\at $T^{(1)} = \{x\}$};
  \node[grp, text width=2.5cm] (g2) at (0,0.6)
    {group $2$: queried\\at $T^{(2)} = \{x, \omega x\}$};
  \node at (0,-0.5) {$\vdots$};
  \node[grp, text width=2.5cm] (gm) at (0,-1.6)
    {group $m$: queried\\at $T^{(m)}$};
  \node[chal] (x1) at (2.35,0.2) {$x_1$};
  \node[stg, text width=2.1cm] (q) at (4.2,0.2)
    {$q_i$, $Q_i$, $r_i$\\one per group};
  \node[chal] (x2) at (6.05,0.2) {$x_2$};
  \node[stg, text width=2.2cm] (f) at (7.9,0.2)
    {$f_i = \dfrac{q_i - r_i}{Z_i}$\\[3pt]$f = \sum x_2^{i-1} f_i$\\[2pt]%
     {\scriptsize send $F$}};
  \node[chal] (x3) at (9.75,0.2) {$x_3$};
  \node[stg, text width=1.9cm] (u) at (11.5,0.2)
    {$\bar q_i = q_i(x_3)$\\$v_f$ computed};
  \node[chal] (x4) at (11.5,-1.5) {$x_4$};
  \node[fin, text width=5.2cm] (fin) at (7.9,-1.9)
    {$f^{*} = f + \sum_i x_4^{i} q_i$, opened once at $x_3$\\
     with value $v^{*}$ by the argument of \S\ref{sec:ipa}};
  \draw[arr] (g1.east) -- (x1);
  \draw[arr] (g2.east) -- (x1);
  \draw[arr] (gm.east) -- (x1);
  \draw[arr] (x1) -- (q);
  \draw[arr] (q) -- (x2);
  \draw[arr] (x2) -- (f);
  \draw[arr] (f) -- (x3);
  \draw[arr] (x3) -- (u);
  \draw[arr] (u) -- (x4);
  \draw[arr] (x4) -- (fin);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The multipoint reduction of Construction~\ref{con:halo2-multipoint}.
Committed polynomials are grouped by the set of points at which they are
queried (blue), folded within each group by $x_1$ into $q_i$, quotiented
by the group's vanishing factor into $f_i$, combined across groups by
$x_2$ into $f$, which is committed as $F$; after the fresh point $x_3$
the prover sends the $q_i(x_3)$, and $x_4$ folds $f$ and the $q_i$ into
$f^{*}$ (red), whose single opening at $x_3$ discharges every original
claim. Challenges are amber.}
\label{fig:halo2-multipoint}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Completeness.}
If every claimed value is true, then $q_i - r_i$ vanishes on $T^{(i)}$
and is divisible by $Z_i$ (the \emph{Math Guide}, \S``Roots and the
factor theorem''), each $f_i$ is a polynomial of degree $< n$, so is $f$,
and $f(x_3) = v_f$ whenever no $Z_i(x_3)$ is $0$; $f^{*}(x_3) = v^{*}$
then holds, and the honest opening of \S\ref{sec:ipa} accepts. The
verifier's rejection when $x_3$ is one of the query points, an event of
probability at most $|\bigcup_i T^{(i)}|/|\F|$, is the reduction's only
completeness defect.

\begin{lemma}[Soundness of the multipoint reduction]
\label{lem:halo2-multipoint}
In Construction~\ref{con:halo2-multipoint}, let the committed
polynomials $p_\nu$ and the claimed values be fixed before $x_1$ is drawn,
let $\hat f$ be a polynomial of degree $< n$ committed in $F$, fixed
before $x_3$ is drawn, let $x_1, \dots, x_4$ be independent and uniform
in $\F$, and let
$Z(X) := \prod_{t \in \bigcup_i T^{(i)}}(X - t)$. If some claimed value is
false, $\hat p_{\nu_0}(t_0) \ne p_{\nu_0}(t_0)$ with $\nu_0$ in group
$i_0$, then the probability that the verifier computes $v_f$ and the
polynomial $\hat f + \sum_i x_4^{i} q_i$ committed in $F^{*}$ takes the
value $v^{*}$ at $x_3$ is at most
\[
\frac{|I_{i_0}| + 2m + n - 1 + \deg Z}{|\F|}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The argument takes one Schwartz--Zippel term per challenge (the
\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The Schwartz--Zippel lemma'').
\emph{The fold.} The difference $\hat q_{i_0}(t_0) - q_{i_0}(t_0) =
\sum_e x_1^{e}\bigl(\hat p_{\nu_{i_0,e}}(t_0) - p_{\nu_{i_0,e}}(t_0)\bigr)$
is a nonzero polynomial in $x_1$ of degree below $|I_{i_0}|$, fixed
before $x_1$ is drawn, so except with probability $|I_{i_0}|/|\F|$ the
folded claim is false: $r_{i_0}(t_0) \ne q_{i_0}(t_0)$.
\emph{The combination.} Each $T^{(i)}$ is contained in
$\bigcup_{i'} T^{(i')}$, so each $Z/Z_i$ is a polynomial. The polynomial
\[
M(X) := \hat f(X)\,Z(X) - \sum_{i=1}^{m} x_2^{\,i-1}
  \bigl(q_i(X) - r_i(X)\bigr)\,\frac{Z(X)}{Z_i(X)}
\]
is fixed once $x_2$ is drawn and $F$ is sent, before $x_3$. At $t_0$ the
term $\hat f(t_0)\,Z(t_0)$ and every term with $t_0 \notin T^{(i)}$
vanish, and
\[
M(t_0) = -\sum_{i \,:\, t_0 \in T^{(i)}} x_2^{\,i-1}
  \bigl(q_i(t_0) - r_i(t_0)\bigr) \prod_{t \notin T^{(i)}}(t_0 - t),
\]
the product over $t \in \bigcup_{i'} T^{(i')} \setminus T^{(i)}$: a
polynomial in $x_2$ of degree below $m$, fixed before $x_2$ is drawn,
whose coefficient at $x_2^{\,i_0 - 1}$ is nonzero. Except with
probability $m/|\F|$ over $x_2$, then, $M(t_0) \ne 0$ and $M$ is a
nonzero polynomial of degree at most $n - 1 + \deg Z$.
\emph{The last fold.} Acceptance means
$\bigl(\hat f(x_3) - v_f\bigr) + \sum_i x_4^{i}\bigl(q_i(x_3) -
\bar q_i\bigr) = 0$, a polynomial equation in $x_4$ of degree at most
$m$ whose coefficients are fixed before $x_4$ is drawn. Unless every
coefficient is zero, that is, unless $\hat f(x_3) = v_f$ and
$\bar q_i = q_i(x_3)$ for every $i$, a uniform $x_4$ satisfies it with
probability at most $m/|\F|$.
\emph{The fresh point.} If $\hat f(x_3) = v_f$ and every
$\bar q_i = q_i(x_3)$, then, since $v_f$ is computed only when
$Z(x_3) \ne 0$, multiplying the first equation by $Z(x_3)$ gives
$M(x_3) = 0$, which a uniform $x_3$ achieves for a nonzero $M$ with
probability at most $(n - 1 + \deg Z)/|\F|$. A union bound over the four
terms gives the stated bound.
\end{proof}

The bound is one more Schwartz--Zippel allowance, of the order of
$n/|\F|$. The opening's own knowledge error, $O(\log d/|\F|)$ by
Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks}, lies below it, and that corollary's
witness-or-relation form supplies the binding opening the lemma assumes.

\paragraph{The deployed instance.}
% proof_bytes.py: scalar 5 160 multipoint evaluations at x_3 (one per
%   distinct point set; 5 sets)
% pinned circuit description: advice query rotation sets {0, +1} (columns
%   0, 2, 3, 4, 5) and {-1, 0, +1} (columns 1, 6, 7, 8, 9); instance and
%   fixed queries at rotation 0
% protocol_params.py: permutation 'last' rotation -6
In the Action circuit the point sets are five. Every fixed, instance and
permuted-label polynomial, the random polynomial of phase~4 and the
collapsed quotient are queried at $\{x\}$; the advice columns, five at
$\{x, \omega x\}$ and five at $\{\omega^{-1}x, x, \omega x\}$, according
to the rotations their gates read; the permuted lookup input $A'$ at
$\{x, \omega^{-1}x\}$ and the permuted table $S'$ at $\{x\}$; the lookup
running products and the last permutation running product at
$\{x, \omega x\}$; and the first two permutation running products at
$\{x, \omega x, \omega^{-6}x\}$, the last rotation reaching the boundary
row of Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows} that chains one product into the
next. The sets $\{x\}$, $\{x, \omega x\}$, $\{x, \omega^{-1}x\}$,
$\{\omega^{-1}x, x, \omega x\}$ and $\{x, \omega x, \omega^{-6}x\}$ give
the five values $\bar q_i$, the multipoint evaluations of
Table~\ref{tab:proof-bytes}, and the whole reduction adds one point and
five scalars to the proof.

\subsection{The complete Halo 2 protocol}
\label{sec:halo2-protocol}

The pieces are now assembled into the Halo~2 protocol (the halo2 book's
\emph{Protocol Description} chapter, instantiated with the arguments of
its \emph{Proving system} chapters), in seven phases. Throughout, $\F$ is the
constraint field, $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$ for Orchard; $\G$ is the commitment
group, the Vesta curve, whose order is $p_{\Pallas}$
(\S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}); the rows are $\omega^{0}, \dots, \omega^{n-1}$
for a primitive $n$-th root of unity $\omega$, with $n = 2^{k}$; and every
commitment is $\Comm(p;\, r) = \ip{\mathbf p}{\mathbf G} + [r]H$ of
Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit}, $H$ the blinding generator. The
protocol has a preprocessed setup and seven phases: (1) advice
commitments; (2) lookup compression, challenge $\theta$; (3) running
products, challenges $\beta, \gamma$; (4) the quotient, challenge $y$;
(5) the evaluation challenge $x$; (6) the multipoint opening, challenges
$x_1, x_2, x_3, x_4$; (7) the inner-product opening. Every challenge is
squeezed from the transcript of \S\ref{sec:halo2-fs} at the point named;
``the verifier sends'' below means that. Table~\ref{tab:halo2-phases}
lists the messages with their Orchard counts and
Figure~\ref{fig:halo2-phases} draws the timeline.

\paragraph{Setup.}
The circuit fixes the columns, gates, lookup arguments and the equality
permutation $\sigma$, as \S\ref{sec:halo2-practice} described; its
selectors and lookup tables are fixed columns. Key generation
interpolates every fixed column and every permuted-label polynomial
$s_i(X)$ of Construction~\ref{con:arith-perm} and commits to them with
the fixed blinding factor $1$ (for the fixed columns, the halo2 book's
\emph{Circuit commitments} chapter); these commitments, with the circuit
description, form the verifying key. The transparent parameters of the
commitment scheme, the $n$ generators $\mathbf G$, the blinding
generator $H$ and the inner-product generator $U$ hashed to the curve as
\S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2} described, together with the domain data, are held
separately. All of it is reproducible from public algorithms and the
circuit; no secret setup trapdoor exists. Verification is a
deterministic function of the parameters, the verifying key, the
instance values and the proof.

\paragraph{Phase 1: advice commitments.}
Both parties first absorb the digest of the verifying key and the
instance commitments of \S\ref{sec:halo2-instance} into the
transcript, so that every subsequent challenge depends on the circuit and
on the public input. The prover constructs the advice columns
$\mathbf w_1, \dots, \mathbf w_{n_a} \in \F^{n}$, which with the fixed and
instance columns form a satisfying assignment in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:plonkish}, fills the reserved trailing rows of each
with uniformly random field elements, the blinding rows of
\S\ref{sec:halo2-zk}, interpolates each to $w_i(X)$ of degree $< n$, and
sends $\Comm(w_i;\, r_i)$ with fresh blinders $r_i$. The verifier
absorbs the commitments.

\paragraph{Phase 2: lookup compression, challenge $\theta$.}
The verifier sends $\theta$. For each lookup the prover compresses the
input expressions and table columns by powers of $\theta$
as in~\eqref{eq:arith-theta}, forms the permuted columns $A'$ and $S'$ of
Construction~\ref{con:arith-lookup}, and sends their two commitments; the
verifier absorbs them.

\paragraph{Phase 3: running products, challenges $\beta, \gamma$.}
The verifier sends $\beta$ and $\gamma$. The prover commits to the
permutation running products, one per chunk of $d_{\max} - 2$ columns as
``The deployed chunking'' in \S\ref{sec:arith-perm} described, and to one
lookup running product $Z_{\mathrm{lookup}}$ per lookup, and sends the
commitments; the verifier absorbs them. The order is not a convenience.
The permuted columns $A'$ and $S'$ must be fixed \emph{before} the
challenges that randomise the products are drawn, since
Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-lookup} bounds the prover's success probability
only over challenges drawn after her columns; a prover who saw
$\beta, \gamma$ first could choose the permutation against them.

\paragraph{Phase 4: the quotient, challenge $y$.}
Before $y$ is drawn the prover commits to a uniformly random polynomial
of degree $< n$, sends the commitment, and the verifier absorbs it; its
role is the zero-knowledge one explained in \S\ref{sec:halo2-zk}. The
verifier then sends $y$. The prover assembles the \emph{gate equation},
one polynomial
\[
C(X) := \sum_{t=0}^{T-1} y^{\,t}\, g_t(X),
\]
the $y$-weighted combination of the $T$ constraint polynomials $g_t$ of
the circuit: each constraint of each custom gate with its selector
factor, the permutation identities~\eqref{eq:arith-perm-rules} and the
chunk-boundary identities, the lookup
identities~\eqref{eq:arith-lookup-rules}, and the boundary identities of
Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows}, each masked by its active-row factor where
that remark requires. If every constraint holds then every $g_t$ vanishes
on the rows, so $C$ does, and $\ZH \mid C$ by~\eqref{eq:arith-divis}: the
\emph{quotient} $h(X) := C(X)/\ZH(X) = C(X)/(X^{n} - 1)$ is a polynomial.
If some constraint fails at some row, Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-y-batch}
bounds the probability that $C$ nonetheless vanishes on the rows, since
$y$ is drawn after every polynomial in the $g_t$ is committed and after
$\theta$, $\beta$ and $\gamma$.

\begin{lemma}[Batching by $y$]\label{lem:halo2-y-batch}
Let $g_0, \dots, g_{T-1} \in \F[X]$ be fixed, and let $y$ be uniform in
$\F$. If some $g_t$ does not vanish on the rows, then
$C = \sum_{t=0}^{T-1} y^{t} g_t$ vanishes on the rows with probability at
most $(T - 1)/|\F|$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $g_{t_0}(\omega^{j}) \ne 0$. Then
$C(\omega^{j}) = \sum_{t} y^{t} g_t(\omega^{j})$ is a polynomial in $y$ of
degree at most $T - 1$ with the nonzero coefficient $g_{t_0}(\omega^{j})$,
so it vanishes for at most $T - 1$ values of $y$ (the \emph{Math Guide},
\S``The Schwartz--Zippel lemma'').
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Degree of the quotient]\label{thm:halo2-quotient-degree}
Let every column polynomial and every permuted-label polynomial $s_i$ have
degree $< n$, and every constraint polynomial degree at most $d_{\max}$,
counting as degree one each column polynomial at a rotation, each $s_i$,
each of $\ell_0$, $\ell_{\mathrm{last}}$ and $\ell_{\mathrm{blind}}$, and
$X$ (as in ``The deployed chunking'' of \S\ref{sec:arith-perm}). Then
\[
\deg h \;\le\; d_{\max}(n - 1) - n \;=\; (d_{\max} - 1)(n - 1) - 1 .
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
A constraint of degree at most $d_{\max}$ is a sum of products of at most
$d_{\max}$ such factors, each of degree at most $n - 1$ (rotations do not
change degrees); so $\deg g_t \le d_{\max}(n-1)$
and $\deg C \le d_{\max}(n - 1)$. Division by $\ZH$, of degree $n$,
subtracts $n$. The two forms agree since
$(d_{\max} - 1)(n - 1) - 1 = d_{\max}(n - 1) - (n - 1) - 1 = d_{\max}(n-1) - n$.
\end{proof}

% protocol_params.py: D = 9, n = 2^11 = 2048: deg h <= (D-1)(n-1) - 1 =
%   16375 (16376 coefficients); quotient chunks D - 1 = 8, each of degree
%   < n
For the deployed $d_{\max} = 9$ and $n = 2048$ the bound is
$\deg h \le 16375$, far above the degree bound $n - 1 = 2047$ that the
commitment of Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit} supports with $d = n$
generators, so $h$ cannot be committed as one polynomial. The prover
therefore uses a fixed proof shape of $d_{\max} - 1$ \emph{chunks}
$h_0, \dots, h_{d_{\max}-2}$, each of degree $< n$, with
\[
h(X) = \sum_{i=0}^{d_{\max}-2} X^{n i}\,h_i(X),
\]
retaining leading zero chunks when the actual degree is smaller, and
commits to each chunk separately with its own blinder; there are $8$
chunks for Orchard. The count is right because $h$ has at most
$(d_{\max}-1)(n-1) = (d_{\max}-1)n - (d_{\max}-1) < (d_{\max}-1)n$
coefficients. Once the evaluation point $x$ is drawn, the verifier
recombines the chunk \emph{commitments}, weighted by powers of $x^{n}$,
into one commitment $\sum_i [x^{n i}]\,\Comm(h_i)$ to the polynomial
$\sum_i x^{n i} h_i(X)$, whose value at $x$ is $h(x)$; no chunk is ever
opened on its own (the halo2 book's \emph{Protocol Description} chapter,
steps~5 to~8, and note~1 of its ``Zero-knowledge and Completeness'').

% numbers.py (.wip/deck-harvest/scripts/math-halo2/), item 5:
% n = 2^11, d_max = 9: deg C <= 18423, deg h <= 16375, 16376 points;
%   2^13 = 8192 < 16376 <= 2^14 = 16384 = (d_max-1) n
% two-adicity needed: 14 (H alone: 11); v2(p_Pallas - 1) = 32
\begin{remark}[The two-adicity the quotient needs]\label{rem:halo2-twoadic}
Theorem~\ref{thm:halo2-quotient-degree} also sizes the domain on which the
prover computes $h$. She evaluates the polynomials from which $C$ is built
on a set $D$ of points on which $\ZH$ does not vanish, forms $C$ there, and
divides by $\ZH$ point by point. Among polynomials of degree at most the
bound of the theorem, these values determine $h$ if and only if
$|D| \ge (d_{\max} - 1)(n - 1)$ (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Lagrange
interpolation''), which is $16376$ for Orchard. Interpolation by the
fast Fourier transform (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The fast Fourier
transform'') takes $D$ to be a coset of a multiplicative subgroup $H'$ of
power-of-two order, and the least such order is
$2^{14} = (d_{\max} - 1)\,n$, since $2^{13} = 8192 < 16376 \le 2^{14}$.
The subgroup $H'$ of order $2^{14}$ consists of all the roots of
$X^{2^{14}} - 1$, so it contains $H$, and every coset of $H'$ other than
$H'$ itself is disjoint from $H'$ (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Cosets and
Lagrange's theorem''), hence free of the roots of $\ZH$. Such a subgroup
exists if and only if $2^{14} \mid p_{\Pallas} - 1$ (\emph{Math Guide},
\S``Existence over finite fields: the condition $n \mid q-1$''): the field
needs two-adicity at least $14$, where $H$ alone needs $11$. The
two-adicity of $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$ is $32$ (the same section, its remark on
two-adicity).
The requirement bears on the prover's running time only; the verifier
checks $C = h\,\ZH$ at one point, and no soundness argument involves $D$.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Phase 5: the evaluation challenge $x$.}
The verifier sends $x$. The prover evaluates every committed polynomial
the verifier's identities query, \emph{except} the quotient chunks, at $x$
and at the rotated points the identities reference, $\omega x$ for the
running products and for any gate reading the next row, $\omega^{-1}x$ for
the permuted lookup input $A'$ and for gates reading the previous row, and
$\omega^{-6}x$, the deployed boundary row, for all but the last
permutation product, and sends the claimed values as field elements. The
deployed proof also carries the evaluations at $x$ of the fixed columns
and of the permuted-label polynomials, which the same opening binds to
the verifying key's commitments, and of the random polynomial, bound to
its phase-4 commitment. No evaluation of $h$ is sent: the verifier
computes the expected quotient value
$C(x)/(x^{n} - 1)$ from the sent evaluations, evaluating $\ell_0$,
$\ell_{\mathrm{last}}$, $\ell_{\mathrm{blind}}$ and $\ZH$ at $x$ himself,
and enforces it against the collapsed chunk commitment inside the
multipoint opening of phases~6 and~7, where the claimed evaluations
themselves are bound to their commitments as well. The gate
equation is thus checked as the identity $C(x) = h(x)\,\ZH(x)$, the
deployed form of~\eqref{eq:arith-check}.

\paragraph{Phase 6: the multipoint opening, challenges $x_1, \dots, x_4$.}
The parties run Construction~\ref{con:halo2-multipoint} on every
committed polynomial with a claimed evaluation: the advice columns, the
instance column, the fixed and permuted-label polynomials, the permuted
lookup columns, the running products, the random polynomial, and the
collapsed quotient with its expected value, the last two joining the
$\{x\}$ group. The prover sends the commitment $F$ after $x_1, x_2$ and
the five values $\bar q_i$ after $x_3$; after $x_4$ both parties hold the
statement $(F^{*}, x_3, v^{*})$.

\paragraph{Phase 7: the inner-product opening.}
The parties execute the deployed opening of \S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}, the
masked argument in the normalisation of
Remark~\ref{rem:ipa-deployed-norm}, on $(F^{*}, x_3, v^{*})$. The prover
sends the mask commitment $S$; the verifier sends $\xi$ and $\zeta$; then
for $k = 11$ rounds the prover sends the pair $(L_j, R_j)$ and the verifier
answers with $u_j$; finally the prover sends the scalars $c$ and $f$. The
verifier accepts if and only if the deployed
equation~\eqref{eq:ipa-dep-final} holds with $P := F^{*}$, $v := v^{*}$
and $x := x_3$, its one multi-scalar multiplication of length $n$
included. Its symmetric counterpart is the masked
check~\eqref{eq:ipa-masked-final}, which differs from it by the three
changes of that remark: the fold normalisation, a change of variables; the
factor $\zeta$ on $U$; and the value carried as $+[v]U$ rather than
$-[v]G_0$. Neither it nor~\eqref{eq:ipa-final} is the deployed equation.
This is the proof's end; Table~\ref{tab:proof-bytes} counts its bytes by
kind, and nothing else is sent.

\paragraph{Accumulation, not deployed.}
In the recursive variant of Halo~2 the verifier is itself a circuit, and
that circuit defers the one expensive step of phase~7, the claim
$G^{(0)} = \ip{\mathbf s}{\mathbf G} = \Comm(g_{\mathrm{dep}};\, 0)$
of~\eqref{eq:ipa-dep-final}, the deployed form
(Remark~\ref{rem:ipa-deployed-norm}) of the claim
$G^{(0)} = \Comm(g;\, 0)$ of \S\ref{sec:ipa-verifier}, into an
accumulator folded with the accumulators of earlier proofs; a single
decider at the end verifies all accumulated claims. Orchard does not do
this. The protocol specification uses Halo~2 only to prove and verify
Action statements and composes no proofs (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#abstractzk}{\S~4.1.13},
``Zero-Knowledge Proving System''), the Orchard design proposal states that it
makes no use of the recursion (\href{https://zips.z.cash/zip-0224}{ZIP~224}),
and the verifier pays every deferred multiplication itself; several
proofs may share it by the randomised batch check of
Lemma~\ref{lem:ipa-batch}. The variant is developed as the coda of
\S\ref{sec:pcd}.

\paragraph{Costs.}
% proof_bytes.py: per-Action block: 10 + 3 + 3 + 3 + 3 = 22 points; shared:
%   1 + 8 + 1 = 10 commitments before the IPA
% halo2_counts.py: length n: 22 a + 10 (a = 1: 32), plus the mask S; cross
%   terms: 2k = 22, two per round, of lengths n/2, n/4, ..., 1
The prover computes one multi-scalar multiplication of length $n$ per
commitment she sends before the opening, $22a + 10$ for a bundle of $a$
Actions: per Action
the ten advice columns, the six permuted lookup columns and the three
permutation and three lookup products; shared, the random polynomial,
the eight quotient chunks and the multipoint polynomial $f$. She also
computes the $a$ instance commitments, as the verifier does
(\S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}). The opening adds one of length $n$, for its mask
commitment $S$, and the $2k$ cross terms of its rounds, two in each, of lengths
$n/2, n/4, \dots, 1$ in successive rounds. The verifier performs $O(a + \log n)$
field and group operations for a proof of $a$ Actions: he commits to the $a$
instance columns (at most ten nonzero values each),
recomputes the challenges, evaluates each Action's gate equation at $x$,
combines the $23a + 46$ commitments of phase~6, and runs the $k$ rounds of
phase~7; to this he adds the single deferred multi-scalar multiplication of
length $n$, which is the linear term that \S\ref{sec:ipa-halo-trick} discussed
and that a batch verifier amortises across proofs with fresh random scalars;
that batching is distinct from the accumulation above.

% proof_bytes.py, per-Action block: point 10 advice; point 3 A'; point 3 S';
%   point 3 permutation products; point 3 lookup products; scalar 1
%   instance eval; scalar 25 advice evals; scalar 8 permutation product
%   evals; scalar 15 lookup evals: 22 points + 49 scalars = 2272 bytes
% proof_bytes.py, shared block: point 1 random; point 8 quotient chunks;
%   scalar 29 fixed evals; scalar 15 permuted-label evals; scalar 1 random
%   eval; point 1 multipoint; scalar 5 multipoint evals; point 1 S; point
%   22 (L_j, R_j); scalar 2 c, f: 33 points + 52 scalars = 2720 bytes
