\subsection{Zero knowledge: hiding the witness in Halo 2}
\label{sec:halo2-zk}

Knowledge soundness, classified in \S\ref{sec:ks}, concerns what a
convincing prover knows; zero knowledge concerns what the proof reveals.
Halo~2 is zero-knowledge through two devices, hiding commitments and
blinded polynomials, assembled into a simulator. This subsection states
what each device achieves on its own, and states the claim for the whole
protocol as a cited proposition with its hypotheses.

\paragraph{Evaluations of the running example.}
% toy_trace.py: a(X) = 90 + 61X + 22X^2 + 24X^3; z = 20; ipa_toy.py: v =
%   a(20) = <a, b> = 59
The prover of the running example sends commitments and the evaluations
$a(20),\allowbreak b(20),\allowbreak c(20),\allowbreak t(20)$ at the random
point. An evaluation is a
linear equation in the secret coefficients:
$a(20) = 90 + 61 \cdot 20 + 22 \cdot 20^2 + 24 \cdot 20^3 = 59$ is one
equation in the four unknowns $(90, 61, 22, 24)$, and evaluations at four
distinct points determine the column, hence the secret witness $3$. Halo~2
therefore blinds: the witness trace reserves independent random rows,
and every commitment carries an independent random blinding term. Adding
random coefficients to a column polynomial would not do: a column
polynomial is determined by its $n$ row values, and a random coefficient
on $X^{i}$ changes every row, breaking the gates; a random multiple of
the Lagrange polynomial $\ell_j$ of a reserved row changes row $j$ alone,
which is what a blinding row is. The random values violate no
constraint: every selector is zero on the reserved rows, so the gates
hold there, and the permutation and lookup identities carry the
active-row factor of Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows}. Each column's
openings are then uniform (Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-blinding-rows}); the
joint statement is the
proposition of ``The simulator'' below, whose simulator is one in the
sense of the \emph{Crypto Guide} (\S``The simulation paradigm and zero
knowledge'').

\paragraph{Hiding commitments.}
The commitment of Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit} is \emph{perfectly
hiding}. The prover commits a polynomial $p$ as
\[
\Comm(p;\, r) = \ip{\mathbf p}{\mathbf G} + [r]\,H,
\qquad r \text{ uniform in } \F,
\]
with $\mathbf p$ the coefficient vector, $\mathbf G$ the generator
vector, and $H$ the blinding generator of \S\ref{sec:ipa}, of no known
discrete-logarithm relation to $\mathbf G$; the term $[r]H$ makes the
commitment a uniform group element for every $p$, since $H$ generates
$\G$, so a commitment by itself leaks nothing about what was committed.
This is the perfect hiding of the Pedersen commitment, proved in the
\emph{Crypto Guide} (\S``The Pedersen commitment'' and \S``Hiding and
binding'') and recalled in \S\ref{sec:arith-commit}. Every commitment
the prover sends in phases~1 to~6, the advice columns, the permuted
lookup columns $A'$ and $S'$, the permutation and lookup running
products, the random polynomial, the quotient chunks and the multipoint
polynomial $f$, has this form with a fresh $r$ (the halo2 book's
\emph{Protocol Description} chapter, which blinds every group element of
the protocol, its note~4). The verifying key's commitments and the
instance commitments carry the fixed blinding factor $1$ instead: they
commit public data both parties recompute (\S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol},
\S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}), so there is nothing to hide, and a random
blinder would prevent the two computations from agreeing.

\paragraph{Blinding rows.}
% halo2_counts.py: openings per polynomial <= 3 rotations of x + x_3 = 4;
%   deployed b = 5; usable rows n - (b + 1) = 2042 (b is n_blind below)
The prover opens commitments, and an opening reveals evaluations. To
keep those evaluations from revealing the witness, the last
$n_{\mathrm{blind}}$ rows of
the table, the \emph{blinding rows}, carry no circuit data: no gate
applies there, and each advice column holds uniformly random field
elements on them. By Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-blinding-rows} below, a column
opened at $m$ distinct points outside the rows needs
$n_{\mathrm{blind}} \ge m$. The
halo2 book's \emph{Protocol Description} chapter accordingly requires
$n_e + 1$ random evaluations of a polynomial opened at $n_e$ rotations of
$x$, the extra one covering its opening at the point $x_3$ of the
multipoint reduction. In the Action circuit a polynomial is opened at
most four times: at three rotations of $x$, for some advice columns and
the first two permutation products, and at $x_3$. The deployed circuit
takes $n_{\mathrm{blind}} = 5$, one more than that, a parameter fixed by
the implementation, \texttt{halo2\_proofs}; with the boundary row of
Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows} its usable rows number
$n - (n_{\mathrm{blind}} + 1) = 2042$. The permutation and lookup running
products are blinded the same way, their trailing rows random, and the remark's
active-row factor exempts every one of those rows from every argument,
so the random values do not violate a constraint
(Figure~\ref{fig:halo2-blinding-rows}; the halo2 book's
\emph{Permutation argument} and \emph{Lookup argument} chapters, their
``Zero-knowledge adjustment'' sections).

\begin{lemma}[Openings of a column with blinding rows]
\label{lem:halo2-blinding-rows}
Let $v(X) = \sum_{j=0}^{n-1} v_j\,\ell_j(X)$ be a column polynomial over
the rows $\omega^{0}, \dots, \omega^{n-1}$, let $B$ be a set of
$n_{\mathrm{blind}}$ row indices, the blinding rows, and let
$z_1, \dots, z_m \in \F$ be distinct points, none of them a row. With
the values on the other rows held fixed, the map
$(v_j)_{j \in B} \mapsto \bigl(v(z_1), \dots, v(z_m)\bigr)$ from
$\F^{n_{\mathrm{blind}}}$ to $\F^{m}$ is affine, and its linear part has
rank $\min(m, n_{\mathrm{blind}})$ (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Bases,
dimension, and linear maps''). Consequently, if $m \le n_{\mathrm{blind}}$
and the blinding values are uniform and independent, the $m$ evaluations
are jointly uniform on $\F^{m}$ and independent of the values on the
other rows.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The map is $v(z_i) = \sum_{j \notin B} v_j\,\ell_j(z_i) + \sum_{j \in B}
v_j\,\ell_j(z_i)$, a constant plus the linear map with matrix
$L := \bigl[\ell_j(z_i)\bigr]_{i \le m,\, j \in B}$. The Lagrange basis on
the rows has the closed form
$\ell_j(X) = \omega^{j}\,(X^{n} - 1)/\bigl(n\,(X - \omega^{j})\bigr)$ (the
\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The Lagrange basis on $H$''), so
$L = D_1\,K\,D_2$ with $D_1 := \operatorname{diag}(z_i^{n} - 1)$, nonzero
on the diagonal because no $z_i$ is a row, $D_2 :=
\operatorname{diag}(\omega^{j}/n)$, nonzero because $n$ is invertible in
$\F$, and $K := \bigl[1/(z_i - \omega^{j})\bigr]$. Hence $\operatorname{rank}
L = \operatorname{rank} K$. Every square submatrix of $K$ is nonsingular:
take rows $I$ and columns $J$ with $|I| = |J| = s$, and suppose
coefficients $c_j$, $j \in J$, not all zero, satisfy
$\sum_{j \in J} c_j/(z_i - \omega^{j}) = 0$ for all $i \in I$. Over the
common denominator, $\sum_{j \in J} c_j/(X - \omega^{j}) = N(X)/\prod_{j \in
J}(X - \omega^{j})$ with $N(X) := \sum_{j \in J} c_j \prod_{j' \in J,\, j'
\ne j}(X - \omega^{j'})$ of degree $< s$; the polynomial $N$ vanishes at
the $s$ distinct points $z_i$, so it is the zero polynomial (the
\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Roots and the factor theorem''), and then
$N(\omega^{j}) = c_j \prod_{j' \ne j}(\omega^{j} - \omega^{j'}) = 0$ forces
$c_j = 0$ for every $j \in J$, since the rows are distinct: a
contradiction. Thus $K$ has an invertible square submatrix of size
$\min(m, n_{\mathrm{blind}})$, and its rank, at most
$\min(m, n_{\mathrm{blind}})$, equals $\min(m, n_{\mathrm{blind}})$.

If $m \le n_{\mathrm{blind}}$ the linear part is onto $\F^{m}$, so the
affine map is onto, and every fibre is a coset of the same kernel, of
the same size $|\F|^{n_{\mathrm{blind}} - m}$. A uniform point of
$\F^{n_{\mathrm{blind}}}$ therefore lands in each fibre
with the same probability: the image is uniform on $\F^{m}$. The
constant term depends on the other rows but not the distribution, so the
evaluations are independent of them.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small, x=1.0cm, y=-0.5cm,
  cell/.style={draw, minimum width=1.6cm, minimum height=0.46cm,
    inner sep=1pt, font=\scriptsize},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.8mm]}, line width=0.7pt, black!65}]
  \foreach \r in {0,1,2,3} {
    \node[cell, fill=tierin!12] (c\r) at (0,\r) {$v_{\r}$};
  }
  \node[cell, fill=tierin!12] (cd) at (0,4) {$\vdots$};
  \node[cell, fill=tierin!12] (cu) at (0,5) {$v_{u-1}$};
  \node[cell, fill=tierfull!25] (cb) at (0,6) {$v_{u}$};
  \foreach \r in {7,8,9,10,11} {
    \node[cell, fill=tierspend!15] (b\r) at (0,\r) {random};
  }
  \draw[black!60] (-0.85,-0.25) -- (-0.95,-0.25) -- (-0.95,5.25)
    -- (-0.85,5.25);
  \node[left=6pt, align=right, font=\scriptsize] at (-0.95,2.5)
    {active rows\\gates, wiring,\\lookups checked};
  \draw[black!60] (-0.85,5.75) -- (-0.95,5.75) -- (-0.95,6.25)
    -- (-0.85,6.25);
  \node[left=6pt, align=right, font=\scriptsize] at (-0.95,6)

  \draw[black!60] (-0.85,6.75) -- (-0.95,6.75) -- (-0.95,11.25)
    -- (-0.85,11.25);

     outside the rows, rotations of $x$ and $x_3$; on the
     $n_{\mathrm{blind}}$ blinding values the map has rank
     $\min(m, n_{\mathrm{blind}})$
     (Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-blinding-rows}), so the $m$ openings are
     uniform and independent of the active rows};
  \draw[arr] (b9.east) -- (op.west);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{One column of the deployed table: the $u = n - 6 = 2042$ active
rows $0, \dots, u - 1$ (blue) on which every argument is checked, the
boundary row $u$ selected by $\ell_{\mathrm{last}}$ (amber) that pins the
running products, and the $n_{\mathrm{blind}} = 5$ blinding rows (red)
selected by
$\ell_{\mathrm{blind}}$, filled with uniform random values and exempted
from every constraint by the active-row factor of
Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows}. Each opening of the column at a point
outside the rows is a linear functional of all $n$ row values, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-blinding-rows} says the blinding rows alone make at
most $n_{\mathrm{blind}}$ such openings jointly uniform.}
\label{fig:halo2-blinding-rows}
\end{figure}

% blinding_rows.py:
% b = 2 blinding rows [2, 3], m = 1 < b points: ranks seen [1]; surjective:
%   True; m = 2 = b: ranks [2]; surjective: True; m = 3 > b: ranks [2];
%   surjective: False
% b = 3 blinding rows [1, 2, 3]: m = 1, 2, 3: ranks [1], [2], [3];
%   surjective: True; m = 4 > b (2000 random samples): ranks [3]; False
% rank = min(m, b) always: the map is onto F^m iff m <= b; at m = b it is
%   a bijection; it fails first at m = b + 1
On the toy domain $\{1, 22, 96, 75\}$ of \S\ref{sec:arith-polys}, the rank
of the lemma's map was computed for every choice of $m \le 3$ distinct
points among the $93$ outside it and for $2000$ random choices of four:
with $n_{\mathrm{blind}} = 2$ blinding rows the rank is $1$ for one
point and $2$ for two, and the map is onto; for three points the rank
stays $2$ and it is not; with $n_{\mathrm{blind}} = 3$ the ranks are
$1, 2, 3$ for one, two and three points, onto each time, and $3$ for
four points, no longer onto. The threshold is exact: the map is a
bijection at $m = n_{\mathrm{blind}}$ and fails first at
$m = n_{\mathrm{blind}} + 1$, so $n_{\mathrm{blind}} \ge m$ is the
condition, and the deployed $n_{\mathrm{blind}} = 5$ exceeds the at most
$m = 4$ openings of any column by one. One
hypothesis of the lemma is that the points lie outside the rows: an
opening \emph{at} a row would return a cell, witness or blinder, and the
halo2 book's \emph{Protocol Description} chapter accordingly excludes
challenges in the domain (its note~5). The deployed transcript squeezes
full field elements with no rejection, and
Corollary~\ref{cor:halo2-zk-deployed} bounds the effect.

\paragraph{Scope of the lemma.}
It is a \emph{marginal} statement about one column. The proof opens many
related polynomials under shared challenges, and the joint distribution
of all their evaluations, subject to the public relations among them,
the gate equation, the running-product updates, the lookup identities, is
not a consequence of the lemma applied column by column. The
composition is addressed under ``The simulator'' below, and its status is
stated there.

\paragraph{The quotient and the random polynomial.}
The quotient chunks admit no blinding-row treatment: $C$ determines them
entirely, and the rows of $h$ mean nothing. Their commitment blinders,
together with the blinding-row randomness they inherit through $C$,
protect them instead, and their single collapsed opening at $x$ is the
publicly computable value $C(x)/(x^{n} - 1)$, which reveals nothing the
verifier did not already hold. There is one more place the quotient could
leak: the multipoint reduction evaluates its $\{x\}$-group polynomial
$q_1$ at the fresh point $x_3$, and $q_1$ contains the collapsed quotient,
whose value at $x_3$ is not a public function of anything. This is why
phase~4 commits a uniformly random polynomial before $y$ and opens it at
$x$ alongside the quotient: it joins the same group with the same
$x_1$-weighting, so $q_1(x_3)$ carries a uniform summand, and the value
$\bar q_1$ the prover sends is uniform whatever the quotient's value at
$x_3$ is. The protocol commits that random polynomial for this reason
(the halo2 book's \emph{Protocol Description} chapter, step~3 and
note~2).

\paragraph{Composition.}
In each phase the verifier sees only two kinds of object: hiding
commitments, which are uniform group elements, and a bounded number of
evaluations of the committed polynomials at the transcript's challenge
points. The blinding rows supply randomness which, by
Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-blinding-rows}, makes the openings of each column
uniform on its own; a full composition proof must show that this
randomness masks the \emph{joint} evaluations subject to the public
relations, and must account for the correlations among advice, products,
quotient and multipoint polynomials. The final inner-product opening
uses the deployed mask of \S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}, the random polynomial
vanishing at $x_3$ whose commitment $S$ opens phase~7, in addition to
the commitment and round blinders, because Remark~\ref{rem:ipa-not-zk}
showed that a final commitment blinder alone would not hide the folded
coefficient; the opening of one polynomial was proved statistically
honest-verifier zero knowledge there, and that proof is one ingredient
of the composition, not the composition.

\paragraph{The simulator.}
Zero knowledge requires a simulator for the \emph{complete joint
transcript}, given only the public input. The usual proof samples
suitably blinded commitments and evaluations satisfying the verifier's
equations and then programs the random oracle so that the Fiat--Shamir
challenges come out as required, in the manner of the \emph{Crypto
Guide}'s one-round simulator (\S``The Fiat--Shamir transform: from
interactive to non-interactive''). For the interactive protocol the
halo2 book exhibits such a simulator and claims the following; this
volume records the claim and does not reprove it.

\begin{proposition}[Zero knowledge of the interactive protocol, cited]
\label{prop:halo2-zk}
The interactive Halo~2 protocol is perfect special honest-verifier zero
knowledge, the simulator being given the verifier's challenges in
advance (the \emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Sigma-protocols''), for challenge
tuples in which every challenge is nonzero and outside the rows and
every factor $1 + u_\ell\,x_3^{2^{\ell-1}}$ of $g_{\mathrm{dep}}(x_3)$
in~\eqref{eq:ipa-g-dep} is nonzero (the halo2 book's \emph{Protocol
Description} chapter, ``Zero-knowledge and Completeness''). The simulator
commits to uniformly random polynomials in place of every polynomial the
prover commits in phases~1 to~3 (the book's $a_i$: the advice columns,
the permuted lookup columns and the running products), the quotient
chunks and the multipoint polynomial, and uses its foreknowledge
of $\xi$ to choose the mask $s(X)$ so that $p + \xi s - v$ vanishes at
$x_3$.
\end{proposition}

% halo2_counts.py: N_ch = 22: theta, beta, gamma, y, x, x1, x2, x3, x4, xi,
%   zeta, u1, ..., u11; excluded values per challenge <= n + 2 = 2050;
%   statistical distance <= N_ch (n + 2)/|F| = 45100/p = 2^-238.5
\begin{corollary}[Unrestricted challenges]\label{cor:halo2-zk-deployed}
Let every challenge be uniform in $\F$ with no value excluded, as the
deployed transcript draws them. Then the transcripts of the interactive
protocol and of the simulator of Proposition~\ref{prop:halo2-zk}, run on
the same challenges, are within statistical distance
$N_{\mathrm{ch}}\,(n + 2)/|\F|$, where $N_{\mathrm{ch}} = 22$ is the
number of challenges, $\theta, \beta, \gamma, y, x, x_1, \dots, x_4,
\xi, \zeta$ and the $k = 11$ values $u_\ell$; for $n = 2^{11}$ this is
$45100/p_{\Pallas} \approx 2^{-238.5}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Each challenge has at most $n + 2$ excluded values: $0$, the $n$ rows,
and, for $u_\ell$, the value that makes its factor of
$g_{\mathrm{dep}}(x_3)$ zero. The challenges have the same distribution
in both experiments; on the event that none takes an excluded value the
two transcripts are identically distributed by
Proposition~\ref{prop:halo2-zk}, and by a union bound that event fails
with probability at most $N_{\mathrm{ch}}\,(n + 2)/|\F|$, which therefore
bounds the statistical distance.
\end{proof}

Perfect hiding of each commitment and Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-blinding-rows}
are ingredients of that argument, not by themselves a proof that the
joint distribution matches a real execution. For the compiled argument,
the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s compilation recipe (Theorem ``The compilation
recipe, informal'', \S``SNARKs: succinct non-interactive arguments of
knowledge'') yields zero knowledge when the polynomial IOP is properly
blinded and the corresponding simulation theorem applies; for a
multi-round protocol that is a multi-round simulation theorem in the
programmable random-oracle model, which this volume neither proves nor
cites from a source that proves it for this protocol. Non-interactive
zero knowledge of the deployed proof is therefore stated, not proved.

\paragraph{The three properties.}
Subject to the hypotheses just named, Halo~2 targets three guarantees
for the relation $\Rel_{\mathcal C}$ of a PLONKish circuit
(Definition~\ref{def:plonkish}), the three that
\S\ref{sec:intro-claim} demanded of a proof. \emph{Overwhelming
completeness}: an honest prover holding a satisfying witness convinces
the verifier except on negligible transcript degeneracies, a zero
inner-product challenge (\S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}, probability $1/|\F|$ per
challenge), a point $x_3$ among the query points of the multipoint
reduction (``Completeness'' in \S\ref{sec:halo2-multipoint}), an
evaluation point $x$ on a row, where the verifier's division by
$x^{n} - 1$ in phase~5 is undefined (probability $n/|\F|$), or challenges
$\beta, \gamma$ at which a running-product denominator factor vanishes
at an earlier row than any numerator factor does
(Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows}; probability at most $N/|\F|$ for the
permutation argument on $N$ participating cells and $2u/|\F|$ per lookup
on $u$ active rows).
\emph{Knowledge soundness}: an efficient extractor, given rewindable
access to any prover the verifier accepts with non-negligible
probability, outputs a satisfying witness, so that an accepted proof
certifies that one is \emph{known}. Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks}
supplies the extractor for the opening layer; \S\ref{sec:ks} lifts it to
the non-interactive argument, whose challenges are transcript hashes,
and classifies what that lifting establishes.
\emph{Zero knowledge}: a witness-free simulator reproduces the proof,
exactly for restricted challenges (Proposition~\ref{prop:halo2-zk}) and
within the distance of Corollary~\ref{cor:halo2-zk-deployed} for the
deployed ones. Knowledge soundness is the property of Action proofs that
the Orchard balance and nullifier arguments consume, together with the
binding signature (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#orchardbalance}{\S~4.14},
``Balance and Binding Signature (Orchard)'') and the consensus nullifier
rules (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#nullifierset}{\S~3.9},
``Nullifier Sets''); zero knowledge is the property the privacy
argument consumes.

\subsection{Halo 2 as a compiled polynomial IOP}
\label{sec:halo2-piop}

The protocol of \S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol} factors into two layers: a
\emph{polynomial interactive oracle proof} for the relation, and a
polynomial commitment scheme, in the sense of the \emph{Crypto Guide}
(\S``Polynomial commitment schemes''), that realises the proof's oracles.
Every phase of the protocol belongs to one of the two layers.

\paragraph{The polynomial IOP, recalled.}
The \emph{Crypto Guide} defines the object (\S``SNARKs: succinct
non-interactive arguments of knowledge'', under ``Polynomial interactive
oracle proofs''): a public-coin interactive protocol in which, in place of
ordinary messages, the prover in each round sends one or more
\emph{oracles} for polynomials $p_i \in \F[X]$ of bounded degree, the
verifier answers with uniformly random field elements, and at the end
the verifier \emph{queries} each oracle at a small number of points,
receiving the evaluations, and accepts or rejects as a deterministic
function of the challenges, the evaluations and the public input.
Completeness, soundness and knowledge soundness are those of interactive
arguments, with oracle access in place of reading the messages, and the
soundness analysis is purely algebraic, resting on the Schwartz--Zippel
lemma. The one respect in which a polynomial IOP differs from a vanilla
interactive argument is that the verifier never reads a prover message in
full: it queries a few evaluations of each oracle. That is what renders
the verifier succinct even when the polynomials have large degree; the
message length is never read, only as many field elements as there are
queries.

\paragraph{The compiler, recalled.}
The \emph{Crypto Guide}'s compilation recipe (the same section) turns a
polynomial IOP into a non-interactive argument with a polynomial
commitment scheme $(\Setup, \Comm, \Open, \mathsf{Eval}, \Verify)$ and a
random oracle $H$. Setup runs the commitment scheme's setup. The prover
simulates the IOP, replacing each oracle message $p_i$ by its commitment
$C_i$, derives every verifier challenge from the transcript by the
Fiat--Shamir transform of \S\ref{sec:halo2-fs}, and supplies, for each
of the IOP's queries, the claimed evaluation together with an evaluation
proof ($\mathsf{Eval}$). The verifier parses the transcript, recomputes
the challenges, checks each evaluation proof ($\Verify$), and runs the
IOP's accept-or-reject check on the opened evaluations
(Figure~\ref{fig:halo2-compile}).

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small,
  layer/.style={draw, rounded corners=3pt, inner sep=6pt, align=flush left},
  itm/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, inner sep=3pt, align=flush center,
    minimum height=6.5mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]}, line width=0.7pt, black!65}]
  \node[layer, draw=tierin, fill=tierin!5, minimum width=13.4cm,
    minimum height=2.1cm] (piop) at (0,1.6) {};
  \node[anchor=north west, tierin!80!black, font=\small\bfseries]
    at (piop.north west) {polynomial IOP (the relation)};
  \node[itm, fill=tierin!12, draw=tierin, text width=3.2cm] (or)
    at (-4.4,1.35) {oracle messages\\$p_i \in \F[X]$};
  \node[itm, fill=tierin!12, draw=tierin, text width=3.2cm] (ch)
    at (0,1.35) {verifier challenges\\$\theta, \beta, \gamma, y, x$};
  \node[itm, fill=tierin!12, draw=tierin, text width=3.2cm] (qu)
    at (4.4,1.35) {oracle queries\\$p_i(x)$, $p_i(\omega^{r} x)$};
  \node[layer, draw=tierspend, fill=tierspend!4, minimum width=13.4cm,
    minimum height=2.1cm] (pcs) at (0,-1.6) {};
  \node[anchor=south west, tierspend!80!black, font=\small\bfseries]
    at (pcs.south west) {polynomial commitment scheme and hash
    (the cryptography)};
  \node[itm, fill=tierspend!10, draw=tierspend, text width=3.2cm] (co)
    at (-4.4,-1.35) {commitments\\$C_i = \Comm(p_i;\, r_i)$};
  \node[itm, fill=tierfull!15, draw=tierfull, text width=3.2cm] (ha)
    at (0,-1.35) {transcript hashes\\(\S\ref{sec:halo2-fs})};
  \node[itm, fill=tierspend!10, draw=tierspend, text width=3.2cm] (op)
    at (4.4,-1.35) {openings: one\\multipoint + IPA (\S\ref{sec:ipa})};
  \draw[arr] (or) -- node[right, font=\scriptsize] {replaced by} (co);
  \draw[arr] (ch) -- node[right, font=\scriptsize] {replaced by} (ha);
  \draw[arr] (qu) -- node[right, font=\scriptsize] {answered by} (op);
  \node[font=\scriptsize, black!60, align=flush center] at (0,-3.1)
    {phases $1$--$5$ of \S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol} are the upper layer
     compiled; phases $6$--$7$ are the lower layer's openings};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The compiler. The polynomial IOP (blue) is the information-%
theoretic layer: oracles, public-coin challenges and evaluation queries,
sound by the Schwartz--Zippel lemma alone. The cryptographic layer (red
and amber) realises it: each oracle becomes a commitment, each challenge
a transcript hash, each query an opening, and Halo~2 collapses every
opening into one. The two layers are separable: the same IOP compiles
with any commitment scheme meeting the hypotheses of
Remark~\ref{rem:halo2-compile}.}
\label{fig:halo2-compile}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{PLONK as a polynomial IOP.}
The IOP that Halo~2 compiles is PLONK's, in the PLONKish generality of
\S\ref{sec:arith-plonkish}. Its oracles, sent by the prover, are one
polynomial per advice column; for each lookup, the two permuted columns
$A'$ and $S'$; the permutation running products, one per chunk, and one
lookup running product per lookup; and the quotient $h$, split into
chunks below the degree bound. The fixed columns, the selectors among
them, and the permuted-label polynomials are \emph{preprocessed},
part of the verifying key rather than prover messages, which is why they
are absent from the list. The verifier's challenges, in order, are
$\theta$ for the lookup compression, $\beta$ and $\gamma$ for the
permutation and lookup products, $y$ for the combination of all
constraints into one gate equation, and $x$ for the evaluation point. The
final check is the gate equation $C(x) = h(x)\,\ZH(x)$, with $C$ the
$y$-combination of every gate, permutation and lookup constraint
polynomial and $\ZH$ the vanishing polynomial of the rows, which the
verifier evaluates from the oracles' values at $x$ and at the rotations
its constraints read: $\omega x$ or $\omega^{-1}x$ for an adjacent row,
and $\omega^{-6}x$, the rotation to the boundary row of
Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows}, for the chunk-boundary identities of the
permutation argument.

\paragraph{The map onto the phases.}
Phases~1 to~5 of \S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol} are this IOP compiled, with one
addition: the commitments to the advice, the permuted lookup columns, the
running products and the quotient chunks are the oracle messages replaced by
commitments, and the random polynomial of phase~4, committed and evaluated at
$x$, is the compiler's addition for zero knowledge (\S\ref{sec:halo2-zk}), not
an IOP message; the challenges are the IOP's challenges squeezed from the
transcript, and the claimed evaluations of phase~5 answer the IOP's oracle
queries. Phases~6 and~7 bind those answers to the commitments: the multipoint
reduction of \S\ref{sec:halo2-multipoint}, with its challenges $x_1, \dots, x_4$
and combined polynomial $f^{*}$, followed by the single inner-product opening of
$f^{*}$. That is the bridge between the abstract compiler and the concrete
protocol, and the phase numbering is the one Table~\ref{tab:halo2-phases} uses.
Under the hypotheses of the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s compilation recipe (Theorem
``The compilation recipe, informal'') and of Remark~\ref{rem:halo2-compile}, the
compiled protocol has the IOP's relation and the commitment scheme's
assumptions, transparency, proof size and verifier cost.

\begin{remark}[The compilation principle, and what it does not say]
\label{rem:halo2-compile}
Soundness of the compiled argument is not the sum of two numbers, and
the notions under which it is proved deserve their names. A polynomial
IOP is \emph{round-by-round} knowledge sound, informally, when its
partial transcripts admit a ``doomed'' labelling that a prover message
cannot escape except with bounded probability over the next verifier
challenge; a related \emph{state-restoration} notion lets a prover
restore an earlier verifier state and redraw its challenge, which is
precisely what a prover attacking a transcript hash can do. Precise
equivalence or implication theorems between the two notions require
additional conditions on the protocol and on the extractor, and this
volume does not identify them. The \emph{Crypto Guide}'s compilation
recipe (Theorem ``The compilation recipe, informal'', \S``SNARKs:
succinct non-interactive arguments of knowledge'') assumes a complete,
knowledge-sound public-coin polynomial IOP; a correct,
evaluation-binding and extractable commitment scheme; compatible
composition hypotheses; and a suitable multi-round Fiat--Shamir theorem
in the random-oracle model. For a multi-round protocol such as Halo~2
this volume reads those hypotheses as three: the IOP has a
round-by-round or state-restoration knowledge-soundness theorem; the
commitment scheme is extractable and evaluation-binding in a form
compatible with it; and the transcript satisfies the hypotheses of an
appropriate multi-round Fiat--Shamir theorem. The conclusion is that the
compiled protocol is a non-interactive argument of knowledge in the
random-oracle model, by layered extraction: the commitment scheme's
extractor recovers the committed polynomials, converting the argument's
prover into an IOP prover; the multi-round Fiat--Shamir theorem
transfers the round-by-round or state-restoration guarantee to the
non-interactive setting; and evaluation binding ensures that the opened
evaluations are those of the extracted polynomials. What it does
\emph{not} say is that the knowledge error is determined by two scalars,
the IOP's knowledge error and the commitment scheme's extraction error,
added together: the exact error and the query loss depend on the
definitions and on the extraction model, and \S\ref{sec:ks} names the
theorems that fix them for the analysed protocol and classifies their
instantiation to the deployed transcript.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{Delegated results.}
The subsection proves nothing afresh. The commitment interface $(\Setup, \Comm,
\Open, \mathsf{Eval}, \Verify)$ with its hiding, binding and extraction
properties is the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s (\S``Polynomial commitment
schemes''); the Fiat--Shamir transform and its random-oracle security
are the same volume's (\S``The Fiat--Shamir transform: from interactive
to non-interactive''); the definitions of completeness, soundness and
knowledge soundness for interactive arguments used above are its
(\S``Interactive proofs, zero knowledge, and SNARKs'' and
\S``Knowledge soundness and extractors''); and the Schwartz--Zippel
lemma and the vanishing polynomial of the rows are the \emph{Math
Guide}'s (\S``The Schwartz--Zippel lemma'' and \S``The evaluation domain
$H$ and its vanishing polynomial''). The volume adds the instantiation:
which oracles, which challenges, which openings, and the one commitment
scheme that realises them.

% read from compute/OUTPUT.md and repeated as a comment above each passage
% that uses it. The deployed stance was read against the halo2 book's
% Protocol Description chapter.

\section{A rigorous treatment of knowledge soundness}
\label{sec:ks}

Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} and Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks} proved
the inner-product opening knowledge sound for a verifier who draws his
challenges: an extractor that runs the prover
as a subroutine, rewinds it and feeds it fresh challenges recovers the
committed vector or a nontrivial discrete-logarithm relation among the
generators, which the hardness of the discrete logarithm on Vesta
excludes. The deployed verifier draws nothing; every challenge is a hash
of the transcript (\S\ref{sec:halo2-fs}), and
Remark~\ref{rem:halo2-compile} named the hypotheses under which a
compiled argument inherits its layers' guarantees without fixing them.
This section determines what the extractor establishes when every
challenge is a hash of the transcript. It combines the
rewinding-extractor framework of the \emph{Crypto Guide}, the
definition of knowledge soundness (\S``Knowledge soundness and
extractors''), the tree extraction of Sigma-protocols
(\S``Sigma-protocols'') and the Fiat--Shamir theorem with its stated
caveats (\S``The Fiat--Shamir transform: from interactive to
non-interactive''), with the algebraic group model, named among the
idealisations of \S``The standard model and the random oracle model''.
It lifts the interactive extractor to the non-interactive argument in two
layers and names the loss of the naive lifting; it states the model in
which the halo2 book proves its extraction theorem and classifies what
that theorem does and does not establish for the deployed transcript;
and it closes on the gap between concrete and asymptotic security. The
running-time vocabulary, polynomial, negligible and $\PPT$, is the
\emph{Math Guide}'s (\S``Polynomial, exponential, and negligible
functions'' and \S``Algorithms, running time, and PPT''); expected
polynomial time is the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s (\S``Adversaries and the
security parameter'', the remark on expected versus strict polynomial
time); tightness and loss are the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s
(\S``Reductions'', \S``Concrete security and the bit-security budget'');
the assumption is its standing one (\S``Standing assumptions'').

\subsection{From the interactive extractor to the non-interactive
  argument}
\label{sec:ks-noninteractive}

\paragraph{What the interactive extractor costs.}
% ks_numbers.py:
% d = 2^11 = 2048; ternary tree 3^11 = 177147 leaves; binary tree 2^11 = 2048; log_2 3 = 1.58496, d^(log_2 3) = 177147
% |F| = p_Pallas ~ 2^254.000 (255 bits); d/|F| = 2^(-243.0)
Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} extracts from a tree of accepting transcripts
with three children at every node and one level per round: $3^{k}$
leaves for $k = \log_2 d$ rounds, that is $d^{\log_2 3}$ with
$\log_2 3 = 1.58496\ldots$, polynomial in $d$ though more than a binary
tree would cost. At the deployed $d = 2^{11} = 2048$ the tree has
$3^{11} = 177147$ leaves against the $2^{11} = 2048$ a binary tree
would have; the extractor runs in expected polynomial time, the
rewinding that assembles the tree being the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s
(\S``Sigma-protocols'', the tree-extraction theorem cited there). Its
knowledge error is the $O(\log d/|\F|)$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks}, one
$O(1/|\F|)$ term per round for the challenges the siblings must avoid.
The scale of these terms is worth fixing once:
% ks_numbers.py:
% identity check: d_max n - 1 = 18431; 18431/p_Pallas = 2^(-239.8)
the proof system's own allowances are linear in $n = d$, the
$18431/|\F| \approx 2^{-239.8}$ of the identity check
(\S\ref{sec:arith-random}), whose difference polynomial $C - h\,\ZH$ has
degree at most $d_{\max}\,n - 1 = 18431$ at the deployed $d_{\max} = 9$
($\deg C \le d_{\max}(n - 1)$ by the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:halo2-quotient-degree}, and the $d_{\max} - 1$ committed
chunks of the quotient have degree $< n$ each, \S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol});
the order-$n/|\F|$ term of
the multipoint reduction (Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-multipoint}); and
$d/|\F| = 2^{11}/2^{254} = 2^{-243}$ is that linear allowance for the
opening. The opening's proved error sits below it, and quoting the
linear figure for the opening loses nothing in a system whose other
terms are already linear in $d$, as the proof of
Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} noted. Every one of these is a Schwartz--Zippel
term, not a security level, in the sense fixed in \S\ref{sec:intro-claim}.

\paragraph{Layered extraction.}
The compiled argument of \S\ref{sec:halo2-piop} was assembled in two
layers, an information-theoretic polynomial IOP and a cryptographic
commitment scheme, and its extractor is assembled in the same two
layers, run backwards (Figure~\ref{fig:ks-layered}).

\emph{The lower layer recovers polynomials.} The prover has sent
commitments $C_\nu$ and, in phases~6 and~7, one opening of the combined
$F^{*} = F + \sum_i [x_4^{i}]\,Q_i$ with $Q_i = \sum_e [x_1^{e}]\,
C_{\nu_{i,e}}$ (Construction~\ref{con:halo2-multipoint}). Phase~7 runs
the deployed opening of \S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}, and the extractor of
Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks}, the tree extraction of
Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} with two further levels, $\xi$ and $\zeta$, the
latter in the root role of the resampled $U$, returns
the coefficient vector behind $F^{*}$, a representation of $F^{*}$ over
$(\mathbf G, H)$, or a nontrivial discrete-logarithm relation among
$\mathbf G$, $H$ and $U$. To reach the individual commitments the
extractor rewinds over the combining challenges as well: the vectors
behind $F^{*}$ for $m + 1$ distinct values of $x_4$ are $m + 1$ linear
equations in the $m + 1$ unknown vectors behind $F, Q_1, \dots, Q_m$,
whose coefficient matrix has the rows $(1, x_4, \dots, x_4^{m})$ and is
invertible because a nonzero polynomial of degree at most $m$ has at
most $m$ roots (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Roots and the factor theorem''),
the argument of the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:ipa-single-round}; the
vectors behind $Q_i$ for $|I_i|$ distinct values of $x_1$ likewise yield
the vectors behind each $C_\nu$ in group $i$. One $C_\nu$ is the
collapsed quotient $\sum_i [x^{ni}]\,\Comm(h_i)$ of
\S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol}; its vectors for $d_{\max} - 1$ values of $x$
with pairwise distinct $x^{n}$ yield the vectors behind the chunks
$h_i$, by the same argument with $x^{n}$ in place of $x_4$. The output
is one polynomial of degree $< n$ per commitment the prover sent, and
two different vectors extracted for one commitment would be a nontrivial
discrete-logarithm relation among $\mathbf G$ and $H$, exactly as at the
root of Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks}.
This layer is the only place the discrete logarithm on Vesta enters, and
it enters in only one way: the extractor either recovers the polynomials
or exhibits a relation.

\emph{The upper layer recovers the witness.} With the polynomials in
hand, the prover's claimed evaluations are the true evaluations of fixed
polynomials except with the Schwartz--Zippel allowance of
Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-multipoint}, since a claimed value that differs from
the extracted polynomial's value survives the reduction only with that
probability; and ``the true evaluations of fixed polynomials'' is
precisely the hypothesis under which \S\ref{sec:arith} proved its
theorems. Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-random} turns the accepted identity
$C(x) = h(x)\,\ZH(x)$ into divisibility of $C$,
Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-y-batch} splits it into the vanishing of every
constraint polynomial on the rows, Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-perm} with
Corollary~\ref{cor:arith-perm-deployed} turns the running products into
the wiring, and Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-lookup} with
Proposition~\ref{prop:arith-theta} turns the lookup identities into table
membership, each with its stated term over $|\F|$;
Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-quotient} reads gate satisfaction off
divisibility. The witness is then read off directly: the advice columns
are the values of the extracted advice polynomials on the rows. Nothing
in this layer uses an assumption; the IOP's extractor outputs the
oracles, which the layer below supplies. The consequence
runs both ways. A break of the discrete logarithm on Vesta breaks the
lower layer and nothing else, in the way \S\ref{sec:ipa-binding}
described, one commitment opening to any vector; and no assumption can
rescue an identity that the upper layer does not actually enforce,
because the upper layer's errors are counting statements about
polynomials.

% ks_numbers.py: x-level tree children: (d_max - 2) n + 1 = 14337 distinct x
%   guarantee d_max - 1 = 8 distinct x^n
\emph{What is proved and what is described.} In the interactive setting
the composed extractor is a tree extractor in the sense of the
\emph{Crypto Guide} with a wider tree: three children at each of the $k$
inner-product levels, two at the $\zeta$ level and two at the $\xi$
level, $m + 1$ at the $x_4$ level, $\max_i |I_i|$ at the $x_1$ level,
one $x_1$ serving every group, and, at the $x$ level,
$(d_{\max} - 2)\,n + 1 = 14337$ children: each value of $x^{n}$ has at
most $n$ preimages, so only that many distinct challenges guarantee the
$d_{\max} - 1$ pairwise distinct $x^{n}$ the quotient step needs, as
Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} took five children for three distinct squares.
The two $\zeta$ children must moreover be nonzero, as in
Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks}. Each
polynomial the upper layer consumes is fixed by a binding commitment sent
before every challenge that tests it: the advice
before $\theta$, the permuted lookup columns $A'$ and $S'$ before
$\beta$ and $\gamma$, the running products and the random polynomial
before $y$, the quotient chunks before $x$ (\S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol});
the polynomial behind $F$ and the mask behind $S$, committed after $x$,
serve only inside the lower layer; and all of the consumed ones precede
$x$, the first challenge at which the tree
branches, so the upper layer's Schwartz--Zippel arguments apply on every
branch without further rewinding. The inner-product part of that tree is
Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks}, proved; the theorems of the upper
layer are proved in \S\ref{sec:arith}; the accounting that joins them
into one tree-extraction statement with one knowledge error is the
compilation principle of Remark~\ref{rem:halo2-compile}, whose
hypotheses this volume names and does not discharge. In particular the
compiled error is not asserted to be the sum of the layers' errors.

\centering

one: from an accepting run, the extractor of

evaluation challenge $x$, recovers the polynomial behind every
commitment or exhibits a discrete-logarithm relation; the assumption
(amber) lives here and nowhere else. The upper layer (blue) is the

\paragraph{The naive lifting, and its loss.}
Everything above is interactive: the extractor chose the challenges it
fed the prover. In the deployed argument the challenges are hashes, and
the extractor of the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s Fiat--Shamir theorem obtains
its second transcript by \emph{forking}: it finds, among the prover's
at most $Q$ hash queries, the critical one whose answer became the
challenge, rewinds to it, and answers it afresh, succeeding with
probability about $\varepsilon(\varepsilon/Q - 1/|\F|)$, so that the
extractor's success is the prover's squared and divided by $Q$
(\S``The Fiat--Shamir transform: from interactive to non-interactive'',
the knowledge-soundness clause of its theorem). That theorem covers one
challenge. The tree of Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} needs three siblings at
each of $k$ levels, and a sibling at level $j$ must share the
transcript prefix through level $j - 1$, which in the hashed setting
means agreeing with the prover's prefix and its earlier hash answers:
in a transcript hash, changing one message redraws \emph{all} later
challenges, so siblings at round $j$ can be found only among the
prover's own queries. Let $\mu$ be the number of verifier challenges of
the protocol being lifted. The simplest lifting guesses, among the at most
$Q$ queries, the $\mu$ that answered them, and runs the
interactive extractor against the interactive prover that the guess
defines; the guess is right with probability at least $Q^{-\mu}$, so
the knowledge error is multiplied by $Q^{\mu}$. Attema, Fehr and
Kloo\ss{} (\emph{Fiat--Shamir Transformation of Multi-Round Interactive
Proofs}) record this $Q^{\mu}$ as the loss for a general
$(2\mu + 1)$-move protocol; nesting the one-round forking level by level
fares worse, since each level squares the success probability before
dividing by $Q$. For the opening alone $\mu$ exceeds $k = \log_2 d$,
the choice of $U$ (in the deployed opening, $\xi$ and $\zeta$) being a
challenge too, and $\mu$ grows once the proof system's own challenges
are counted. The loss
belongs to this analysis. For $(k_1, \dots, k_\mu)$-special-sound
protocols (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Sigma-protocols'', the definition of
tree special soundness) the same authors prove, in the random oracle
model and with no idealisation of the group, a knowledge error that
grows only linearly in $Q$. The rounds of the opening's extraction tree
have that shape, but its root step, which rewinds the choice of $U$ (in
the deployed opening, $\xi$ and $\zeta$), and its alternative output, a
discrete-logarithm relation, are not checked here against that
theorem's hypotheses, and this volume does not assess that route.

% ks_numbers.py:
% Q^(log d) at Q = 2^80, log d = 11: 2^880; challenge space 2^255: the bound Q^(log d) * d/|F| = 2^637 > 1 is vacuous
With the deployed $k = \log_2 d = 11$, so $\mu > 11$ for the opening
alone, and a query budget of $Q = 2^{80}$ hash evaluations, the factor
$Q^{\mu}$ exceeds $Q^{11} = 2^{880}$; multiplied by the linear allowance
$d/|\F| = 2^{-243}$ it exceeds $2^{637}$, a bound above $1$, which is no
bound at all. Nor does the proved $O(\log d/|\F|)$ help: the challenge
space is a $255$-bit field with fewer than $2^{255}$ elements, so
$2^{880}$ exceeds $|\F|$ itself, and a factor larger than $|\F|$ turns
every term of the form $c/|\F|$ with $c \ge 1$ into a number above $1$.
Asymptotically the loss falls elsewhere. With $Q$ and $d$ polynomial in
the security parameter $\lambda$ (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Adversaries
and the security parameter'') and $|\F| = 2^{\Theta(\lambda)}$, the factor
$Q^{\mu} = 2^{O(\log^{2}\lambda)}$ is super-polynomial and
sub-exponential in the \emph{Math Guide}'s classification
(\S``Polynomial, exponential, and negligible functions''). The
Schwartz--Zippel terms survive it:
$Q^{\mu}\,O(\log d)/|\F| = 2^{O(\log^{2}\lambda) - \Theta(\lambda)}$ is
negligible. The reduction does not survive it: the guessing extractor
succeeds only with probability reduced by $Q^{\mu}$, or runs $Q^{\mu}$
times longer to compensate, and so is not $\PPT$, and the prover's
success is then bounded only by $Q^{\mu}$ times the discrete-logarithm
advantage, which need not be negligible when that advantage is merely
negligible. The interactive extractor is polynomial,
$d^{\log_2 3}$ transcripts in expected polynomial time; the looseness
arises in the naive analysis of the passage to the non-interactive
argument.

\paragraph{What the tighter notions are for.}
Remark~\ref{rem:halo2-compile} named the two notions built to avoid
this: \emph{round-by-round} knowledge soundness, which labels partial
transcripts as doomed and bounds the probability that one verifier
challenge lifts the doom, and \emph{state-restoration} soundness, which
lets the prover restore an earlier verifier state and redraw its
challenge, precisely the freedom a prover attacking a transcript hash
has. Each exists to obtain a bound on the compiled argument that loses
a factor linear in $Q$ rather than $Q^{\mu}$; no equivalence between
them is asserted here, as none was there. Claiming a single factor $Q$
for a compiled protocol nevertheless requires the precise theorem's
state-restoration, transcript, challenge and extraction hypotheses to
hold of that protocol, and this volume does not assert a factor-$Q$
bound for Halo~2's compiled protocol. The next subsection names the
model in which the halo2 book proves such a bound, in the
state-restoration form, for its abstract interactive protocol, what
that analysis assumes, and why its instantiation to the deployed
transcript remains unwritten.

\subsection{The algebraic group model}
\label{sec:ks-agm}

The nested forking of the previous subsection rewinds because the
extractor has no other way to learn what is behind a group element:
three accepting continuations are the price of one representation. The
\emph{algebraic group model} of Fuchsbauer, Kiltz and Loss (\emph{The
Algebraic Group Model and its Applications}) removes the
need by restricting the adversary's group operations, and it enables
tighter analyses for protocols that suit it. It is not by itself a
theorem eliminating every Fiat--Shamir loss; what it eliminates is the
rewinding, and what remains must still be proved.

\begin{definition}[Algebraic adversary]\label{def:ks-algebraic}
An \emph{algebraic adversary} against a group $\G$ is a $\PPT$
algorithm that, whenever it outputs a group element $P \in \G$,
additionally outputs a vector $\mathbf a$ of scalars with
\[
P = \ip{\mathbf a}{\mathbf Y},
\]
where $\mathbf Y$ is the vector of group elements it has previously
seen: the generators it was given and the elements it has received. The
vector $\mathbf a$ is the \emph{representation} of $P$.
\end{definition}

\paragraph{Where the model sits.}
The algebraic group model sits between the standard model, in which the
adversary is any $\PPT$ algorithm and every primitive is concrete code
it may inspect (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``The standard model and the
random oracle model''), and the generic group model, in which the
adversary may perform group operations only through an oracle on opaque
handles and never exploits the representation of an element (the same
section, the remark on the generic and algebraic group models). An
algebraic adversary is less restricted than a generic one, since it may
compute on the bit representation of group elements however it likes;
it must only be able to explain each element it outputs as a
combination of elements it has seen. The model is an idealisation (the
same remark): a proof in it says nothing about an adversary that
outputs a group element it cannot explain. Figure~\ref{fig:ks-models}
draws the three models as nested restrictions on the adversary and
places this section's analyses on them; the random oracle model, which
idealises the hash rather than the group, is an independent axis, and an
analysis may combine either group model with it.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small,
  ring/.style={draw, rounded corners=4pt, line width=0.8pt},
  itm/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, inner sep=3pt, align=flush left,
    font=\scriptsize, fill=white}]
  \node[ring, draw=tierin, fill=tierin!4, minimum width=13.4cm,
    minimum height=6.8cm] (std) at (0,0) {};
  \node[anchor=north west, tierin!80!black, font=\small\bfseries]
    at (std.north west) {standard model: any $\PPT$ adversary};
  \node[ring, draw=tierfull, fill=tierfull!7, minimum width=9.4cm,
    minimum height=4.5cm] (agm) at (1.3,-0.55) {};
  \node[anchor=north west, tierfull!80!black, font=\small\bfseries]
    at (agm.north west) {algebraic group model: every output explained};
  \node[ring, draw=tierrecv, fill=tierrecv!9, minimum width=5.4cm,
    minimum height=2.3cm] (ggm) at (2.5,-1.1) {};
  \node[anchor=north west, tierrecv!80!black, font=\small\bfseries]
    at (ggm.north west) {generic group model};
  \node[itm, text width=4.5cm] at (2.5,-1.4)
    {group operations through an oracle on opaque handles; the setting
     of the generic lower bounds on the discrete logarithm};
  \node[itm, text width=2.7cm, draw=tierfull] at (-1.85,-1.05)
    {Ghoshal--Tessaro: state-restoration bounds for Bulletproofs-style
     openings, tight in $Q$; the halo2 book's extraction theorem};
  \node[itm, text width=2.7cm, draw=tierin] at (-5.1,0.75)
