\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Three models as nested restrictions on the adversary. Every
adversary admitted by an inner ring is admitted by the rings around it,
so a proof in an outer ring is the stronger statement and a bound in an
inner ring is the easier to make tight. The interactive extractor of
Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} lives in the standard model for uniform
generators; the tight
state-restoration bounds of Ghoshal and Tessaro, and the halo2 book's
extraction theorem that invokes them, live in the algebraic group model
(amber). The generic group model (green) is where the discrete
logarithm's own lower bounds are proved.}
\label{fig:ks-models}
\end{figure}

% ks_numbers.py: round bad set 4n = 8192; n_g(n-1) = 18423 > 4n: True
\paragraph{Online extraction in the algebraic group model.}
In this model the prover outputs every group element with its
representation, and the extractor reads the witness off the
representations of a single accepting transcript instead of assembling
it from rewound ones; no rewinding occurs, and the price is the
assumption that the prover is algebraic. For the deployed opening,
equation~\eqref{eq:ipa-dep-final}, the halo2 book's \emph{Protocol
Description} chapter carries this out (``Witness-extended Emulation'', the
definition of the bad-challenge sets): comparing the two sides of the
accepting equation coordinate by coordinate either exhibits a
nontrivial discrete-logarithm relation among the generators or, unless
some challenge falls in a set of \emph{bad challenges} fixed by the
messages before it, forces the representation of $P$ to open to the
claimed value. The analysed protocol draws every challenge from one
space $\mathcal C$ of field elements, none zero or a row, and each round
challenge $u_j$ further so that its factor $1 + u_j\,x_3^{2^{j-1}}$
of $g_{\mathrm{dep}}(x_3)$ in~\eqref{eq:ipa-g-dep} is nonzero. Each round
challenge $u_j$ has at most $4n$ bad values in $\mathcal C$, and $\zeta$
and $\xi$ have one each; the book bounds the bad sets of the proof
system's other challenges, $x_1, \dots, x_4$, $x$ and those of the
committed rounds, by the degrees of the identities they test, the
largest being $n_g(n - 1)$, the bound on the constraint polynomial's
degree in each indeterminate, provided $n_g(n - 1) \ge 4n$, as at the
deployed $n_g = 9$, where $18423 > 4n = 8192$; the book admits
$n_g \ge 4$, for which this can fail.

\paragraph{Ghoshal and Tessaro, and the deployed stance.}
Ghoshal and Tessaro (\emph{Tight State-Restoration Soundness in the
Algebraic Group Model}) carry this analysis out for
Bulletproofs-style inner-product arguments: they prove tight
state-restoration soundness in the algebraic group model and derive
Fiat--Shamir security bounds from it under their stated hypotheses, the
transfer from the state-restoration game to the compiled argument being
their theorem. The deployed analysis follows them. The protocol
specification defines the proving system by reference to the halo2 book
(protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#halo2}{\S~5.4.10.3},
``Halo 2''), and the book's \emph{Protocol Description} chapter fixes
the framework: it adopts state-restoration soundness because the
concrete protocol is realised through the Fiat--Shamir transformation
and a cheating prover can fork the transcript at will, and it invokes
Ghoshal and Tessaro's theorem for the passage from that game to
soundness of the compiled argument. Its abstract interactive protocol is
stated for a relation of the form ``a fixed multivariate constraint
polynomial in the column polynomials and the challenges vanishes on the
rows'', with the column polynomials as the witness, over public
parameters of $n + 2$ group elements, $\mathbf G$, $U$ and the blinding
generator $H$ (written $W$ there).

\begin{definition}[State-restoration witness-extended emulation]
\label{def:ks-srwee}
Let $\Pi$ be a public-coin interactive argument for a relation $\Rel$
with $r$ challenges from a space $\mathcal C$. A
\emph{state-restoration prover} $\Prov^{*}$, given the public
parameters, outputs a statement $\phi$ and then queries an oracle on
pairs $(\tau, a)$, where $\tau$ is a transcript prefix the oracle has
recorded (initially only the empty one) and $a$ is a next prover
message; it may thus extend any earlier prefix. In the \emph{real} game
the oracle answers a message of rounds $1$ to $r$ with a fresh uniform
$c \in \mathcal C$ and records $(\tau, a, c)$, and answers a final
message with the verifier's decision on $(\tau, a)$, recording
$(\tau, a)$. In the \emph{ideal} game an algorithm $\Ext$, given the
public parameters and $\phi$ and keeping state across queries, receives
each query $(\tau, a)$, for an algebraic prover together with the
representations (Definition~\ref{def:ks-algebraic}) of the group
elements in $a$, returns the answer, which is recorded with the query,
and at the end outputs $w$. For a distinguisher
$\mathcal D$ given the recorded transcripts,
\[
\mathrm{Adv}^{\mathrm{srwee}}_{\Pi, \Rel}(\Prov^{*}, \mathcal D, \Ext)
:= \Pr[E_{\mathrm{real}}] - \Pr[E_{\mathrm{ideal}}],
\]
where $E_{\mathrm{real}}$ is the event that $\mathcal D$ outputs $1$ in
the real game, and $E_{\mathrm{ideal}}$ the event that $\mathcal D$
outputs $1$ in the ideal game and, if an accepting transcript was
recorded, $(\phi, w) \in \Rel$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Extraction for the abstract protocol; interface]
\label{thm:ks-srwee}
Let $\Pi$ be the halo2 book's abstract interactive argument for its
relation $\Rel$, over a group $\G$ of prime order with scalar field
$\F$, for $n$ rows, a positive integer $n_g$ with $n_g(n - 1) \ge 4n$
and a constraint polynomial of degree at most $n_g(n - 1)$ in each
indeterminate, with challenges from $\mathcal C$.
There is an extractor
$\Ext$ such that for every non-uniform algebraic state-restoration
prover $\Prov^{*}$ making at most $Q$ queries to its oracle there is an
adversary $\mathcal B$ with
\[
\mathrm{Adv}^{\mathrm{srwee}}_{\Pi, \Rel}(\Prov^{*}, \mathcal D, \Ext)
\;\le\; Q\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bad}}
  + \mathrm{Adv}^{\mathrm{dlrel}}_{\G, n+2}(\mathcal B),
\qquad
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bad}} \le \frac{n_g(n - 1)}{|\mathcal C|},
\]
for every computationally unbounded distinguisher $\mathcal D$. Here
$\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bad}}$ bounds the fraction of bad challenges after
every partial transcript, and
$\mathrm{Adv}^{\mathrm{dlrel}}_{\G, n+2}(\mathcal B)$ is the probability
that $\mathcal B$, given $n + 2$ uniform elements of $\G$, outputs a
nontrivial discrete-logarithm relation among them.
\end{theorem}

% ks_numbers.py:
% n_g = d_max = 9, n = 2048: n_g(n-1) = d_max(n-1) = 18423; 18423/p_Pallas = 2^(-239.8); over |Ch| = p - n - 1 (0 and the rows excluded): 2^(-239.8)
% Q = 2^80: Q * n_g(n-1)/|Ch| = 2^(-159.8)
The theorem is quoted as an interface and not proved in this volume: it
is the halo2 book's (\emph{Protocol Description}, ``Witness-extended
Emulation''), proved there by instantiating Theorem~1 of Ghoshal and
Tessaro with the bad-challenge sets described above. Its
extractor works online, from the representations of a single
transcript, and never rewinds. At the deployed $n_g = d_{\max} = 9$ and
$n = 2^{11}$, $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bad}} \le 18423/|\mathcal C| \approx
2^{-239.8}$, and $Q\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bad}} \approx 2^{-159.8}$ at
$Q = 2^{80}$ queries.

\paragraph{The instantiation gap, classified.}
Applying those bounds to the deployed Halo~2 transcript requires
matching the precise folding, masking, challenge distribution and
composition assumptions of the analysed protocol to the deployed one,
and this volume does not prove that instantiation theorem. Four
mismatches are visible from the earlier sections. The book's abstract
relation sees the permutation and lookup arguments of \S\ref{sec:arith}
only through the constraint polynomial and its committed columns; the
Orchard circuit's actual gates and table columns
(\S\ref{sec:halo2-practice}) and its chunked running products (``The
deployed chunking'' in \S\ref{sec:arith-perm}) instantiate that
abstraction and must be checked against its degree hypotheses. The
book's challenges exclude zero, the rows and, for each $u_j$, the value
that zeroes its factor of $g_{\mathrm{dep}}(x_3)$, while the deployed
transcript squeezes full field elements with no rejection
(\S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}), so the deployed challenge distribution differs
from the analysed one by the excluded events. The analysed folding is
the deployed normalisation of Remark~\ref{rem:ipa-deployed-norm}, and
the masking is the deployed mask of \S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}, so those two
match; the hash, however, is BLAKE2b (\S\ref{sec:halo2-fs}), and the
transfer theorem's random oracle must be instantiated by it. And the
book's theorem is for the interactive argument against a
state-restoration prover, the compiled bound following by the transfer
theorem under its hypotheses. In the classification of
\S\ref{sec:intro-claim}, the status of the transfer is
\emph{designed-but-unspecified}: the bounds exist for the analysed
protocol and the design intends them for the deployed one, but the
theorem that carries them across is not written, here or in the
specification.

\subsection{Concrete versus asymptotic security}
\label{sec:ks-concrete}

The \emph{Crypto Guide} distinguishes a reduction's existence from its
tightness (\S``Reductions'', the definition of loss and the remark on
why loss matters concretely) and reads security as a budget of bits
(\S``Concrete security and the bit-security budget''): a scheme
inherits the strength of its assumption only up to the loss of the
reduction that connects them. Two tightness gaps separate the proved
statements from the deployment.

\paragraph{The first gap: the naive lifting.}
% ks_numbers.py:
% d = 2^11 = 2048; ternary tree 3^11 = 177147 leaves; log_2 3 = 1.58496, d^(log_2 3) = 177147
% Q^(log d) at Q = 2^80, log d = 11: 2^880
The naive Fiat--Shamir knowledge-soundness analysis of the opening loses the
factor $Q^{\mu} > Q^{\log d}$ of \S\ref{sec:ks-noninteractive}, exponential in
the round count $\log d$ with base the query count $Q$: above $2^{880}$ at the
deployed size for a $2^{80}$-query prover, against a challenge space of fewer
than $2^{255}$ elements. The interactive extraction itself is polynomial,
$d^{\log_2 3} = 177147$ transcripts at $d = 2048$ in expected polynomial time,
with the proved knowledge error $O(\log d/|\F|)$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks}.
The gap lies in the naive analysis of the lifting, and the notions of
\S\ref{sec:ks-noninteractive} and the model of \S\ref{sec:ks-agm} exist to close
it.

\paragraph{The second gap: the model.}
% ks_numbers.py:
% Q = 2^80: Q * n_g(n-1)/|Ch| = 2^(-159.8)
Each analysis cited in this section that avoids the naive loss relies on
an idealisation: the halo2 book's extraction theorem
(Theorem~\ref{thm:ks-srwee}) on the algebraic group model, and its
passage to the compiled argument on the random oracle model as well;
the special-soundness analysis of Attema, Fehr and Kloo\ss{} on the
random oracle model alone. No standard-model tight bound is claimed for the
deployment, by this volume or by the analyses it cites; this is a
statement about the design's analyses, not a specification claim, and
the specification makes none. Read against the bit-security budget, the
consequence is concrete. The discrete-logarithm layer offers about
$2^{126}$ operations of classical security (\S\ref{sec:ipa-binding};
\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Standing assumptions''); a reduction tight in
$Q$ lets the argument inherit that strength, less the algebraic term
$Q\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{bad}}$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:ks-srwee}, about
$2^{-159.8}$ at $2^{80}$ queries, which is not the bottleneck; a
reduction losing $2^{880}$ lets it inherit nothing. For the analysed
protocol the number is therefore the assumption's, in the algebraic group
and random oracle models; for the deployed argument it is so only through
the instantiation that \S\ref{sec:ks-agm} classifies
designed-but-unspecified.

\paragraph{The stance.}
Proved without idealisation of the adversary or of the transcript hash
are the interactive statement, Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks}, for uniform
generators (the deployed hashed generators add its hypothesis that
hash-to-curve is a random oracle), and the information-theoretic layer of
\S\ref{sec:arith}; described, with the hypotheses named, are the layered
extraction that joins them and the naive lifting's loss; cited, with its
instantiation to the deployed transcript classified
designed-but-unspecified, is the halo2 book's bound in the algebraic
group model.

% 06-verdict.tex -- sec:verdict. This section proves nothing new: it
% assembles, by reference, results proved in sec:arith, sec:ipa and
% sec:halo2 and weighed in sec:ks. Every number recalled is a toy_trace.py,
% ipa_toy.py, protocol_params.py, orchard_params.py or proof_bytes.py output
% already printed in an earlier section; the relevant lines of
% compute/OUTPUT.md are repeated as comments above each passage that uses
% them. The deployed column and lookup counts are the proof_bytes.py
% counts, as sec:halo2 and sec:ipa recorded them.

\section{The verifier's decision and its implication chain}
\label{sec:verdict}

This section adds no theorem: it lists the verifier's checks in the
order he performs them, assembles the implication chain from an accepted
proof back to the claim, each link labelled by the statement that
proves it and by its error term, repeats the chain for the Orchard
Action circuit, and maps the five reductions of \S\ref{sec:intro-claim}
to the formal statements that own them. Nothing here depends on the
recursion of \S\ref{sec:pcd}, which Orchard does not deploy.

\subsection{The verifier's checks, read in order}
\label{sec:verdict-checks}

\paragraph{What he holds.}
The verifier of the toy holds the fixed data of the circuit, the five
selector polynomials and the permuted-label polynomials of
Construction~\ref{con:arith-perm}; the public input, the column
$(0, 0, 0, -35)$ interpolated to $\mathrm{PI}(X)$
(\S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}); and the proof. The proof is a byte string
(\S\ref{sec:halo2-fs}): commitments, claimed evaluations, and one
opening, in the order of the phases of \S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol}
(Table~\ref{tab:halo2-phases}), counted by kind in
Table~\ref{tab:proof-bytes}. He never sees a column polynomial, a
blinder or the advice; he sees curve points and field elements, and he
performs the checks below in this order.

\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{The challenges.} He first commits to the instance column
  himself, from the public input and with the fixed blinder $1$
  (\S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}), so that the public input never comes
  from the prover. He then replays the transcript
  (\S\ref{sec:halo2-fs}): he absorbs the key digest, that instance
  commitment and each prover message in turn, and squeezes every
  challenge at its prescribed point. In the toy the point $z = 20$ was
  drawn by decree; in the deployed protocol the evaluation point $x$
  is a hash of everything before it.
% toy_trace.py, block "z = 20":
% Z_H(20) = 46, t(20) = 67, G(20) = 75; t(20) Z_H(20) = 75: accepted
% cheat at z = 20: G*(20) = 20 != t*(20) Z_H(20) = 64: rejected
\item \textbf{The identity at the point.} From the claimed evaluations
  and the fixed polynomials he evaluates himself, he forms both sides
  of the gate identity. In the toy this is the one field
  equation~\eqref{eq:arith-check}: $G(20) = 75$ against
  $t(20)\,\ZH(20) = 67 \cdot 46 = 75$, accepted; the $c_0 = 10$ cheat
  of Example~\ref{ex:arith-cheat} gives $20$ against $64$, rejected.
  In the deployed protocol the identity is $C(x) = h(x)\,\ZH(x)$, with
  $C$ the $y$-combination of every constraint polynomial and $h$ the
  chunked quotient, and it is enforced by opening the collapsed
  quotient commitment at $x$ to the value $C(x)/\ZH(x)$ that the
  verifier computes himself from the evaluations of phase~5, inside the
  multipoint opening (phases~6 and~7 of \S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol}).
% toy_trace.py, block "permutation argument":
% grand product prod (v + beta label + gamma) / (v + beta sigma-label + gamma) at beta = gamma = 2: honest = 1; with b_1 = 4: 61
\item \textbf{The wiring.} He evaluates the boundary and update identities
  of the permutation argument~\eqref{eq:arith-perm-rules} at the point
  and, in the deployed form, the chunk-boundary identities of
  \S\ref{sec:arith-perm} and the boundary identity
  $\ell_{\mathrm{last}}\,(Z^2 - Z)$ of Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows}.
  In the toy the ratio of the two products~\eqref{eq:arith-perm-product}
  at $\beta = \gamma = 2$ is $1$ for the honest trace and $61$ for the
  tampered $b_1 = 4$ (Example~\ref{ex:arith-perm-toy}).
% toy_trace.py, block "lookup":
% multiset check at (beta, gamma) = (5, 7): prod (A' + beta)(S' + gamma) = 82 = prod (A + beta)(S + gamma) = 82
% forged A = [2, 7, 2, 2]: over all 4 arrangements A' and all 24 reshuffles S', row checks passing: 0
\item \textbf{The tables.} He evaluates the four lookup
  identities~\eqref{eq:arith-lookup-rules} and, in the deployed form,
  the boundary identity $\ell_{\mathrm{last}}\,(Z^2 - Z)$ of
  Remark~\ref{rem:active-rows} at the point, five for each lookup
  argument. The toy's grid has none; the two-bit check of
  Example~\ref{ex:arith-lookup-toy} showed the shape, with both
  products equal to $82$ and the forged $7$ stranded in every
  arrangement. In the deployed protocol items~3 and~4 are not separate
  checks: their identities are summands of $C$ and are enforced by the
  one check of item~2; only the toy checks them separately.
% ipa_toy.py, block "(1) symmetric fold": LHS = (54, 39), RHS = (54, 39); equal: True
% false v' = 60: LHS becomes (62, 15) != RHS (54, 39): rejected
% ipa_toy.py, block "(3) deployed opening": verifier ... = (5, 72) = [c]G^(0) + [c g_dep(20) zeta]U + [f]H = (5, 72); equal: True
% false v' = 60: LHS (67, 31) != RHS (5, 72): rejected
\item \textbf{The openings.} Every evaluation he received for the three
  checks above was \emph{claimed}. He now binds each claim to its
  commitment: the multipoint reduction of
  Construction~\ref{con:halo2-multipoint} folds every claim into one
  statement, and the inner-product equation~\eqref{eq:ipa-dep-final}
  discharges it with one multi-scalar multiplication of length $n$. In
  the toy, the opening of $a(X)$ at $z = 20$ to the value $59$ closes at
  $(54, 39)$ on both sides (Table~\ref{tab:ipa-toy-verifier}) and the
  false value $60$ moves the left-hand side to $(62, 15)$; the deployed
  opening of the same commitment closes at $(5, 72)$ and rejects $60$
  likewise (Table~\ref{tab:ipa-toy-deployed}). Among the claims is
  $\pi(x)$, which the opening binds to his own instance commitment
  (\S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}).
\end{enumerate}
If every check passes he accepts. The accepted checks imply the
following chain, read backwards from the last check to the first;
Figure~\ref{fig:verdict} draws it.

\paragraph{The chain, read backwards.}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=3pt]
\item \emph{The identity check passed at his random point.} Because the
  commitment binds, the polynomials behind the openings were fixed
  before the point was chosen: the Pedersen vector commitment is
  binding under the discrete logarithm on Vesta (the \emph{Crypto
  Guide}, \S``Pedersen vector commitments'', recalled in
  \S\ref{sec:arith-commit}), and the opening argument is knowledge
  sound (Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} and
  Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks}), so from any prover who passes the
  opening an extractor recovers the committed vector and the value it
  opens to, or else a discrete-logarithm relation among the hashed
  generators; its knowledge error is $O(\log d/|\F|)$, and the
  multipoint reduction that fed it adds one Schwartz--Zippel allowance
  of order $n/|\F|$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-multipoint}). With a
  hash in place of coins the ordering holds by construction, every
  commitment being an input of the hash that produced the challenge
  testing it (\S\ref{sec:halo2-fs}). Because the point was random and a
  false identity disagrees with the true one at all but $\deg D$
  points, passing supports $G(X) = t(X)\,\ZH(X)$ as polynomials except
  with the error $\deg D/|\F|$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-random} for
  this fixed check:
% toy_trace.py: roots of D in F_97: [22, 75, 96] = H minus {1}: Pr[random z hides this cheat] = 3/97 = 0.0309
% toy_trace.py: general bound: deg G* = 9, deg t* <= 2n - 1 = 7 (two pieces of degree < n = 4): deg D <= 11: 11/97 = 0.1134
% toy_trace.py: at Orchard scale: deg of the identity ~ 2^14, |F| = p_Pallas ~ 2^254.000: 2^14 / p = 2^(-240.0)
  $11/97$ for the toy's degree budget, $3/97$ for the particular cheat
  of Example~\ref{ex:arith-random-toy}, about $2^{-240}$ at Orchard
  scale, one term in the sense \S\ref{sec:intro-claim} fixed. In the
  deployed form the identity is $C = h\,\ZH$, and the challenge $y$
  that combined every constraint into $C$ costs one more term,
  $(T - 1)/|\F|$ for $T$ constraint polynomials
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-y-batch}).
\item \emph{The identity holds as polynomials.} Then $\ZH \mid G$, so
  $G$ vanishes on every row (Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-quotient},
  equivalence~\eqref{eq:arith-divis}), so every gate equation holds at
  every row (Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-gate-vanish}). Both steps are exact
  equivalences with no error term: in the toy, the four rows of
  Table~\ref{tab:arith-trace} each satisfy~\eqref{eq:arith-gate}. In
  the deployed form every constraint polynomial combined into $C$
  vanishes on the active rows: every custom gate, and the permutation
  and lookup identities that the next two links consume.
\item \emph{The permutation identities passed.} Except with the error
  $2N/|\F|$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-perm} over $\beta, \gamma$, with
  $N$ the number of participating cells, twelve in the toy, and, in the
  deployed form, $N/|\F|$ more for the challenges at which a product
  factor vanishes (Corollary~\ref{cor:arith-perm-deployed}),
  the copy constraints hold: the
  per-row gates are wired into one coherent computation, the witness
  $3$ of row~$0$ is the witness of rows~$1$ and~$2$, and each row's
  output is the next row's input. The lookup identities passed likewise:
  except with the error $4n/|\F|$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-lookup}, and
  $n(m - 1)/|\F|$ more for the $\theta$-compression
  of~\eqref{eq:arith-theta} (Proposition~\ref{prop:arith-theta}) when a
  lookup has $m > 1$ columns, every looked-up value is in its table.
\item \emph{The public input fixed the target at $35$.} In the toy,
  $35$ enters the gate polynomial $G$ through $\mathrm{PI}(X)$, which the
  verifier interpolates himself (\S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}), so it
  enters the identity of link~1. In the deployed circuit the instance
  polynomial $\pi(X)$ of Definition~\ref{def:halo2-instance-poly} is
  the verifier's own: he committed to it from the values he intends to
  verify against, and its designated cells are copy-constrained to the
  advice cells that carry the same values, so the permutation of
  link~3 forces agreement. There is no error term of its own. The
  relation certified is the one at the public input the verifier
  supplied (\S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}).
\end{enumerate}
Together with the observation that closed \S\ref{sec:arith-grid}, that
filling the advice columns \emph{is} knowing the witness, because those cells
are the wires carrying it and its powers, the four links say: there
exists an assignment of the advice columns, a value of the witness, making the
entire arithmetisation of $X^3 + X + 5 = 35$ hold, at the public $35$.

\paragraph{From existence to knowledge.}
The chain so far establishes that satisfying advice \emph{exists}. That
the prover \emph{knows} it is the extraction question. The extractor of
Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} and Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks}
recovers, from any prover who passes the opening, the committed vector
and its blinder: it establishes knowledge of committed polynomial data,
and nothing more. It does not by itself
prove that those polynomials encode a satisfying circuit witness; that
is the upper layer's task in the layered extraction of
Remark~\ref{rem:halo2-compile}, where the commitment scheme's extractor
turns commitments into polynomials and the polynomial IOP's extractor
turns polynomials into a witness. Under the composed hypotheses named
there, a round-by-round or state-restoration knowledge-soundness
theorem for the IOP, extractability and evaluation binding for the
commitment scheme, and a multi-round Fiat--Shamir theorem for the transcript, an
extractor for the \emph{full} protocol obtains such a witness;
\S\ref{sec:ks} weighs those hypotheses against the deployed transcript,
and in particular states in which model the tight bound is proved.

\paragraph{What he has not learnt.}
For the interactive protocol and an honest verifier the transcript
reveals nothing beyond the truth of the statement
(Proposition~\ref{prop:halo2-zk},
cited, and Corollary~\ref{cor:halo2-zk-deployed}); for the
non-interactive deployed proof this is stated, not proved
(\S\ref{sec:halo2-zk}). Every
commitment the prover sent is a uniform group element (perfect hiding,
recalled in \S\ref{sec:halo2-zk}); the verifying key's and the instance
commitments carry the fixed blinding factor $1$ and commit only public
data; the evaluations
of each blinded column are uniform by
Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-blinding-rows}; the final scalar of the
opening, which folding alone would have leaked as a linear form of the
coefficients (Remark~\ref{rem:ipa-not-zk}), is masked by the random
polynomial $s(X)$ vanishing at the opening point
(\S\ref{sec:ipa-zk}, in the deployed form of \S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}); and
the joint transcript is simulated by the
witness-free simulator of Proposition~\ref{prop:halo2-zk}, within the
distance of Corollary~\ref{cor:halo2-zk-deployed} for the deployed
challenges.
% toy_trace.py, block "the toy leaks its witness":
% roots of x^3 + x + 5 = 35 in F_97: [3, 18, 76]; a(20) for each: [59, 40, 6]
% x in F_97 with a_x(20) = 59 (advice column (x, x^2, x^3, x^3 + x)): [3] (1 of 97)
The toy has no blinding rows and is not zero knowledge: its claimed
value $a(20) = 59$ alone singles out the witness $3$ among the three
roots $3, 18, 76$ of $X^3 + X + 5 = 35$ in $\F_{97}$, and indeed among all
$97$ candidates; the blinding rows of
Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-blinding-rows} are what make the deployed
evaluations uniform. The deployed mask makes the opening's own scalar a
fresh uniform value:
% ipa_toy.py, block (3): second mask m' = [32, 68, 64, 88]: c' = 46 != c = 20 (same challenges); both accepted
$c = 20$ under one mask (Table~\ref{tab:ipa-toy-deployed}) and $c' = 46$
under another (\S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}), both accepted.

\paragraph{The error budget.}
The verifier has checked a small transcript, $2720 + 2272\,a$ bytes for
$a$ Actions (Table~\ref{tab:proof-bytes}), and a logarithmic opening
% orchard_params.py: k = 11 rounds; 11 pairs (L_j, R_j) = 22 points; plus S: 2k + 1 = 23 points
% toy_trace.py: Orchard scale: epsilon = 2^-240, Q = 2^64: Q epsilon = 2^-176
of $2k + 1 = 23$ points. Each link of the chain carries its own error
term, which bounds the failure of that link alone: the identity's
$\deg D/|\F|$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-random}); the $y$-combination's
$(T - 1)/|\F|$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-y-batch}); the permutation's
$2N/|\F|$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-perm}) and $N/|\F|$ for the challenges
at which a product factor vanishes
(Corollary~\ref{cor:arith-perm-deployed});
the lookup's $4n/|\F|$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-lookup}) and the
$\theta$-compression's $n(m - 1)/|\F|$
(Proposition~\ref{prop:arith-theta}); the multipoint reduction's
allowance of order $n/|\F|$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-multipoint}); and the
opening's $O(\log d/|\F|)$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} and
Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks}). Combining them
into one knowledge error for the protocol is the compilation principle
of Remark~\ref{rem:halo2-compile}, whose hypotheses \S\ref{sec:ks} names
and does not discharge; no sum of the terms is asserted. Beyond these
terms lie the loss of replacing coins by a hash, of which the grinding
route alone multiplies a term by the query budget $Q$, so that
$2^{-240}$ becomes $2^{-176}$ after $2^{64}$ attempts
(\S\ref{sec:halo2-fs}, Proposition~\ref{prop:halo2-grinding}), while
the multi-round transform's full loss is weighed in \S\ref{sec:ks},
which asserts no factor-$Q$ bound for the compiled protocol; and the
assumptions beneath the chain, the discrete logarithm on Vesta that
makes the commitments bind and the extractor's alternative output
infeasible (Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} and
Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks}), the random-oracle model for the
transcript hash, and the extraction model in which the non-interactive
bound is proved (\S\ref{sec:ks}). The isolated $2^{-240}$ is not the
verdict's error: it is one Schwartz--Zippel term for one fixed lie, read
as \S\ref{sec:intro-claim} fixed.

\begin{figure}[htb]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\footnotesize,
  st/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, inner sep=5pt, align=flush center,
    text width=6.0cm, line width=0.8pt},
  act/.style={st, draw=tierfull, fill=tierfull!10},
  alg/.style={st, draw=tierin, fill=tierin!8},
  con/.style={st, draw=tierrecv, fill=tierrecv!10},
  lab/.style={font=\scriptsize, align=flush left, text width=7.1cm,
    anchor=west, inner sep=2pt},
  assum/.style={lab, text=tierspend!80!black},
  exact/.style={lab, text=tierin!70!black},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2.2mm]}, line width=0.9pt, black!70},
  tie/.style={black!35, line width=0.5pt}]
  \node[act] (A) at (0,0) {\textbf{accepted.} Transcript replayed and
    every challenge recomputed (\S\ref{sec:halo2-fs}); the gate
    identity $C(x) = h(x)\,\ZH(x)$, whose summands include the
    permutation and lookup identities at $x$, the multipoint reduction
    and the inner-product equation~\eqref{eq:ipa-dep-final} all pass
    (\S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol}).};
  \node[alg, below=8mm of A] (B) {every claimed evaluation is the
    value, at the\\hashed point, of a polynomial fixed before\\that point
    was drawn};
  \node[alg, below=8mm of B] (C) {the identity holds as polynomials,
    $C = h\,\ZH$: every constraint polynomial vanishes on the rows};
  \node[alg, below=8mm of C] (D) {every gate holds at every row; the
    copy constraints hold; every looked-up value is in its table};
  \node[con, below=8mm of D] (E) {advice exists satisfying every gate,\\
    every wire and every table at the public\\input the verifier chose:
    filling the advice\\columns is knowing the witness
    (\S\ref{sec:arith-grid})};
  \node[con, below=8mm of E] (F) {the prover knows the witness $3$; what the
    verifier learns is bounded as \S\ref{sec:halo2-zk} classifies};
  \foreach \p/\q in {A/B, B/C, C/D, D/E, E/F}
    \draw[arr] (\p.south) -- (\q.north);
  \coordinate (mAB) at ($(A.south)!0.5!(B.north)$);
  \coordinate (mBC) at ($(B.south)!0.5!(C.north)$);
  \coordinate (mCD) at ($(C.south)!0.5!(D.north)$);
  \coordinate (mDE) at ($(D.south)!0.5!(E.north)$);
  \coordinate (mEF) at ($(E.south)!0.5!(F.north)$);
  \node[assum] (lAB) at ($(mAB)+(3.3cm,0)$) {the openings bind:
    Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} and Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks},
    knowledge error $O(\log d/|\F|)$, under the discrete logarithm on
    Vesta; the multipoint reduction, one term of order $n/|\F|$
    (Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-multipoint}); a hash in place of
    coins: the grinding factor $Q$ (\S\ref{sec:halo2-fs}) and the
    multi-round loss weighed in \S\ref{sec:ks}};
  \node[exact] (lBC) at ($(mBC)+(3.3cm,0)$) {Schwartz--Zippel:
    Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-random}, error $\deg D/|\F|$, which is
    $11/97$ for the toy's degree budget and about $2^{-240}$ at Orchard
    scale; the $y$-combination: Lemma~\ref{lem:halo2-y-batch},
    $(T - 1)/|\F|$};
  \node[exact] (lCD) at ($(mCD)+(3.3cm,0)$) {gates:
    Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-quotient} and
    Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-gate-vanish}, exact. wiring:
    Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-perm}, $2N/|\F|$ over $\beta, \gamma$, plus
    $N/|\F|$ for a vanishing factor in the deployed form
    (Corollary~\ref{cor:arith-perm-deployed}).
    tables: Theorem~\ref{thm:arith-lookup}, $4n/|\F|$, plus
    $n(m-1)/|\F|$ for $\theta$};
  \node[exact] (lDE) at ($(mDE)+(3.3cm,0)$) {the instance: in the toy,
    $\mathrm{PI}(X)$, interpolated by the verifier; deployed, the
    verifier's own commitment to $\pi$, blinding factor $1$, absorbed
    before the first challenge, and the copy constraints from its designated
    cells (\S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}); no error term of its own};
  \node[assum] (lEF) at ($(mEF)+(3.3cm,0)$) {existence becomes
    knowledge under the hypotheses of Remark~\ref{rem:halo2-compile},
    weighed in \S\ref{sec:ks}; what else is learnt is bounded by the
    interactive protocol's zero knowledge (\S\ref{sec:halo2-zk}, cited),
    stated, not proved, for the deployed proof};
  \foreach \m/\l in {mAB/lAB, mBC/lBC, mCD/lCD, mDE/lDE, mEF/lEF}
    \draw[tie] (\m) -- (\l.west);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The implication chain from the verifier's accepted checks
(amber) back to the claim (green), read downwards; each arrow carries
the statement that proves it and its error term. Blue labels are
algebraic links whose only losses are Schwartz--Zippel terms or none;
red labels are the two links that rest on an assumption or a model
hypothesis, the binding of the openings at the top and the extraction
of the witness at the bottom.}
\label{fig:verdict}
\end{figure}

\subsection{The Action circuit at deployed scale}
\label{sec:verdict-orchard}

Nothing in the chain used that the statement was small. Replace the
four gates by the constraints of the Orchard Action statement, the nine
conditions the protocol specification imposes on the public input and
the witness (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actionstatement}{\S~4.18.4},
``Action Statement (Orchard)''): the integrity of the old and new note
commitments, the Merkle path from the spent commitment to the anchor
unless the spent value is $0$ (the \emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Merkle trees
and commitments to sets''),
the opening of the net value commitment, a Pedersen commitment to the
value difference (the \emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``The Pedersen
commitment''), the nullifier's derivation, the spend authority, that
the randomised verification key $\rk$ is the re-randomisation of the
spend validating key $\aksym$ by a witnessed randomiser (the
\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Key re-randomisation and unlinkability''), the
integrity of the diversified address, that the spent note's address
$(\gd, \pkd)$ satisfies $\pkd = [\mathsf{ivk}]\,\gd$ for the incoming
viewing key $\mathsf{ivk}$ derived in the witness from $\aksym$ and
further key material (the \emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Shielded-note
delivery as deployed hybrid encryption'') unless that derivation fails,
and the two enable flags,
which force the spent value to $0$ unless $\mathsf{enableSpends} = 1$
and the created value to $0$ unless $\mathsf{enableOutputs} = 1$. From
NU6.3 the circuit checks a tenth condition on the further public bit
$\mathsf{disableCrossAddress}$
(\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actiondesc}{\S~4.6};
\href{https://zips.z.cash/zip-0258}{ZIP~258}): when it is set, the
receivers $(\gd, \pkd)$ of the spent and created notes coincide
(\S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}). Each condition unfolds into the gates of the
deployed circuit
(\S\ref{sec:halo2-practice}), and the grid grows

% proof_bytes.py: 10 advice column commitments (one per advice column); A', S' and a running product for each of 3 lookups; 29 fixed column evaluations (one per fixed column); 1 instance column evaluation
% protocol_params.py: D = 9, n = 2^11 = 2048: deg h <= (D-1)(n-1) - 1 = 16375; quotient chunks D - 1 = 8; permutation: 15 equality columns in chunks of D - 2 = 7: 3 running products (chunk sizes [7, 7, 1]); IPA: 2 log_2 n + 1 = 23 points
% proof_bytes.py: per-Action 49 scalars (1 instance + 25 advice + 8 permutation + 15 lookup evaluations); shared 29 + 15 + 1 = 45 evaluations at x; a = 1: 4992 bytes
accordingly (Table~\ref{tab:verdict-scale}): the four rows become
$2^{11} = 2048$; the one universal gate of degree $3$ becomes custom
gates reading rotations, under the degree bound $d_{\max} = 9$, the
incomplete addition of Theorem~\ref{thm:halo2-addinc} among them; the
quotient $t$ of degree $5$ becomes $h$ of degree up to $16375$, cut
into $8$ chunks (Theorem~\ref{thm:halo2-quotient-degree}); the
four-class permutation on twelve cells becomes fifteen equality-enabled
columns in three running products; a Sinsemilla generator table of
$2^{10}$ rows, reached by three lookup arguments, joins the
permutation (\S\ref{sec:halo2-lookups}; the hash is the \emph{Crypto
Guide}'s, \S``Sinsemilla: an algebraic hash-based commitment''); the
four openings at $z$ become $49$ claimed evaluations per Action and
$45$ shared (Table~\ref{tab:halo2-phases}), collapsed into one opening
of $23$ points; and the point
$z = 20$ drawn by decree becomes an $x$ squeezed from the BLAKE2b
transcript. The spine is identical: arithmetise into a grid,
interpolate the columns, compress ``all rows correct'' into one
identity, $G = t\,\ZH$ in the toy and $C = h\,\ZH$ in the deployed
protocol, commit, and test at a random point.

\paragraph{The algebraic core of Action verification.}
For the verification of an Action proof that the consensus rules require
(protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actiondesc}{\S~4.6},
``Action Descriptions''), the randomised polynomial-identity test of
link~1 is the algebraic core: a Schwartz--Zippel argument on one
identity at one hashed point. The full verifier of \S\ref{sec:halo2-protocol}
evaluates that identity's permutation and lookup summands from the claimed
evaluations, binds every claimed evaluation through the multipoint reduction,
recomputes every transcript challenge, and closes the single inner-product
equation with its one multi-scalar multiplication of length $n$, before
concluding that the circuit's constraints hold at the public input it verified
against, the anchor, the net value commitment, the nullifier, the randomised
verification key, the extracted note commitment and the flags of
\S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}.
Under the knowledge soundness whose status \S\ref{sec:ks} classifies
(designed-but-unspecified for the deployed transcript), an accepted
Action proof certifies, for a known witness, that the constraints of the
circuit whose verifying key the consensus rules fix (protocol
specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actiondesc}{\S~4.6})
hold. That these imply the conditions of the Action statement (protocol
specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actionstatement}{\S~4.18.4})
is a property of that circuit, which this volume does not verify and
which the specification records as having failed for the Orchard circuit
deployed before NU6.2 (the same section); value
balance additionally requires the binding signature (protocol
specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#orchardbalance}{\S~4.14},
``Balance and Binding Signature (Orchard)''), and privacy rests also on
components outside the proof, such as note encryption.

\paragraph{What the formal treatment supplies.}
Each of the five reductions of \S\ref{sec:intro-claim}, ``The chain of
reductions'', is owned by a formal statement.
Table~\ref{tab:verdict-map} lists, reduction by reduction, what the toy
exhibited and which statement carries it; the last two rows point
beyond the spine, to the extractor that turns existence into knowledge
and to the recursion, which Orchard does not deploy and on which nothing
above depends.

% 07-pcd.tex -- sec:pcd. Every number in this section is read from
% compute/OUTPUT.md, blocks toy_accumulate.py and pasta_cycle.py; the
% relevant lines are repeated as comments above each passage that uses them.
% Classification (sec:intro-claim): the Pasta 2-cycle and Orchard's use of
% Pallas inside a Vesta-committed circuit are specified; recursion and
% accumulation over the cycle are designed-but-unspecified; BCMS20 Theorem
% 7.1 (cited) and Proposition prop:pcd-fold (proved) are theorems.

\section{Recursive proof composition and accumulation schemes}
\label{sec:pcd}

This section states recursive proof composition and accumulation for
the inner-product argument; Orchard deploys neither. The protocol
specification uses Halo~2 for one purpose, to prove and verify Action
statements (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#abstractzk}{\S~4.1.13},
``Zero-Knowledge Proving System''), and requires every Action proof to be
valid
(\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#actiondesc}{\S~4.6},
``Action Descriptions''); the design proposal that introduced the pool
states that it makes no use of the proof system's support for recursive
proofs (\href{https://zips.z.cash/zip-0224}{ZIP~224}, under ``Proving
system''). A verifier holding several proofs may still check them
together. The complete verification equation $E_i$ of each proof is a
multi-scalar multiplication over all $d$ generators that should evaluate
to $\mathcal O$ (\S\ref{sec:ipa-halo-trick}, ``Batch verification''); for
independent uniform
$\mu_1, \dots, \mu_b \in \F$ the verifier of $b$ proofs checks
$\sum_i [\mu_i]\,E_i = \mathcal O$ instead. If some $E_j \ne \mathcal O$,
then $E_j$ generates the group of prime order, so for any fixed values
of the other factors exactly one $\mu_j$ satisfies the combined check, and
a false batch passes with probability $1/|\F|$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:ipa-batch}). Batch verification verifies the batch and
emits nothing; it is not the accumulation developed here. In the
classification of \S\ref{sec:intro-claim}, the Pasta curves and the use
Orchard makes of them (\S\ref{sec:pcd-cycle}) are \emph{specified};
recursion and accumulation over those curves are
\emph{designed-but-unspecified}: their constructions are published and
the halo2 book describes them (\emph{Recursion} chapter), and ZIP~224
leaves them to future protocol updates, but no specification defines a
recursive statement or an accumulation rule for Zcash.
Theorem~\ref{thm:pcd-bcms} and Proposition~\ref{prop:pcd-fold} below are
theorems, the first cited and the second proved.

\subsection{Recursion and its two classical obstacles}
\label{sec:pcd-obstacles}

\paragraph{The setting.}
Consider a computation iterated many times,
\[
z_m = f^{(m)}(z_0) := f\bigl(f(\cdots f(z_0)\cdots)\bigr),
\]
one step $f$ applied $m$ times to an initial state, and suppose a
verifier wants to be sure that $z_m$ is the honest result without
recomputing the $m$ steps. Valiant's \emph{incrementally verifiable
computation} asks for a proof of $z_m$ that is updated step by step,
each update costing about one step's worth of proving and the finished
proof no larger than the proof of a single step. The more general
setting, in which computations branch and merge and each step consumes
the proofs of its predecessors, is the \emph{proof-carrying data} of
Chiesa and Tromer: every message in a distributed computation carries a
proof that it was produced correctly from messages that themselves
carried such proofs.

\begin{remark}[A circuit has a fixed size]\label{rem:pcd-fixed-size}
A PLONKish circuit (Definition~\ref{def:plonkish}) fixes its $n$ rows,
its columns, its gates and the permutation $\sigma$, and the verifying
key is a deterministic function of these and the commitment parameters
(\S\ref{sec:halo2-practice}, ``Key and witness''); for the Action circuit
$n = 2^{11}$. Every proof under one key therefore attests to a
computation laid out in those $n$ rows. The circuit computes nothing: the
prover supplies every advice value, and the gates check them. A branch on
a witness value lays out every arm, and an advice bit $c$, constrained by
$c\,(1 - c) = 0$, selects the result; a loop is unrolled to a bound fixed
with the circuit, one copy of its body per iteration. One circuit
therefore expresses the statement $z_m = f^{(m)}(z_0)$ only for $m$ up to
a bound fixed with its key. A single proof of a computation of unbounded
length needs the recursive composition that follows, in which one circuit
for a single step is proved as often as the computation runs.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{The construction.}
The natural construction is \emph{recursive proof composition}: proving,
inside one proof, that another proof verifies. Write $\Ver$ for the
verifier of a non-interactive argument (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``SNARKs:
succinct non-interactive arguments of knowledge''), and suppose its
algorithm is itself arithmetised, in the sense of \S\ref{sec:arith},
into a PLONKish circuit of Definition~\ref{def:plonkish}, the
\emph{verifier circuit}: its instance columns hold the statement and
its advice columns hold a proof, and its gates are satisfiable exactly
when $\Ver$ accepts that proof of that statement. At step $i + 1$ the
prover proves the compound statement
\begin{quote}
\emph{``$z_{i+1} = f(z_i)$, and there exists a proof $\pi_i$ that the
verifier circuit accepts for the statement of step $i$.''}
\end{quote}
The proof $\pi_i$ is advice of the step-$(i+1)$ circuit, never sent
onward; the proof $\pi_{i+1}$ that results attests to step $i + 1$ and,
through the embedded verifier, to $\pi_i$, hence to step $i$, and so on
down to $z_0$. One proof attests to the entire history, and the cost of
producing $\pi_{i+1}$ is the cost of one step of $f$ plus one run of
the verifier circuit, independent of how long the history is. The
requirement on which everything rests is the one just used: the
argument's verifier must be efficiently expressible as a circuit. A
verifier whose circuit is as large as the computation it checks would
make each step as expensive as the whole history, and the construction
would buy nothing.

\paragraph{Two obstacles.}
The classical instantiations meet that requirement badly, in two
different ways.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt]
\item \emph{Pairings in a circuit, and a ceremony.} The pairing-based
  systems, Groth16, which Sapling deploys, and PLONK compiled with the
  KZG commitment (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``SNARKs: succinct
  non-interactive arguments of knowledge'' for Groth16 and the bilinear
  pairing; \S``Polynomial commitment schemes'', under ``KZG: evaluation
  in the exponent''), have constant-size proofs and verifiers that run
  in time independent of the circuit. But those verifiers evaluate
  pairings, and a pairing lands in a target group that lives in an
  extension field $\F_{p^{e}}$ of the curve's base field, of degree $e$
  equal to the curve's embedding degree (\emph{Crypto Guide},
  \S``Instantiation on elliptic curves; the Pasta curves'', the
  large-embedding-degree criterion). A circuit whose native arithmetic
  is one prime field must emulate arithmetic in another field with
  many gates per operation, so a pairing is costly to express inside a
  circuit. These particular systems also need a trusted setup, the
  ceremony that \S\ref{sec:ipa-halo-trick} contrasted with hashed
  generators.
\item \emph{A linear-time verifier.} The transparent systems built on
  the inner-product argument of \S\ref{sec:ipa} need no pairing and no
  ceremony, but their verifier is linear in the size of the committed
  vectors: the one multi-scalar multiplication $G^{(0)} =
  \ip{\mathbf s}{\mathbf G}$ of length $d$ that \S\ref{sec:ipa-verifier}
  singled out. Inside a verifier circuit that multiplication is $d$
  scalar multiplications of curve points, each a double-and-add with
  $\ell - 1$ doublings for an $\ell$-bit scalar (\emph{Math Guide},
  \S``Scalar multiplication and double-and-add''), so the verifier
  circuit grows linearly in $d$, the length of the committed vectors of
  the proof it verifies. A linear-time check dominates the verifier circuit and
  negates the per-step economy that recursion was meant to deliver.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Deferred verification.}
Halo, the construction of Bowe, Grigg and Hopwood (\emph{Halo: Recursive
Proof Composition without a Trusted Setup}) from which Halo~2 descends,
\emph{defers} the linear-time part of verification instead of
recomputing it at every step, and uses the inner-product argument, in
which no pairing occurs. B\"unz, Chiesa, Mishra and Spooner formalised
the deferral as an \emph{accumulation scheme} (\emph{Proof-Carrying Data
from Accumulation Schemes}; BCMS20), the notion the next subsection
defines; their Theorem~7.1 is quoted below, without proof, as
Theorem~\ref{thm:pcd-bcms}.

\subsection{Accumulation schemes}
\label{sec:pcd-acc}

The idea is to separate what a verifier must check at each step from
what can be checked once, at the end of a chain. Fix a
\emph{predicate} $\Phi$, a property of an input $q$ decidable in
polynomial time, for example ``this inner-product opening is correct''
of a transcript of Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit}. An accumulation
scheme replaces a sequence of checks of $\Phi$ by a sequence of
accumulation steps and one run of a decider.

\begin{definition}[Accumulation scheme]\label{def:pcd-acc}
Let $\lambda$ be the security parameter (\emph{Crypto Guide},
\S``Adversaries and the security parameter'') and $\Phi$ a predicate on
inputs $q$. An \emph{accumulation scheme} for $\Phi$ is a triple
$(\mathsf P, \mathsf V, \mathsf D)$ of polynomial-time algorithms with
access to a common random oracle, whose \emph{accumulators} are bit
strings.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt]
\item The \emph{accumulation prover} $\mathsf P$, on input $q$ and an
  old accumulator $\mathsf{acc}_{\mathrm{old}}$, outputs a new
  accumulator $\mathsf{acc}$ and an accumulation proof $\pi_{\mathsf V}$.
\item The \emph{accumulation verifier} $\mathsf V$, on input
  $(q, \mathsf{acc}_{\mathrm{old}}, \mathsf{acc}, \pi_{\mathsf V})$,
  outputs a bit; the bit $1$ asserts that $\mathsf{acc}$ correctly
  accumulates $q$ and $\mathsf{acc}_{\mathrm{old}}$.
\item The \emph{decider} $\mathsf D$, on input an accumulator, outputs
  a bit; an accumulator is \emph{valid} if $\mathsf D$ outputs $1$ on it.
\end{enumerate}
The scheme is \emph{complete} if, whenever $\Phi(q) = 1$,
$\mathsf D(\mathsf{acc}_{\mathrm{old}}) = 1$ and
$(\mathsf{acc}, \pi_{\mathsf V})$ is an output of $\mathsf P$ on
$(q, \mathsf{acc}_{\mathrm{old}})$, both
$\mathsf V(q, \mathsf{acc}_{\mathrm{old}}, \mathsf{acc}, \pi_{\mathsf V})
= 1$ and $\mathsf D(\mathsf{acc}) = 1$. It is \emph{sound} if for every
adversary of size polynomial in $\lambda$ that outputs
$(q, \mathsf{acc}_{\mathrm{old}}, \mathsf{acc}, \pi_{\mathsf V})$ the
probability, over the random oracle and the adversary's coins, of
\[
\mathsf V(q, \mathsf{acc}_{\mathrm{old}}, \mathsf{acc}, \pi_{\mathsf V})
= 1 \;\wedge\; \mathsf D(\mathsf{acc}) = 1 \;\wedge\;
\bigl(\Phi(q) = 0 \,\vee\, \mathsf D(\mathsf{acc}_{\mathrm{old}}) = 0\bigr)
\]
is negligible in $\lambda$.
\end{definition}

The definition is that of BCMS20 (Section~4.1) with one input and one
old accumulator per step; theirs admits any number of each and adds a
generator and an indexer, which fix the public parameters and keys left
implicit here. For recursion the size of an accumulator must also be
bounded independently of the number of inputs accumulated into it
(BCMS20, Definition~5.1).

Soundness carries the checks along a chain.

\begin{lemma}[Soundness along a chain]\label{lem:pcd-chain}
Let $(\mathsf P, \mathsf V, \mathsf D)$ be sound in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:pcd-acc}, let $\mathsf{acc}_0$ be valid, and let $m$
be polynomial in $\lambda$. For any efficient algorithm producing
$q_1, \dots, q_m$ and $\mathsf{acc}_1, \dots, \mathsf{acc}_m$, where
$\mathsf{acc}_i$ accumulates $q_i$ and $\mathsf{acc}_{i-1}$, such that
$\mathsf V$ accepts every step and $\mathsf D(\mathsf{acc}_m) = 1$, the
probability that $\Phi(q_i) = 0$ for some $i$ is at most $m$ times the
soundness error of the adversary that outputs step $i$ for a uniform
$i \in \{1, \dots, m\}$; it is therefore negligible.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If some $q_i$ violates $\Phi$, then either
$\mathsf{acc}_i$ is valid and step $i$ exhibits the event of the
soundness definition, or some later step $j > i$ turns an invalid
$\mathsf{acc}_{j-1}$ into a valid $\mathsf{acc}_j$ and exhibits it.
Let $\mathcal A$ be the algorithm that produces the chain and outputs
step $i$ for a uniform $i \in \{1, \dots, m\}$; since the bad event
forces some step to exhibit the soundness event, its probability is at
most $m$ times the soundness error of $\mathcal A$, which is negligible
because $m$ is polynomial (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Polynomial,
exponential, and negligible functions'', the closure properties).
\end{proof}

The verifier of each step runs the accumulation verifier and never the
decider. In a recursive chain the accumulation verifier runs inside the
circuit of the step's proof, so the argument also needs the knowledge
soundness of each step's proof, to recover what that circuit checked;
BCMS20 prove the resulting proof-carrying data secure for computations
of constant depth, which for a chain means constant length, because the
extractor is applied recursively once per level (Theorem~5.2 and
Remark~5.3).

For an inner-product polynomial commitment such a scheme is known under
the hypothesis of Theorem~\ref{thm:pcd-bcms} below, and it resolves
the second obstacle of \S\ref{sec:pcd-obstacles} where it arose: the
verifier circuit runs the $O(\log d)$ part of the opening check, the
rounds, the scalars and the small combinations of points, and
accumulates the $O(d)$ part, the claim about $G^{(0)}$, into a running
accumulator; the decider pays that multi-scalar multiplication once, at
the end of the chain. The next subsection defines the accumulator,
states the scheme as an interface theorem, and works the fold on the toy
curve.

\subsection{Accumulation and the Halo trick}
\label{sec:pcd-halo-trick}

\paragraph{The asymmetry, recalled.}
Everything the verifier of Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit} does to
decide the check~\eqref{eq:ipa-final}, or its deployed
form~\eqref{eq:ipa-dep-final}, is logarithmic in $d$: $3k - 2$ field
multiplications for $g(x; \mathbf u)$, a combination of the $2k$
received points and a handful of fixed ones with $O(k)$ scalar
multiplications, and a comparison of two curve points. The single
exception is the evaluation
\[
G^{(0)} = \ip{\mathbf s}{\mathbf G} = \Comm\bigl(g(X;\, u_1, \dots,
  u_k);\, 0\bigr),
\]
a multi-scalar multiplication of length $d$ over \emph{all} the
generators, which \S\ref{sec:ipa-verifier} named the one linear step and
recognised as the unblinded commitment to the polynomial $g$ whose
\emph{value} the verifier had just computed cheaply. Verifying an
Action proof as the consensus rules require includes that step for every
proof; batch verification, recalled at the start of this section,
evaluates one combined multiplication for the proofs of a batch, and the
batch is verified and nothing survives it. Accumulation takes the same
kind of linear combination and does something else with it: it carries
the combined claim forward as data, unverified, and pays for it once at
the end of a chain. Figure~\ref{fig:batch-vs-acc} sets the two side by side;
the rest of this subsection is the right-hand panel, and none of it is
deployed by Orchard.

(amber); the verifier combines them with fresh random factors and

Right: accumulation. Each proof's deferred claim is accumulated into a
running accumulator (green), each step being checked at logarithmic

\paragraph{The atomic accumulator.}
The claim to be deferred has a fixed shape, and the accumulator is that
shape written down.

\begin{definition}[Atomic accumulator for the inner-product argument]
\label{def:pcd-ipa-acc}
An \emph{accumulator instance} is a tuple
\[
\Acc := \bigl(G^{(0)}, u_1, \dots, u_k\bigr) \in \G \times (\F^{\times})^{k},
\]
a curve point and the $k$ round challenges of one opening. It is
\emph{valid} if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pcd-acc-valid}
G^{(0)} = \ip{\mathbf s(\mathbf u)}{\mathbf G}
 = \Comm\bigl(g(X;\, u_1, \dots, u_k);\, 0\bigr),
\end{equation}
with $\mathbf s(\mathbf u)$ and $g$ the structured scalars and the
polynomial of Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-structured}, in the symmetric
normalisation of Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit}. The deferred claim
concerns the \emph{unblinded} commitment: the blinder of the opened
commitment was discharged by the $[r^{*}]H$ term
of~\eqref{eq:ipa-final}, or the $[f]H$ term of~\eqref{eq:ipa-dep-final},
and plays no part in it. From here on the unary $\Comm(\cdot)$
abbreviates $\Comm(\cdot\,;\, 0)$.
\end{definition}

The deployed analogue replaces $\mathbf s(\mathbf u)$ and $g$ by their forms in
Remark~\ref{rem:ipa-deployed-norm}; Proposition~\ref{prop:pcd-fold} and, with
verifier-drawn challenges, the closure argument below hold for it verbatim, with
Corollary~\ref{cor:ipa-deployed-ks} in place of Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks}, since
they use only the linearity of the commitment, the degree of $g$ and the
knowledge soundness of the opening.

A verifier who is handed a point $G^{(0)}$ together with the challenges
can complete the check~\eqref{eq:ipa-final} in $O(\log d)$ operations
\emph{provided} the tuple is valid; validity is precisely what he has
not paid for. The scheme quoted next lets him defer that payment.

\begin{theorem}[Accumulation for the inner-product argument; interface]
\label{thm:pcd-bcms}
Let $\mathrm{PC}_{\mathrm{DL}}$ be the inner-product polynomial
commitment of BCMS20 (Appendix~A.2), for polynomials of degree below
$d$ over a group $\G$ of prime order, and suppose that
$\mathrm{PC}_{\mathrm{DL}}$ is a polynomial commitment scheme in the
random oracle model, its openings in particular being extractable. Then
(BCMS20, Theorem~7.1) there is an accumulation scheme in the sense of
Definition~\ref{def:pcd-acc}, in the random oracle model, for the
predicate ``this $\mathrm{PC}_{\mathrm{DL}}$ opening is correct'',
whose accumulators are themselves $\mathrm{PC}_{\mathrm{DL}}$ openings,
such that:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt]
\item the accumulation prover $\mathsf P$ runs in time dominated by
  $O(d)$ scalar multiplications in $\G$;
\item the accumulation verifier $\mathsf V$ runs in time dominated by
  $O(\log d)$ scalar multiplications in $\G$;
\item the decider $\mathsf D$ is the opening check of
  $\mathrm{PC}_{\mathrm{DL}}$: a succinct part, which yields a tuple of
  the form of Definition~\ref{def:pcd-ipa-acc} in the normalisation of
  Remark~\ref{rem:ipa-deployed-norm}, followed by the validity
  check~\eqref{eq:pcd-acc-valid} of that tuple, one multi-scalar
  multiplication of length $d$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

The theorem is quoted as an interface and not proved in this volume.
Its hypothesis, the extractability of $\mathrm{PC}_{\mathrm{DL}}$
openings, is stated by BCMS20 as a conjecture from the binding of the
Pedersen commitment, the extractor they record for the
Fiat--Shamir-compiled opening running in superpolynomial time
(BCMS20, Appendix~A.3.2). The scheme $\mathrm{PC}_{\mathrm{DL}}$ is
neither Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit}, whose fold is symmetric, nor
the deployed opening of Remark~\ref{rem:ipa-deployed-norm}, which blinds
every commitment and subtracts $[v]\,G_0$ where
$\mathrm{PC}_{\mathrm{DL}}$ adds a multiple of its inner-product
generator (the halo2 book's \emph{Comparison to other work} chapter); no theorem
transferring it to either is proved here or cited, and in the
classification of \S\ref{sec:intro-claim} that transfer, like
accumulation over these curves, is designed-but-unspecified. What an
accumulation step does for Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit}, and why
its random combination is sound, is worked out below at the level the
volume proves things.

\paragraph{The accumulation step in the verifier circuit.}
Consider a Halo~2 verifier circuit, expressed over the scalar field of
one Pasta curve and verifying a proof produced over the other curve, an
arrangement whose necessity \S\ref{sec:pcd-cycle} explains. At step $i$
of a chain the circuit receives the previous proof $\pi_{i-1}$, whose
opening is an instance of Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit}, and the
accumulator $\Acc_{i-1}$ exposed as a public input of $\pi_{i-1}$, of
which it uses the instance on the curve $\pi_{i-1}$ is committed on (see
below), and does three things.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt]
\item \emph{Run the succinct part.} It recomputes the challenges
  $\mathbf u'$ of the opening from the transcript, evaluates
  $g(x; \mathbf u')$ with $O(k)$ scalar operations, and forms the
  left-hand side of~\eqref{eq:ipa-final} and every term of the
  right-hand side except the one on the final generator, here written
  $G'^{(0)}$, with $O(k)$ point operations. The point operations are
  native, since the points of $\pi_{i-1}$ have coordinates in the
  circuit's field. The challenges, $g(x; \mathbf u')$ and the scalar
  products lie in the scalar field of the curve $\pi_{i-1}$ is committed
  on, the other Pasta field; they are computed non-natively, or exposed
  as public inputs and checked by the next circuit of the chain, which
  is native to that field (``What the cycle does and does not buy'' in
  \S\ref{sec:pcd-cycle}; the halo2 book's \emph{Recursion} chapter).
\item \emph{Witness the expensive part.} It does not compute
  $G'^{(0)}$. The prover of the step supplies the claimed point as
  advice, and the circuit uses it to complete the check, which is then
  passed \emph{conditionally on the validity of the fresh tuple}
  $(G'^{(0)}, \mathbf u')$ of that opening.
\item \emph{Accumulate.} The step now holds two claims of the
  shape~\eqref{eq:pcd-acc-valid}: the inherited instance
  $(G^{(0)}, \mathbf u)$ of $\Acc_{i-1}$ and the fresh
  $(G'^{(0)}, \mathbf u')$. For a challenge $\rho$ derived by the random
  oracle from both tuples, the two reduce to the single claim
  \begin{equation}\label{eq:pcd-fold}
  G^{(0)} + [\rho]\,G'^{(0)} \;=\; \Comm\bigl(g(X; \mathbf u)
    + \rho\, g(X; \mathbf u')\bigr)
   = \ip{\mathbf s(\mathbf u) + \rho\,\mathbf s(\mathbf u')}{\mathbf G},
  \end{equation}
  whose coefficient vector on the right anyone holding both challenge
  tuples can recompute. Proposition~\ref{prop:pcd-fold} shows that a
  false input survives the combination for at most one $\rho$. The
  step's prover then opens the combined point at a fresh point, outside
  the circuit, and the circuit checks that opening succinctly as in
  item~1; the tuple the opening leaves behind is the new accumulator
  $\Acc_i$ (over a 2-cycle, its instance on that curve), exposed as a
  public input of the proof $\pi_i$ the step produces. The paragraph
  ``Closure: the prover-assisted step'' below describes that opening.
\end{enumerate}
Over a 2-cycle of curves (\S\ref{sec:pcd-cycle}) the proofs alternate
between the two curves, and the accumulator is a pair of instances, one
per curve. The step that verifies a proof committed on one curve folds
that proof's fresh tuple into the instance on the same curve, which was
last updated two steps earlier and forwarded unchanged by the
intervening step, and it forwards the other curve's instance in the
same way, without point arithmetic.
Figure~\ref{fig:acc-chain} draws the chain. At its end a single
multi-scalar multiplication of length $d$, run outside any circuit,
checks the last accumulator and with it every opening in the history:
for the scheme of Theorem~\ref{thm:pcd-bcms} by
Lemma~\ref{lem:pcd-chain}.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering

    fill=tierin!8, draw=tierin, text width=3.5cm},

    fill=tierrecv!12, draw=tierrecv, minimum height=6mm},
  msm/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, inner sep=4pt, align=flush center,

  note/.style={font=\scriptsize, black!60, align=flush center},

\caption{A chain of recursively composed proofs. Each step's verifier
circuit (blue) runs the logarithmic part of the previous proof's opening
check, witnesses the expensive point instead of computing it, combines
the resulting tuple with the accumulator (green) it inherited
by~\eqref{eq:pcd-fold}, and checks succinctly the opening of the
combined point at a fresh point, whose tuple is the new accumulator, a
public input of the step's own proof. Over a cycle of curves each
$\Acc_i$ is a pair of instances, one per curve: each step folds into
the instance on the curve of the proof it verifies and passes the other

\label{fig:acc-chain}
