\paragraph{Why the fold is sound.}
The soundness of the fold~\eqref{eq:pcd-fold} is one line of linear
algebra in the group.

\begin{proposition}[Soundness of the $\rho$-fold]\label{prop:pcd-fold}
Let $(G^{(0)}, \mathbf u)$ and $(G'^{(0)}, \mathbf u')$ be two
accumulator instances and $\rho$ uniform in $\F$, drawn after both are
fixed. If both instances are valid, the folded
equation~\eqref{eq:pcd-fold} holds for every $\rho$. If at least one is
invalid, it holds for at most one value of $\rho$, hence with
probability at most $1/|\F|$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Set $\Delta := G^{(0)} - \Comm(g(X; \mathbf u))$ and
$\Delta' := G'^{(0)} - \Comm(g(X; \mathbf u'))$, the two errors. By
linearity of the commitment (\emph{Crypto Guide}, \S``Pedersen vector
commitments'', the remark that linearity survives),
$\Comm(g + \rho g') = \Comm(g) + [\rho]\Comm(g')$, so the left-hand side
of~\eqref{eq:pcd-fold} minus the right is
$\Delta + [\rho]\,\Delta'$, and the equation holds exactly when
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pcd-fold-error}
\Delta + [\rho]\,\Delta' = \mathcal O .
\end{equation}
If both instances are valid, $\Delta = \Delta' = \mathcal O$ and the
equation holds for all $\rho$. Otherwise two cases. If
$\Delta' \ne \mathcal O$, then, $\G$ being cyclic of prime order,
$\Delta'$ generates $\G$ and the map $\rho \mapsto [\rho]\Delta'$ is a
bijection $\F \to \G$ (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The mixed inner product
with group elements'', the one-dimensional vector space $\G$ over
$\F$), so exactly one $\rho$ sends $\Delta'$ to $-\Delta$: the discrete
logarithm of $-\Delta$ to the base $\Delta'$, which exists whether or
not anyone can compute it. If $\Delta' = \mathcal O$, then $\Delta \ne
\mathcal O$ and~\eqref{eq:pcd-fold-error} holds for no $\rho$. A uniform
$\rho$ hits the at most one bad value with probability at most
$1/|\F|$.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}[The fold on the toy curve]\label{ex:pcd-fold}
% toy_accumulate.py:
% claim 1: challenges (52, 44), s = [80, 10, 68, 57], G^(0) = <s, G> = (21, 10)
% claim 2: challenges (41, 39), s' = [64, 11, 53, 47], G'^(0) = <s', G> = (23, 2)
% both true: G^(0) + [rho]G'^(0) = Comm(g + rho g') for all 97 rho
% claim 1 false: rho surviving the folded check: [] (0 of 97)
% claim 2 false: rho surviving the folded check: [0] (1 of 97)
% both false: rho surviving the folded check: [50] (1 of 97)
% a false claim survives for at most one rho: soundness error 1/|F| = 1/97
The fold is run on the $97$-point curve and the four generators
$G_0, \dots, G_3$ of \S\ref{sec:ipa-toy}, with $d = 4$ and the
symmetric structured scalars of Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-structured}, on
two challenge tuples, $(u_1, u_2) = (52, 44)$ with
$\mathbf s = (80, 10, 68, 57)$ and
$G^{(0)} = \ip{\mathbf s}{\mathbf G} = (21, 10)$, and
$(u_1', u_2') = (41, 39)$ with $\mathbf s' = (64, 11, 53, 47)$ and
$G'^{(0)} = (23, 2)$, both valid by construction, for every
$\rho \in \F_{97}$. With both instances valid the folded
equation~\eqref{eq:pcd-fold} holds for all $97$ values of $\rho$.
Spoiling an instance by adding a nonzero multiple of $G_0$ to its point
makes its error $\Delta$ a nonzero point, and the surviving values of
$\rho$ are counted again. With the first instance false and the second
valid, no $\rho$ survives: this is the case $\Delta' = \mathcal O
\ne \Delta$ of the proof. With the second false and the first valid,
exactly one survives, $\rho = 0$, the value that discards the second
instance altogether. With both false exactly one survives, $\rho = 50$,
the discrete logarithm of $-\Delta$ to the base $\Delta'$ on this curve,
where such logarithms are a short search. In every spoiled case the
count is at most one of $97$: the bound $1/|\F|$ of
Proposition~\ref{prop:pcd-fold}, attained.
\end{example}

\paragraph{Closure: the prover-assisted step.}
The folded claim~\eqref{eq:pcd-fold} is not an accumulator instance.
Its coefficient vector $\mathbf s(\mathbf u) + \rho\,\mathbf s(\mathbf u')$ is
in general not the structured vector of any single challenge tuple, so carrying
it means carrying both tuples, and after $m$ folds all $m$ of them: the decider
would still pay one multiplication of length $d$, but the accumulator would grow
with the chain, against the size bound that recursion requires
(\S\ref{sec:pcd-acc}). The tuple type of
Definition~\ref{def:pcd-ipa-acc} is therefore \emph{not} closed under
verifier-side linear combination alone. It is closed under one more
step, which the prover assists. Write $A := G^{(0)} + [\rho]\,G'^{(0)}$
for the combined point and $a(X) := g(X; \mathbf u) + \rho\,g(X;
\mathbf u')$ for the combined polynomial, of degree below $d$. The
combined claim says exactly that $A = \Comm(a)$. The step's prover
opens $A$ by the opening protocol of Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit}
at a fresh point $x'$, derived by the random oracle from $A$ and both
tuples, claiming the value $a(x')$, which the verifier computes for
himself in $O(k)$ field operations from the two tuples, since
$g(x'; \mathbf u)$ and $g(x'; \mathbf u')$ are each a product of $k$
factors. That opening does two things at once. First, it carries the
combined claim up to a blinder.

\begin{proposition}[Prover-assisted step, verifier-drawn challenges]
\label{prop:pcd-assisted}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks}, let $\rho$, $x'$ and
the opening's challenges be drawn uniformly by the verifier, in this
order, $\rho$ after the two tuples are fixed. There is an extractor
which, given rewindable access to a step prover accepted with
non-negligible probability, runs in expected polynomial time and outputs
either blinders $\delta, \delta'$ with
$G^{(0)} = \Comm(g(X; \mathbf u);\, \delta)$ and
$G'^{(0)} = \Comm(g(X; \mathbf u');\, \delta')$, or a nontrivial
discrete-logarithm relation among $\mathbf G$, $H$, $U$, $U'$. Its
knowledge error is $O(\log d/|\F|) + (d - 1)/|\F| + 1/|\F|$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks} the opening yields a representation $A = \ip{\mathbf
a^{*}}{\mathbf G} + [r_A]H$, unique since a second one would be a
discrete-logarithm relation among $\mathbf G$ and $H$, and certifies
$a^{*}(x') = a(x')$ for the polynomial $a^{*}$ with coefficient vector
$\mathbf a^{*}$; two polynomials of degree below $d$ that differ agree
at a uniform $x'$, drawn after $A$ was fixed, with probability at most
$(d - 1)/|\F|$ (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The Schwartz--Zippel lemma''),
so except with that probability $\mathbf a^{*}$ is the coefficient
vector of $a$ and $A = \Comm(a;\, r_A)$. The blinder $r_A$ is the
prover's and need not be $0$. Rewinding the choice of $\rho$ and extracting
again at a second value $\tilde\rho \ne \rho$ gives representations of $A$
and of $\tilde A := G^{(0)} + [\tilde\rho]\,G'^{(0)}$, whose difference is
$[\rho - \tilde\rho]\,G'^{(0)}$; solving yields $G'^{(0)} =
\Comm(g(X; \mathbf u');\, \delta')$ and $G^{(0)} = \Comm(g(X; \mathbf
u);\, \delta)$ for blinders $\delta, \delta'$ that the extractor
computes. This is the argument of Proposition~\ref{prop:pcd-fold}
applied to the $\mathbf G$-coordinates of the representations: a
combination correct at two values of $\rho$ has both errors zero. It is
all the chain needs: a tuple whose point is
$\Comm(g(X; \mathbf u);\, \delta)$ still certifies the opening it came
from, because, writing $c$ for the final scalar that~\eqref{eq:ipa-final}
calls $a$,
\[
[c]\,\Comm(g;\, \delta) + [r^{*}]H = [c]\,\Comm(g) + [r^{*} + c\delta]H ,
\]
so that opening satisfies the honest check with the blinder
$r^{*} + c\delta$ in place of $r^{*}$, and Theorem~\ref{thm:ipa-ks}
applies to it; in~\eqref{eq:ipa-dep-final} the offset joins the term
$[f]H$ likewise. The chain thus carries the claim that each deferred
point's $\mathbf G$-coordinates are $\mathbf s(\mathbf u)$; the exact
equality~\eqref{eq:pcd-acc-valid}, blinder $0$ included, is required
only of the last accumulator, which the decider checks and an honest
prover satisfies. An intermediate tuple may miss~\eqref{eq:pcd-acc-valid}
by such a multiple $[\delta]H$ without any opening it serves being
false.
\end{proof}

Second, the opening leaves behind a deferred claim of the atomic form: it
ends, as every opening does, in a point $G''^{(0)}$ and challenges
$\mathbf u''$ that the verifier circuit witnesses rather than computes.
The accumulator after the step is again one tuple. This is the
accumulation step of BCMS20 (Section~7.1), which likewise derives the
combining challenge and the fresh point by the random oracle from the
tuples and the combined point; there the accumulator is the opening
itself, and its succinct part runs at the next step. With $\rho$, $x'$
and the opening's challenges drawn by a verifier, the step's soundness is
Proposition~\ref{prop:pcd-assisted}. With
random-oracle challenges, the step's soundness is BCMS20's Theorem~7.1
for $\mathrm{PC}_{\mathrm{DL}}$ under its conjectured extractability
(Theorem~\ref{thm:pcd-bcms}); for Construction~\ref{con:ipa-commit} it
is designed-but-unspecified.

\paragraph{What the accumulator carries.}
The deferred equation~\eqref{eq:pcd-acc-valid} is a claim of
\emph{commitment equality}, equivalently a claim about the value of one
multi-scalar multiplication, for a coefficient vector
$\mathbf s(\mathbf u)$ that is public and structured: anyone holding
the challenges can write it down. It is not itself an evaluation
opening; no polynomial is being evaluated at any point when the decider
runs, only a point recomputed and compared. The accumulation protocol
reduces this claim, and carries it along the chain, through the
prover-assisted opening just described, which turns each commitment
equality into one evaluation claim at a fresh point and each opening
back into one commitment equality. The Orchard verifier, once more,
does none of this: it computes $G^{(0)}$ and compares.

\subsection{A cycle of curves for efficient native recursion}
\label{sec:pcd-cycle}

The verifier circuit of \S\ref{sec:pcd-halo-trick} was ``expressed over
the scalar field of one Pasta curve and verifying a proof produced over
the other''. This subsection explains why the two curves are needed,
what the arrangement buys, and where Orchard already uses half of it.

\paragraph{Two fields per curve, recalled.}
A curve $E$ over a prime field has two fields attached to it
(\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Base fields, scalar fields, and the Pasta
cycle''): the \emph{base field}, in which its points' coordinates lie
and in which the group law's slopes, squarings and inversions are
computed, and the \emph{scalar field}, the integers modulo the prime
order of its group, in which the scalars of $[k]P$ live. Write $E_p$
for a curve over $\F_p$ and $q := \#E_p(\F_p)$ for its prime order, so
that its base field is $\F_p$ and its scalar field $\F_q$. A PLONKish
circuit of Definition~\ref{def:plonkish} is defined over one prime
field $\F$, its \emph{native field}: its cells hold elements of $\F$
and its gates are polynomial identities over $\F$, so an addition or
multiplication in $\F$ costs one cell or one gate. Arithmetic in any
\emph{other} prime field is \emph{non-native}: an element of the other
field must be carried as several $\F$-cells, each range-checked by the
lookups of \S\ref{sec:arith-lookups}, and each of its operations
becomes many gates with carries and reductions. Which field a
circuit is native to is not a free choice. Its column polynomials are
committed with the inner-product commitment of \S\ref{sec:ipa} on some
curve, and a Pedersen commitment to a vector of $\F$-elements needs a
group whose scalar field is $\F$; so \emph{the native field of a
circuit is the scalar field of the curve its proof is committed on},
exactly as the Action circuit, native to $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$, is
committed on Vesta, whose order is $p_{\Pallas}$
(\S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}).

\paragraph{Why one curve cannot host its own verifier.}
Take a proof committed on $E_p$. Its group elements, the column and
quotient commitments, the cross terms $(L_j, R_j)$ and the deferred
point of its opening, are points of $E_p$ with coordinates in $\F_p$. The
in-circuit folding of \S\ref{sec:pcd-halo-trick} adds and scales such
points, which is base-field arithmetic in $\F_p$, so a verifier circuit
handles them natively only if its native field is $\F_p$. But a circuit
committed on $E_p$ is native to the \emph{scalar} field $\F_q$: its
challenges, its structured scalars and every field operation of the
succinct part are $\F_q$-arithmetic. One curve would host both jobs
only if $\F_p = \F_q$, that is, $\#E_p(\F_p) = p$. Curves with exactly
$p$ points exist; by Hasse's theorem (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``The group
structure of $E(\F_p)$''), which writes $\#E_p(\F_p) = p + 1 - t$ with
the trace $t$, they are the curves with $t = 1$, called
\emph{anomalous}. On an anomalous curve the discrete logarithm is
computable in polynomial time, the attack of Smart, of Semaev and of
Satoh and Araki, and the \emph{Crypto Guide} lists ``not anomalous''
among the criteria a curve must meet to be used at all
(\S``Instantiation on elliptic curves; the Pasta curves'', the
anti-Smart criterion). So no \emph{secure} curve has $\F_p = \F_q$, and
no one curve can host both the coordinates of its proofs and its own
verifier circuit natively.

\paragraph{The 2-cycle.}
The efficient resolution uses two curves that trade fields. A
\emph{2-cycle} of elliptic curves is a pair $(E_p, E_q)$ with
\[
\#E_p(\F_p) = q \qquad\text{and}\qquad \#E_q(\F_q) = p ,
\]
both primes: the scalar field of each curve is the base field of the
other. Then:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt]
\item a circuit committed on $E_q$ has native field $\F_p$, the scalar
  field of $E_q$; and $\F_p$ is the coordinate field of $E_p$, so that
  circuit manipulates the points of a proof produced over $E_p$
  natively, and verifies such a proof at the cost
  \S\ref{sec:pcd-halo-trick} counted, the proof's scalars aside;
\item the proof that circuit produces is committed on $E_q$, and its
  points have coordinates in $\F_q$, the native field of a circuit
  committed on $E_p$; so a circuit over $\F_q$ handles its points
  natively in turn;
\item the proofs of a chain alternate between the two curves, each step
  handling its predecessor's points natively (Figure~\ref{fig:cycle}).
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small,
  cv/.style={draw, rounded corners=3pt, inner sep=5pt, align=flush center,
    text width=4.3cm},
  pal/.style={cv, draw=tierin, fill=tierin!8},
  ves/.style={cv, draw=tierrecv, fill=tierrecv!10},
  pf/.style={font=\scriptsize, align=flush center, inner sep=2pt},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2.2mm]}, line width=0.9pt}]
  \node[pal] (P) at (0,0)
    {\textbf{Pallas} $E_p$\\[2pt]
     base field $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$ (coordinates)\\
     scalar field $\F_{p_{\Vesta}}$ (order)\\[3pt]
     a circuit committed here\\is native to $\F_{p_{\Vesta}}$};
  \node[ves] (V) at (8.6,0)
    {\textbf{Vesta} $E_q$\\[2pt]
     base field $\F_{p_{\Vesta}}$ (coordinates)\\
     scalar field $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$ (order)\\[3pt]
     a circuit committed here\\is native to $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$};
  \draw[arr, tierin] (P.north) to[bend left=22] (V.north);
  \node[pf, tierin!80!black] at (4.3,2.9)
    {a proof on Pallas: points with $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$ coordinates,\\
     handled natively by the $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$-circuit on Vesta};
  \draw[arr, tierrecv] (V.south) to[bend left=22] (P.south);
  \node[pf, tierrecv!80!black] at (4.3,-2.9)
    {a proof on Vesta: points with $\F_{p_{\Vesta}}$ coordinates,\\
     handled natively by the $\F_{p_{\Vesta}}$-circuit on Pallas};
  \node[draw=tierfull, fill=tierfull!10, rounded corners=2pt, inner sep=4pt,
    align=flush center, font=\scriptsize, text width=6.2cm] at (4.3,-4.15)
    {\textbf{deployed half} (Orchard): the Action circuit is native to
     $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$, committed on Vesta, and computes on Pallas
     points; no proof is verified in a circuit};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The Pasta 2-cycle. The base field of each curve (its
coordinate field) is the scalar field of the other, so a circuit
committed on Vesta (green) is native to $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$ and handles
Pallas points (blue) natively, and symmetrically. In a recursive chain
the proofs alternate along the two arrows. Orchard (amber) uses one
direction of the cycle only: Pallas arithmetic inside a Vesta-committed
circuit, with no verifier circuit at all.}
\label{fig:cycle}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{What the cycle does and does not buy.}
The cycle makes the \emph{preceding} curve's coordinate arithmetic
native; it does not identify the two scalar fields, which remain
distinct primes. Challenges squeezed in the previous proof's field and
the scalars derived from them are $\F_p$-elements that the
$\F_q$-native circuit must handle: they need compatible encodings
across the two fields, range checks where an element of the larger
field is carried in the smaller, and, where a genuine $\F_p$
multiplication is unavoidable, non-native scalar arithmetic. Nor is the
cycle a logical necessity. A single curve could verify its own proofs
with non-native coordinate arithmetic throughout, at a cost of many
gates per point operation multiplied by the $O(\log d)$ point
operations of the succinct part; that is possible and substantially
more expensive. The cycle is the efficient native design, not the only
correct one.

\paragraph{The deployed instance.}
The Pasta cycle of the \emph{Math Guide} (\S``Base fields, scalar
fields, and the Pasta cycle''; \S``Pallas and Vesta assembled'') is a
2-cycle: Pallas is defined over $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$ with group order
$p_{\Vesta}$, and Vesta over $\F_{p_{\Vesta}}$ with group order
$p_{\Pallas}$, both curves $y^{2} = x^{3} + 5$ (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#pallasandvesta}{\S~5.4.9.6},
``Pallas and Vesta''). The cycle follows from the specification's two
primes and nothing else.
% pasta_cycle.py:
% Pallas: y^2 = x^3 + 5 over F_p, p = 0x40000000000000000000000000000000224698fc094cf91b992d30ed00000001;
%   [p_Vesta](-1, 2) = O, and only one multiple of that prime lies in the Hasse window, so #E = p_Vesta
%   trace t = p + 1 - #E = -86663725065984043395317759 (|t| < 2 sqrt(p): True); anomalous iff t = 1: False
% Vesta: y^2 = x^3 + 5 over F_p, p = 0x40000000000000000000000000000000224698fc0994a8dd8c46eb2100000001;
%   [p_Pallas](-1, 2) = O, and only one multiple of that prime lies in the Hasse window, so #E = p_Pallas
%   trace t = p + 1 - #E = 86663725065984043395317761 (|t| < 2 sqrt(p): True); anomalous iff t = 1: False
% p_Pallas - p_Vesta = -86663725065984043395317760
% toy curve for contrast: y^2 = x^3 + 3 over F_79 has 97 points, t = 79 + 1 - 97 = -17
With
\begin{align*}
p_{\Pallas} &= \mathtt{0x40000000\,00000000\,00000000\,00000000\,
  224698fc\,094cf91b\,992d30ed\,00000001},\\
p_{\Vesta}  &= \mathtt{0x40000000\,00000000\,00000000\,00000000\,
  224698fc\,0994a8dd\,8c46eb21\,00000001},
\end{align*}
the point $(-1, 2)$ lies on Pallas, $[p_{\Vesta}]\,(-1, 2) =
\mathcal O$ there, and exactly one multiple of
$p_{\Vesta}$ lies in the Hasse interval $[\,p + 1 - 2\sqrt p,\; p + 1 +
2\sqrt p\,]$ for $p = p_{\Pallas}$. That settles the order: the order
of the point $(-1, 2)$ divides the prime $p_{\Vesta}$ and is not $1$,
so it is $p_{\Vesta}$, which therefore divides $\#\Ep(\F_{p_{\Pallas}})$
by Lagrange's theorem (\emph{Math Guide}, \S``Cosets and Lagrange's
theorem''); the group order lies in an interval of length $4\sqrt p$,
far shorter than $p_{\Vesta}$, which contains one multiple of
$p_{\Vesta}$; hence $\#\Ep(\F_{p_{\Pallas}}) = p_{\Vesta}$. The
symmetric check on Vesta shows that the Vesta group has order
$p_{\Pallas}$. The traces are
\begin{align*}
t_{\Pallas} &= p_{\Pallas} + 1 - p_{\Vesta} = -86663725065984043395317759,\\
t_{\Vesta}  &= p_{\Vesta} + 1 - p_{\Pallas} = 86663725065984043395317761,
\end{align*}
each far inside the Hasse bound and neither equal to $1$: neither curve
is anomalous, as the \emph{Crypto Guide}'s criterion requires. The two
traces sum to $2$, as they must for any 2-cycle, since $(p + 1 - q) +
(q + 1 - p) = 2$; the toy curve of \S\ref{sec:ipa-toy}, $y^{2} = x^{3} +
3$ over $\F_{79}$ with $97$ points, has trace $79 + 1 - 97 = -17$, and
the case excluded in that subsection, a curve over $\F_{97}$ with $97$
points and so trace $1$, is exactly the anomalous one.

\paragraph{The cycle in the accumulation chain.}
The Pasta cycle renders each step of \S\ref{sec:pcd-halo-trick} native.
A proof committed on Vesta is produced by a circuit native to
$\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$; that circuit verifies the previous proof of the
chain, committed on Pallas, whose group elements, the column and
quotient commitments, the pairs $(L_j, R_j)$ and the point of the
Pallas accumulator instance it forwards, have coordinates in exactly
$\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$, so the point
arithmetic of the succinct checks and of the fold~\eqref{eq:pcd-fold} is
native. The next proof, committed on Pallas by a circuit native to
$\F_{p_{\Vesta}}$, verifies the Vesta proof by the symmetric argument.
This cross-curve alternation avoids non-native emulation of the
preceding curve's coordinate field; as ``What the cycle does and does
not buy'' said, it does not eliminate the scalar-field conversions.

\paragraph{The half Orchard uses.}
This is the part of the cycle in consensus. The Action circuit is
native to $\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$ and committed on Vesta
(\S\ref{sec:ipa-halo2}), and the statement it proves manipulates
Pallas points: the note commitment $\cmsym$, a Pallas point whose
$x$-coordinate $\cmx$ is a public input; the randomised verification
key $\rk$; and the net value commitment $\cvsym^{\mathrm{net}}$, the
last two exposed by their coordinates in the instance column of
\S\ref{sec:halo2-instance}. Their coordinates lie in
$\F_{p_{\Pallas}}$, the circuit's native field, by the very coincidence
the cycle is built on, so the circuit computes on them natively, with
gates such as the incomplete-addition gate of \S\ref{sec:halo2-addinc}.
The protocol
specification records the arrangement: Vesta for the proof system,
Pallas for the application circuit, both curves designed to be
efficiently implementable inside a circuit though only Pallas is so
used (protocol specification,
\href{https://zips.z.cash/protocol/protocol.pdf\#pallasandvesta}{\S~5.4.9.6},
``Pallas and Vesta''); ZIP~224 says the same in the language of this
subsection, that the proposal uses half of the cycle, Pallas being the
curve embedded in Vesta's circuits, and that the full cycle is left for
future use (\href{https://zips.z.cash/zip-0224}{ZIP~224}, under
``Curves''). One direction of Figure~\ref{fig:cycle} is thus in
consensus today; the return arrow, and everything in this section that
depends on it, is not.
